--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This is the start of the preface for Philip Ulstad’s "Coelum Philosophorum" (The Heaven of Philosophers), a 16th-century work on distillation and alchemy in medicine.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

You have here, friend reader, that most noble part of medicine, in which no bitter juice is painfully forced upon the patient, nor does a crude mixture of substances serve as a remedy for the human body. Rather, its purer and more secret power—like a little soul <term>animula: a "soul-let" or quintessence, the distilled essence of a substance</term> drawn forth from materials by art—winds itself into our spirits and makes life more vigorous. 

This matter was truly unknown for a long time to the common "little doctors" <note>mediculis: a slightly derogatory term for unlearned physicians</note> of our age, and even longer desired by the common people who stood in need of its help. Although first discovered by the most illustrious physicians and developed through laborious processes, this medicine was once familiar only to princes, Caesars, kings, and dukes through the industry of their personal doctors. Now, by the kindness of the Great God, Ulstadius <note>Philip Ulstad, a 16th-century Nuremberg physician and alchemist</note> has taken care through this work that it should become both healthful and available to the common people. 

Let others praise the noble help of Theriac <note>A famous ancient "universal" antidote made of dozens of ingredients, including viper flesh</note> against the most cruel sicknesses; let others cry out with wonderful proclamations for the medicine of Mithridates <note>Mithridatium, a complex preventive antidote named after King Mithridates VI of Pontus</note> as "protective remedies" <note>original Greek: ἀλεξητήρια (alexētēria), meaning substances that ward off poison or disease</note> against a harvest of afflictions. To be sure, these are never praised enough for their own abilities; but because they are composed everywhere of crude, heavy matter, they drive away no diseases other than those of the body. 

But who will not clearly approve of this work? For it is most effective not only for driving away the diseases of the body, but once these are driven out, it is equally capable of increasing the spirit and wonderfully prolonging life, whereby...

<summary>Philip Ulstad introduces his work on distilled medicines, arguing that alchemical essences are superior to traditional herbal mixtures like Theriac because they treat both the body and the spirit.</summary>
<keywords>Philip Ulstad, distillation, alchemy, medical history, Theriac, Mithridatium, quintessence, spirit, prolongation of life</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>Continues from the discussion of crude, heavy medicines on the previous page. The manuscript shows heavy ink bleed-through from the reverse side of the leaf.</meta>

...y substances. Indeed, it is most often the case that, just as the spirit is superior to all elements, humors, limbs, and the other particles of the body, so too are the medicines that preserve and refresh this spirit far more effective than the earthy, coarse, and impure remedies used for the physical limbs. 

Therefore, excellent reader, look kindly upon this little work of Ulstad’s—this "little night-study" <term>lucubratiunculam: a scholarly work produced by the light of a midnight lamp</term>—and be convinced that for those suffering miserably in their spirits, this separation of pure essence from the coarsest materials provides a healthy remedy. With this, you will never ward off the "Royal Disease" <note>original: *morbum regium*, a historical term for jaundice, so named because it was thought to require the expensive diet and leisure of a king to cure</note>, stupor <note>original: *attonitionem*, literally a state of being "thunderstruck" or stunned</note>, fatal convulsions, lethargy, delirium, a failing spirit, or similar illnesses of a fading soul with any greater reliability or skill.

->**Farewell.**<-

<summary>This page concludes the preface, arguing that distilled "spirits" are superior to coarse, physical medicines because they directly treat the vital spirit of the patient. It lists several serious neurological and systemic conditions that these refined remedies can address more effectively than traditional cures.</summary>
<keywords>vital spirit, distillation, jaundice, epilepsy, lethargy, medical alchemy, Philipp Ulstad, pharmacology</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory front matter of the book. Having just argued for the superiority of distilled "spirits" in the previous section, the author now begins his formal dedication to his patron, Felix de Gennasio.</meta>

<page-type>dedication</page-type>
<sig>a iij</sig>

# TO THE ENERGETIC AND NOBLE FE-
# LIX DE GENNASIO, VALEN-
## tinian, Lord of Dauphiné, and fel-
## low countryman, Philipp Ulstad
### Sends many greetings. <note>original: *S. D. P.*, abbreviation for *Salutem Dicit Plurimam*</note>

IF you are in good health, it is well; I indeed, by the 
kindness of God, am in the best of health, Felix, most 
happy <note>A play on words; the name *Felix* means "happy" or "lucky" in Latin</note> of all men. I have always intended in my mind
what I might be able to return to you for the count-
less favors you have bestowed upon me, for I know that
ingratitude is detested by all more than any other vice. 
Therefore, it came to mind to dedicate to you this 
small gift of literature, such as it is—gathered by my 
own diligence from the reading of various authors—
and to bring it into the light under the protection of 
your name; I have no doubt that you will receive it 
with a favorable mind. Although it is quite brief 
and succinct, I nevertheless do not doubt that it will 
delight you greatly, both because it is a repository, as 
it were, of new and old medicines, and because it 
contains many philosophical, medical, and alchemical 
matters written very obscurely by John of Rupescissa, 
Raymond Lull, Arnald of Villanova, and Albertus 
Magnus—those supreme seekers of the secret things 
of Nature <note>These four men were the most famous medieval authorities on alchemy and the "quintessence" or distilled alcohol</note>. Indeed, they wrote so obscurely that 
these things can be understood by few, or rather almost 
none, in our age. This must be because people have, 
up until now, seen nothing or very little of the old 
teachings and books. For these works seem to have 
come into the hands of only a few...

<summary>This page contains the opening of Philipp Ulstad's dedication to his patron, Felix de Gennasio. Ulstad explains that he has compiled this "repository" of medical and alchemical knowledge from the difficult and obscure writings of medieval masters like Raymond Lull and Albertus Magnus.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Felix de Gennasio, Philipp Ulstad, John of Rupescissa, Raymond Lull, Arnald of Villanova, Albertus Magnus, alchemy, medical history, gratitude</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's dedication to Felix de Gennasio. The previous page ended while explaining that contemporary readers struggle with ancient alchemical texts because they lack access to the core manuscripts of the tradition.</meta>

...the golden <note>The Latin text begins mid-word with "deo," likely completing "aureo" (golden) from the previous page.</note> Book of Mercuries, or the *Testament*, or the *Codicil*, or the remaining books of Raymond <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and major figure in the alchemical tradition</note>; if we lack these, it will not be easy to reach our desired goal. Although I am well aware that there are some excellent books which are nevertheless despised by those ignorant of the nature of things and are considered secondary to old wives' tales, I fear—not without reason—that this offspring of mine <note>An authorial metaphor for his book</note> will be mocked. After all, did this not happen to Lull himself, the most expert man in this discipline? Therefore, since even such a great man did not lack his <term>Zoilus</term> <note>A "Zoilus" is a harsh, malicious critic, named after a Greek grammarian who famously attacked Homer</note>, I easily suspect that traps will be set for my work by the tongues of the envious. 

For it will upset some—even the more learned and those philosophers searching for the hidden secrets of nature—that a work so long concealed by many is being published and made common to everyone. The unlearned, on the other hand, will not stop hissing their disapproval—not because they envy my labor, but because they understand so little of it. 

Therefore, my sweetest Felix, defend this <term>little nighttime study</term> <note>original: *lucubratiunculam*, referring to a work written late at night by lamplight</note> of ours, published under your watchful eye, from the tongues of the envious. Do this all the more because it was not written for a display of knowledge, nor for the sake of winning praise, but so that it might benefit the common race of men. Farewell.

->Exceptional glory of the heroes of Dauphiné,<-
->and love me, as you usually do. From<-
->Fribourg, Switzerland, on the eighth day<-
->before the Kalends of May,<-
-><note>This date corresponds to April 24</note><-
->In the year from the birth<-
->of Christ, 1 5 4 3.<-

<summary>Philipp Ulstad concludes his dedication to Felix de Gennasio by defending his decision to publish secret alchemical knowledge for the public good, despite the potential for criticism from both elites and the unlearned. He dates the work to April 1543 in Fribourg.</summary>
<keywords>Raymond Lull, alchemy, Zoilus, public knowledge, Fribourg, 1543, Dauphiné, scholarship</keywords>

# PROLOGUE.

It is known to everyone that many of the ancient authors have written distinguished volumes, both regarding the <term>Quintessence</term> <note>original: "Quinta Essentia"; the "fifth element" or purest essence of a substance, beyond the four terrestrial elements of earth, water, air, and fire.</note>, as well as <term>potable gold</term> <note>original: "auro potabili"; gold rendered into a liquid, drinkable form, considered the ultimate medicine.</note> and the <term>waters of life</term> <note>original: "aquis vitalibus"; distilled spirits or medicinal waters believed to restore vitality.</note>. It is likewise clear that the same has been done by more recent authors. However, since all those who have published anything concerning this art have written so minutely or concisely, and so obscurely, even the most diligent investigators of the matter have been more likely to fall into error than to obtain any fruit. Knowing how miserably these seekers have been led astray, we could not fail to provide a more certain instruction, which contains the theorems and the actual operation of the art, by which—if followed—we might easily reach the intended result. 

We admit that we have published this book in an unpolished style and perhaps too prematurely; this was done, first, so that those laboring miserably might not lack this medicine during the time it was being kept secret, and also so that this simple language might be more accessible to beginners. But let us now proceed to the task, with a general rule placed at the start.

## GENERAL CANON.

The <term>Philosophical heaven</term> <note>original: "coelo Philosophico"; in alchemical practice, this often refers to the controlled environment within a distillation vessel or the purified state of the "mercury of the philosophers" which receives celestial influences.</note> most perfectly draws to itself the power of the stars that are applied to it, of whatever complexion, nature, or property they may be, according to the authors <term>Lullius</term> <note>original: "Lullio"; Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a major figure in medieval logic and alchemy.</note>, <term>Arnoldus</term> <note>original: "Arnoldo"; Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note>, and <term>Rupescissa</term> <note>original: "Rupescissa"; Johannes de Rupescissa (c. 1310–1366), a Franciscan friar who wrote extensively on the medicinal use of the quintessence.</note>.

<summary>This prologue introduces the author's intent to provide a clear, accessible guide to alchemy and the quintessence to prevent students from being misled by the obscure writings of earlier authors. It establishes a "General Canon" stating that the alchemical environment (the "heaven") attracts stellar influences, citing major authorities like Ramon Llull and Arnold of Villanova.</summary>

<keywords>Prologue, Quintessence, Potable Gold, Water of Life, General Canon, Philosophical Heaven, Ramon Llull, Arnold of Villanova, Johannes de Rupescissa, Alchemical Education</keywords>

# THE HEAVEN
## OF THE PHILOSOPHERS, OR CONCERNING THE
secrets of nature, by Philippus Ulstadius. A book 
most useful to those studious in medicine, dedicated 
to a certain Felix Gennasius.

### What the Quintessence of any simple matter is,
and toward what it contributes.
Chapter one.

<margin>What the quintessence is</margin>
First, therefore, it must be known what the Quintessence of any thing and simple matter is; once this is known, the remaining matters will become clear more easily. For, as <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and philosopher whose works were essential to Renaissance learning.</note> Marcus Cicero testifies: *Every instruction given regarding any matter ought to proceed from a definition, so that it may be understood what the discourse is about.* <note>original: "Omnis ea quæ de re aliqua datur inſtitutio, à definitione proficiſci debet, vt intelligatur de quo ſermo habendus ſit."</note> Therefore, know that the <term>Quintessence</term> <note>Literally the "fifth essence"; in alchemy, it is a substance's purest form, distinct from the four physical elements of earth, water, air, and fire.</note> is the "fifth being" of any thing that possesses <term>form and species</term> <note>Philosophical terms referring to the essential nature and outward appearance of an object.</note>, and a most subtle soul. It is extracted from its body—as if from coarser matter—and from the excess of the four elements by means of the most subtle and extreme <term>distillation</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to create vapor and then cooling it to collect the purified liquid.</note>, the method of which we shall teach below. Furthermore, let no one wonder that this art was unknown to our people for a long time; for many of the ancient philosophers labored in this matter in such a way that they might nourish and cherish human life, grant it the longest duration, delay death, and [reach] the established limit of Nature...

<summary>This page introduces the concept of the Quintessence, defining it as the purest, spiritual essence of a substance extracted through distillation to prolong life. It establishes the work's academic authority by citing Cicero and framing alchemy as a tool for medicine and longevity.</summary>
<keywords>Quintessence, Alchemy, Distillation, Philippus Ulstadius, Marcus Cicero, Four Elements, Longevity, Medical Secrets, Form and Species</keywords>

The transition suggests this text may have been bound or organized in a way where the introductory matter follows the initial chapter, or it serves as the foundation for the specific operations to follow.</meta>

->PROLOGUE.<-

No one is unaware that very many of the ancient authors have written distinguished volumes, both regarding the **Quintessence**, as well as **potable gold** <note>Gold in a liquid, drinkable form, highly prized in alchemy as a universal medicine for longevity and health.</note> and composed **vital waters** <note>original: "aquis vitalibus"; usually refers to *aqua vitae* or distilled alcohol used as a medicinal solvent.</note>; it is likewise evident that the same has been done by more recent authors. However, since all those who have published anything concerning this art have written so minutely, or concisely, and so obscurely, that it is easier for even the most diligent searchers of the matter to fall into error than to obtain any fruit, and as we have recognized that they are miserably led astray, we could not fail to provide a more certain instruction. This instruction contains the theorems and the operation of the art, by observing which we might easily reach the intended result. We have published this book—we confess—in a rough style and perhaps too prematurely; consider that this was done, first, so that those laboring miserably might not lack this medicine at a time when it should have been kept secret, and also so that the plain language might be more accessible to beginners. But let us now proceed to our purpose, having first set down a general rule.

### GENERAL CANON.

The power of the stars, which are applied to the **Philosophical Heaven** <note>A term often used to describe the alchemist's vessel or the purified state of matter that mirrors the celestial realm.</note>, of whatever temperament, nature, or property they may be, is drawn most perfectly to itself by that very heaven, according to the authors <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), and John of Rupescissa (c. 1310–1362) were the primary medieval authorities on distillation and the medicinal quintessence.</note> Lull, Arnold, and Rupescissa.

<summary>In this prologue, the author explains his decision to write a clearer, more accessible guide to alchemy to help those confused by the obscure writings of past masters. He introduces a "General Canon" asserting that the "Philosophical Heaven" attracts the celestial powers of the stars.</summary>

<keywords>Quintessence, potable gold, vital waters, Philosophical Heaven, Ramon Llull, Arnold of Villanova, John of Rupescissa, alchemy theory</keywords>

is the "fifth being" <note>term: Quintessence <gloss>from the Latin 'quinta essentia,' meaning fifth essence</gloss>. ->### BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTAD<-

[Al]though the Essence sometimes takes on the temperament <term>complexio: the specific balance of the four qualities—heat, cold, moisture, and dryness—within a body</term> of the thing joined to it (for whatever tempered thing it is joined to, it draws that temperament to itself), even though it exists by itself, it contains absolutely none of the four qualities within it, not even from the air.

<margin>The excellence of the Quintessence.</margin>
Fourthly and finally, you should know that the Quintessence itself contains nothing of the earthly element—neither cold nor dry—because it cures melancholic illnesses (which are cold and dry). Therefore, in conclusion, you see that it is neither hot nor cold, nor moist nor dry, since it is a thing of a balanced nature <term>temperate: in alchemy, a "temperate" substance is perfectly balanced, possessing the virtues of all qualities without the destructive extremes of any</term>, exceeding all elements that are under heaven. For when it is administered to someone, it makes that body balanced, nor does it depart from its own balance by taking on any of the qualities or temperaments. Nor does it follow that it is a medicine for cold infirmities because it is of a hot nature, nor that it is of a cold nature because it cures hot ones.

For two opposites cannot exist in one body, because one opposite is expelled by the other. Therefore we see that it should not be called hot or cold, nor dry or moist, because it cures consumptives <note>original: "ptisicos," referring to phthisis or pulmonary tuberculosis, traditionally viewed as a "hot and dry" disease</note>, who are hot and dry, and those with dropsy <note>original: "hydropicos," referring to edema or fluid retention, viewed as "moist and cold"</note>, who are moist and cold; instead, all four of those qualities are destroyed and utterly removed within it. And although it is not an element, it is nevertheless a balanced thing, purified through the elements themselves and extracted from the dregs <term>feces: the "dregs" or "lees," referring to the impure, physical remains of matter left behind after distillation</term> of the four elements, which are the primary cause of decay.

<margin>Another definition of the Quintessence.</margin>
These dregs are therefore separated like a very coarse body from its matter, as if from a most subtle soul, through the science of distillations.

A 2
<meta>catchword: Because</meta>

<summary>This page defines the Quintessence as a "temperate" substance that transcends the four elements, arguing that its medical efficacy comes from its lack of extreme qualities (hot, cold, wet, dry). It explains that the substance cures opposing diseases not by being an opposite itself, but by restoring a perfect, incorruptible balance to the body.</summary>

# ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE

<margin>
The Quintessence can be drawn out from all things.
</margin>
Because the <term>Quintessence: literally the "fifth essence," a substance believed by alchemists to be the pure soul of a material, distinct from the four physical elements</term> is a common term for all things that have form and appearance to be extracted from the material itself. Although it is most commonly understood to refer to wine, there are nevertheless many other materials from which this Quintessence can be drawn and elicited—such as from all metals, all fruits, flesh, eggs, roots, herbs, and many others, as will be taught below. Yet, the "fifth being" excels all others because of its extreme subtlety; for this reason, many philosophers have called it the <term>Heaven of the Philosophers: a metaphor comparing the purity of the medicine to the eternal, unchanging nature of the celestial heavens</term>. For just as the heavens relate to the four elements <note>The ancient belief was that the heavens were made of a fifth element (aether) that governed the four earthly elements (earth, air, fire, water)</note>, so too does this "Heaven of the Philosophers" (by which I mean the Quintessence) relate to the four qualities of the human body, which is composed of those very elements. It is also called by some <term>Burning Water: originally "Aqua ardens," an early term for high-proof alcohol or "spirits"</term>, because it burns in fire—provided it has been brought to its perfection and final distillation, leaving no excess moisture in the vessel, as will be taught later in the section on how to recognize it.

<margin>
The Quintessence is given various names by different people.
</margin>
By some, it is called the <term>soul of wine: or "anima vini"</term>. For just as the soul is more noble than its body, so also the Quintessence, which is extracted through true distillation, is more noble than the wine from which it is led forth. It is also named by others <term>Water of Life: "Aqua vitae," a term still used today for certain spirits, though here it refers to its supposed power to prevent the decay of the human body</term>, because it preserves human life from corruption; we see this, for instance, in how it is administered to those suffering from <term>syncope: a sudden loss of consciousness or fainting spell</term>. and because it is extracted in various ways, we shall now speak of the first method of extraction, having provided an illustration of the furnace for distilling the Water of Life.

### How the Fifth Essence is extracted through a still. Chapter Two.

Therefore, having understood what the <term>Fifth Essence <gloss>Latin: Quinta Essentia. The purest, most concentrated medicinal "spirit" of a substance, believed by alchemists to be the same material as the stars.</gloss></term> is, what it is used for, and why it was discovered, we must now weigh and consider the various methods by which it can be extracted and from which substances. Since it is extracted from moist, dry, hot, and cold things, we will first explain how it is drawn from moist things, such as from wine itself. This is done as follows: 

Take the finest red wine, leaning slightly toward sweetness, which has not been mixed or adulterated in any way but is entirely natural. It should not be too fresh, nor too old, but of a balanced age.

For Nature herself (as <note>Galen was a 2nd-century Greek physician; Lullius refers to Ramon Llull, a 13th-century philosopher to whom many alchemical works were attributed.</note> Galen and Lullius say in the *Clarification of Mercury*) can suffer nothing violent without the corruption of its original state.

<margin>Four degrees of fire.</margin>
For there are, as <note>Avicenna, or Ibn Sina, was a Persian polymath whose medical "Canon" was a standard university textbook in Europe for centuries.</note> Avicenna witnesses, four degrees of heat, according to the four complexions or qualities. The first of these is lukewarm, namely like lukewarm water: heated in such a way that no limb placed into it can be harmed. The second degree is indeed hot, such that it can be endured by any human limb without injury. The third degree is hot in such a way that if a limb were placed into it, it could be hurt by that heat, and it is the degree closest to <term>boiling <gloss>Latin: ebulitio.</gloss></term>. The fourth degree of heat is so

### THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTADIUS.

...boiling itself <note>original: *ebullitio*</note>. It is called by some a **bath fire** <note>original: *ignis balnei*; today known as a bain-marie or water bath</note>, a fire of the first degree. A **fire of ashes** <note>original: *ignis cinerum*</note> is a fire of the second degree. Finally, a **burning fire** <note>original: *ignis ardens*</note> is of the third degree. However, the fourth is rejected. According to other practitioners <note>original: *Artistas*</note>, the bath fire is in the first degree. The fire of ashes is in the second degree. The **fire of sand** <note>original: *ignis arenae*</note> is in the third. If one were to proceed to a fire of the fourth degree—which happens very rarely—**iron filings** <note>original: *limatura Martis*; 'Mars' was the alchemical name for the metal iron</note> should be put in place of the sand, and it will be a most scorching fire. And then the <term>cucurbit <gloss>The gourd-shaped glass vessel that holds the substance to be distilled.</gloss></term> itself must be <insert>sealed</insert> all over with clay <note>original: *lutari*; the process of 'luting' involved coating glass vessels to protect them from cracking under intense heat</note>, just as is done in the distillation of **aqua fortis** <note>literally "strong water," referring to nitric acid</note>, so that it does not break from excessive heat, because the iron filings become somewhat incandescent. And by these four last degrees, you will be able to govern the fire best, though according to **Avicenna** <note>A famous Persian physician (c. 980–1037) whose medical and alchemical theories were highly influential in Europe</note> you can barely endure it, or only with the greatest labor. Concerning this, I wish the diligent follower of this 

<margin>Fire must not be increased up to the fourth degree in the art of distilling.</margin>
art—that is, whoever wishes to perform the service of distilling—to note that in any distillation whatsoever, the fire must never be increased up to the fourth degree, because the fire would be more violent than the nature of the material to be distilled can bear, and consequently, that nature itself could be violated by the excessive and violent heat. Therefore, this fourth method is rejected by all practitioners. And so there are certain spice-sellers <note>original: *aromatarii*</note>, pharmacists <note>original: *pharmacopolae*</note>, and others of that kind, to whom the 

<margin>To Philosophers and doctors, not spice-sellers.</margin>
faculty of distilling is undeservedly permitted, since it is the particular duty of **Philosophers** and **Doctors** (who are the investigators of nature). For nature itself was so ordered by God the Best and Greatest that it can tolerate nothing vehement without its own corruption; as can be proven from the opinions of many Philosophers. Therefore

<meta>This page continues the technical classification of heat, specifically moving from the general definitions of the four degrees to their practical application in the sequence of seven distillations.</meta>

# ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE.

<margin>
...ries, and everywhere it was anciently permitted to no worthless pharmacists <note>original: *pharmacopolis*; meaning a seller of drugs or an apothecary</note> to distill.
</margin>

I advise the **lovers of this art** <note>original: *huius artis amatoribus*; a common term for students of alchemy</note> that they should in no way involve themselves in this excellent work—given to us by divine providence for the preservation of human life—unless they have a perfect knowledge of these **four degrees of heat**, and know how to temper them and the fire as must be done in all things. And therefore, in the **first distillation**, the fire must be made with gentle coals, or with very dry wood, up to the end of the **third degree of heat**, so that the <term>bath <gloss>A water bath or "bain-marie" used to heat vessels gently and evenly.</gloss></term> almost boils.

<margin>
How one must descend in the tempering of fire during distillation.
</margin>

In the **second distillation**, however, you will descend while making the fire, and you will decrease it by a third part of one degree, or to two-thirds, and nearly to one degree, so that it is diminished as if down to the **second degree**. In the **third distillation**, you will always descend in this way, gradually diminishing the fire until it is reduced to the **second degree and one-third part** of a degree. In the **fourth**, let it be diminished down to the **second degree**. In the **fifth**, you will descend so that the fire is at **one degree and two-thirds** of a degree. In the **sixth**, let it decrease so that it is reduced to the **first degree with one-third**. In the **seventh** and final distillation, the fire is to be so softened that it is brought to the **first degree of heat** (which is **tepidity**), and you shall not descend further. And note that every degree of heat has three intervals: namely, the **beginning**, the **middle**, and the **end**. This is further to be understood in this way—why the degrees of heat must always be diminished—because in the first distillation the <term>matter is at its coarsest <gloss>The raw, unrefined substance before the "spirit" has been separated from the "dregs."</gloss></term>, so that it can in no way easily obey the distillation because of its impurity and raw substance, and because of this, it is not like the other following distillatio-

<meta>catchword: -ns (from "distillationibus")</meta>

<summary>Ulstadius provides specific instructions for a seven-stage distillation process, explaining that the heat must be gradually lowered as the substance becomes more refined and less "coarse." He emphasizes that mastery of the four degrees of heat is essential for any practitioner of the alchemical arts.</summary>

# THE BOOK OF PHILIPP ULSTAD.

[...of the distillatio]ns, whether the second or third, and others following. Therefore, in the **first distillation**, the fire is to be raised through one degree up to the **third**, so that it reaches the final part of the third degree, and the <term>bath <gloss>The *balneum Mariae* or water bath, used to ensure the substance is heated evenly without burning.</gloss></term> should be very hot, yet it must not boil. In the **second distillation**, one must act more gently, because through the first process the coarseness of the matter has been somewhat thinned <note>The author uses the term "attenuated," meaning the substance is becoming more refined and "spiritual" in the alchemical sense.</note>, and therefore it does not require such a great fire. And in the **third distillation**, one acts even more subtly, and so always descending little by little in each distillation, diminishing the fire. For as has already been said quite often, if you were to force the substance itself which is to be distilled too much, nature itself would be corrupted. For **Galen** and **Aristotle** <note>These two figures represent the pinnacle of medical and philosophical authority in the 16th century; Ulstad cites them to give his chemical advice scientific weight.</note> say that Nature is incompatible with, and intolerant of, more violent motion.

The art of distilling consists in tempering the degrees of fire.

### ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE.

...and frequently allowed to worthless <term>pharmacopolists <gloss>A historical term for street-selling pharmacists or apothecaries, often used disparagingly here to imply they lacked true alchemical knowledge.</gloss></term> of old, by whom it was distilled.

I advise lovers of this art that regarding this excellent work, given to us by divine providence for the preservation of human life, they should by no means involve themselves unless they have a perfect knowledge of these four degrees of heat, and know how to temper them and the fire as must be done in all things. And therefore, in the **first <term>distillation <gloss>The process of heating a liquid to create vapor and then condensing it back into a liquid to purify or separate its components.</gloss></term>** the fire must be made with gentle coals, or with very dry wood, up until the end of the third degree of heat, so that the <term>water bath <gloss>The *balneum Mariae*, a vessel of hot water used to heat the distillation flask gently and evenly.</gloss></term> almost boils.

<margin>
How one must descend in the tempering of the fire during distillation.
</margin>

In the **second distillation**, however, you will descend, making the fire and diminishing it by a third part of one degree, or to two-thirds, and almost to one degree, so that it is, as it were, diminished down to the second degree.

In the **third distillation**, you will always descend in this way, gradually diminishing the fire, until it is reduced to the second degree and a third part of one degree. In the **fourth**, however, let it be diminished down to the second degree. In the **fifth**, you will descend so that the fire is at one degree and two-thirds of a degree. In the **sixth**, let it decrease so that it is reduced to the first degree with one-third. In the **seventh** and final distillation, the fire is to be so softened that it is brought to the first degree of heat (which is **<term>tepidity <gloss>A gentle, lukewarm heat, similar to the temperature of a human body.</gloss></term>**), and you shall not descend further. And note that any degree of heat has three intervals: namely, the beginning, the middle, and the end. Furthermore, this is to be understood in this way: the reason why the degrees of heat must always be diminished is because in the first distillation the matter is at its coarsest, so that it cannot easily obey the distillation in any way on account of its impurity and crude substance; and through this, it is not similar to the other subsequent distillations...

<summary>Ulstad provides precise instructions for a seven-stage distillation process, explaining that the heat must be gradually reduced as the substance becomes more refined and "spiritual." He warns that only those who master the four degrees of heat should attempt this work, as the material starts in a "coarse" state and requires less force as it purifies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for tempering heat during the seven-fold distillation process. It transitions from the fifth distillation discussed on the previous page to the general logic of diminishing heat as the substance becomes more refined.</meta>

### THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTADIUS. 7

...[distillati]ons, of the second or third, and others following. And therefore in the **first distillation**, the fire is to be raised through one degree up to the third, so that it reaches the final part of the third degree, and the <term>bath <gloss>The *balneum Mariae* or water bath, used to ensure even, indirect heating.</gloss></term> should be very hot, yet it must not boil. In the **second distillation**, one must act more gently, because through the first, the thickness of the matter has been somewhat thinned out, and therefore it does not stand in need of such a great fire. And in the **third**, so much more subtly; and so always descending little by little in each distillation, you diminish the fire. Because, as has now been said quite often, if you force the substance itself which must be distilled too much, nature itself would be corrupted.

<margin>
The art of distilling consists in tempering the degrees of fire.
</margin>

For <term>Galen <gloss>An influential Greek physician (129–c. 216 AD) whose theories dominated Western medical science for over a millennium.</gloss></term> and <term>Aristotle <gloss>The Greek philosopher whose physics and theories on the elements formed the basis of alchemical thought.</gloss></term> say that Nature is incompatible with <note>original: "incompatibilis"</note> more vehement motion. And therefore, it must be looked into with all possible diligence, and surveyed

### Chapter Three.

<margin>The seven degrees of putrefaction.</margin>
Every care and precise industry must be used to consider the substance of the matter to be digested, whether it be hard or soft: that is, whether it is coarse or subtle. One must consider by what means or art it can be putrefied or digested, so that it might better submit to distillation, and so that the pure might be sequestered from the impure, the coarse from the subtle, and vice versa. Therefore, whatever the substance may be from which you wish to extract the Fifth Essence <term>Quintessence; the purest, most refined part of a substance, believed to be the "spirit" of the material</term> through distillation, let it first and before all else rot and be digested in horse manure <note>original: "fimo equino"; horse manure was a standard laboratory tool for alchemists, providing a gentle, steady, and natural heat for long periods</note>, to which no accidental color is attributed. Let it sit for two months, and then again between the first and second distillation for one month; between the second and third for three weeks; between the third and fourth for fifteen days; between the fourth and fifth for eight days; between the fifth and sixth for four days; and between the sixth and seventh for two days. And you must know that the manure must always maintain the same level of heat, because if the heat should fail, the circulation of the water <note>refers to the movement of vapors rising and falling within the sealed vessel</note> would be ruined; consequently, the matter itself which ought to be reduced into the Fifth Essence would be separated in the "heat of heaven" <note>original: "calore cœli"; this refers to the refined state of the substance where it mimics celestial purity</note>. You will be able to see this in the dividing line which separates the Fifth Essence—which is the upper part—from its dregs <term>feces; the solid, impure residue left at the bottom</term>, which is the lower part. And here you shall note that these degrees of digestion and putrefaction of the matter are to be assigned just as was described above regarding the degrees of heat. For this reason, a longer period of putrefaction is required before the first distillation than afterward. Once the first distillation is completed, the matter itself no longer contains as much coarseness within it, and it is more fit for making 

<meta>The text cuts off here, continuing with "the Fifth Essence" on the following page.</meta>

...[distilla]tion and the burning of a linen cloth, it is not a certain rule, but it is a true indication that some watery substance still remained within it <note>original: "non est ratum, sed est verum inditium." This refers to the "linen test": if you soak a cloth in alcohol and light it, the cloth should only burn if the alcohol is pure and free of water</note>. Therefore, effort must always be applied so that it is digested as many times and for as long as needed until a successful test can be performed on it. There are some, however, who say that sugar <note>original: "zuccarum"</note> should be added, and if the wine is well rectified, the sugar will be completely burned away along with the wine itself.

You will note this, therefore: wine digested in this way cannot yet be called the **Fifth Essence**, <margin>Subject of the Fifth Essence</margin> but rather the *subject* of the Fifth Essence. However, when the linen cloth is burned along with it, and when it has completely lost every horrible, sharp, and sulfurous taste—so that it seems almost sweet to the taster, and no smokiness remains in the burning itself—then at that time you can truly call it the **Fifth Essence**.

But in things that are insensible and have no taste, one must pay attention to the smoke, as is the case with gold, silver, other metals, and precious stones; from these, the Fifth Essence is sometimes extracted, which enters into <term>potable gold; "aurum potabile," a legendary medicinal liquid made from dissolved gold, believed to be a universal cure</term>. Indeed, the vessels for circulatory distillation are diverse, handed down by various authors. For there are some who make a vessel in such a way that a <term>blind alembic; "alembicus caecus," a distillation cap without a spout, which prevents vapors from escaping and forces them to condense and fall back down</term> is placed upon a <term>cucurbit; "cucurbita," a gourd-shaped glass vessel used as the base in distillation</term>, and in the highest part they make a small hole through which the material is put in or taken out when it must be distilled circulation-wise; and it is stopped up with a small piece of glass made for that purpose so that it is well-sealed on all sides, lest the matter be able to breathe out. A closure can also be made of silver, yet in such a way that the hole is completely obstructed. And this vessel is called by some a <term>Pelican; a famous alchemical vessel with two curved "arms" connecting the top to the bottom, named for the myth of the pelican feeding its young with its own blood</term>. ->BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTADIUS. 12<-

B 4

## How, and in how many ways, distillation for circulation should be done. Chapter Five.

And when you have learned, and have experienced through the proof written above, that the aforementioned material has been sufficiently **digested** <term>digestion; a process of slow, gentle heating used to soften and prepare a substance</term> through frequent **putrefaction** <note>In alchemy, "putrefaction" is the stage where a substance breaks down or "dies" so it can be reborn in a purified state.</note>, and you have vessels fit for **circulation** <term>circulation; the process where vapors rise and flow back into the liquid repeatedly to refine it</term>, place the material in the said noblest vessel for circulation. Then, bury it in **horse dung** <note>original: "fimum æquinum"; decomposing manure was used as a reliable "biological furnace" to provide steady, low heat for months at a time.</note>, or during the **Dog Days** <note>original: "diebus Canicularibus"; the hottest days of summer, usually late July to August, associated with the rising of the Dog Star, Sirius.</note> place it in the sun, or in a **Mary’s bath** <term>balneum Mariæ; a water bath or bain-marie</term> heated up to the first degree of heat, or a little more.
<margin>Three ways of distilling for circulation.</margin>
This should be done according to how you see the material has been tempered and refined through digestion. Let it be left there for a long time, until it is converted into the **Fifth Essence** <term>Quinta Essentia; the purest, most medicinal part of a substance, separate from the four elements</term> you desire. You will be able to recognize this from the taste itself, as was said above. And note carefully, that if there appears at the bottom of the vessel a **hypostasis** <note>original: "ypostesis"; a Greek-derived term for sediment or dregs that settle at the bottom of a liquid.</note>, which is to say a certain cloudy little cloud, it must be separated from its material as soon as possible through distillation. Once this is done, let it be circulated again as it was begun. Or, if it can be conveniently done, it should be strained into another circulatory vessel so that the coarseness of the material is left behind in the first vessel.

Therefore, when the diligent seeker of this art perceives the Fifth Essence in the refining and purification of the material of wine or fruits, let him judge and believe that a similar thing happens in all others of this kind. And for this reason, one should firmly believe the opinion and judgment of **Aristotle** <note>The author cites the ancient Greek philosopher to give scientific weight to the idea that highly refined substances change their very nature.</note>: that matter thus ennobled and **immaterialized** <note>original: "immaterialata"; meaning the substance has been stripped of its physical "coarseness" to become more like a spirit.</note> through distillation cannot be further reduced to any [other] form or spirituality, since it is without elemental substance, exceeding the [normal] state of matter.

...and corruption, and therefore it is like a celestial body. For just as the heavens relate to all things below, so too the **Fifth Essence** <term>Quinta Essentia; the purest part of a substance, believed to be a "heavenly" medicine</term> relates to all temperaments and qualities. And just as all stars follow their course through the noble influence of the **First Mover** <note>original: "primi mobilis"; in medieval cosmology, this was the outermost sphere of the universe that moved all other celestial bodies.</note>, so all medicines with which the Fifth Essence is mixed draw their power from it, just as a **lodestone** <note>original: "adamas"; while this can mean diamond, in this context it refers to a magnet.</note> draws iron. But because it requires such great labor and produces much weariness, and for that reason is disdained by everyone, it happens that in our age very few philosophers are found who devote their efforts to this business.

### *How Circulation and Digestion differ from one another.*
## Chapter Six.

One must consider most carefully the difference between **circulation** and **digestion**. First, we must speak of the glass vessels necessary for each of these. Therefore, you must have for **digestion** <term>digestion; the process of softening a substance through gentle heat</term> instruments and **cucurbits** <term>cucurbita; a gourd-shaped glass vessel used as the base of a still</term> that are wide in the upper part, so that thick substances can be placed inside them. Upon these must be placed **blind alembics** <term>alembicus caecus; a still-head without a spout or "beak," designed to send vapors back down rather than out</term>, without a beak, so that when the substance has been digested in them, you can extract the thick material once the blind alembic is removed and another with a beak is put in its place. However, if the material is clear—as it is once it has been distilled and the dregs are not mixed with the distillate, such as in the Fifth Essence of wine—then a circulatory vessel can be used, so that it is equal and wide in form both above and below. In the middle, however, it ought to be narrow, and near the top of the lower belly it should have a pipe the length of one fin—

**Pelicans**, the form of which is this. In this vessel, things can be clearly **digested**, and anything can also be **circulated** <term>circulation; a process where vapors rise, condense, and flow back into the base liquid in a continuous loop</term>. But these glasses, since they are of difficult workmanship, cannot be made by our local glassmakers. I myself have a very dear friend at Freiburg who, in the regions of **Dauphiné** <note>original: "Delphinatus"; a former province in southeastern France</note> near **Vienne**, wished to have such a pelican made by a certain glassmaker, but he could not obtain it for the price of three **Tours pounds** <note>original: "librarum Thuronésium"; the Livre Tournois, a standard French currency of the period</note>, which among us are worth two **Rhenish florins**. 

<margin>The best vessel for circulation.</margin>

Whatever the case may be, know that just as this instrument surpasses all others in price, so too is it nobler for all purposes than any other circulating vessel that can be made. And therefore I advise all students of this work that if they have need of this glass—provided they can find an experienced glassmaker—they should by no means spare silver or gold to acquire it.

<margin>The difference between circulation and digestion.</margin>

There is, therefore, a difference between **fermenting** or **digesting**, and **circulating**, and especially in this: one must consider with all their mental strength how the glass is to be positioned. When you wish to **digest**, let a single **cucurbit** <term>cucurbita; the gourd-shaped lower part of a still</term> with a **blind alembic** <term>alembicus caecus; a still-head without a spout that forces vapors back down</term> be taken. If, however, the matter has already been purified, let the circulating vessel be taken, and let the matter to be digested be placed within it; then let a **pit** <note>likely referring to a sand bath or "athanor" furnace pit for controlled, indirect heat</note> be prepared, just as was written above, in which let there be made...

<meta>catchword: be made</meta>

<summary>This page describes the 'Pelican,' a specialized glass vessel for constant circulation, noting its high cost and the difficulty of its manufacture. It also begins to distinguish the practical laboratory setups required for digestion versus those used for circulation.</summary>

<keywords>Pelican vessel, circulation, digestion, alchemical glassware, Freiburg, Dauphiné, Rhenish florins, cucurbit, blind alembic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for alchemical apparatus, specifically focusing on the construction and value of the "Pelican" vessel and distinguishing between the physical setups for digestion versus circulation.</meta>

# ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE.

...a layer should be made of powdered quicklime <term>quicklime; calcium oxide, which generates intense heat when mixed with water</term>, three or four fingers thick. Upon this, another layer of seven or eight fingers should be made of horse manure <term>horse manure; used in alchemy as a source of gentle, steady heat through fermentation</term> that is not too soft or rotten, nor too hard. And all around the circulating vessel, manure should be placed at the same height as the layer itself. Once this is done, the pit is filled with earth to a height of one and a half feet, and water is poured over the glass, so that by descending through the manure into the lime, it heats the manure itself. Every week, the lime and manure should be renewed twice. One could also place fresh manure or grape pomace <term>pomace; the pulpy residue remaining after grapes are crushed for wine</term> into the pit without the lime. When you wish to remove the glass—once the matter is sufficiently digested—it should be taken out carefully, so that it is not shaken or broken by any violent movement. 

When you wish to perform **circulation** <term>circulation; a process where vapors rise, condense, and flow back into the base liquid in a continuous loop</term>, the glass must be put in place in every way and manner previously described. Except for this: the circulating vessel should not be hidden in its uppermost part or obstructed at the bottom; rather, at least the lower part of the instrument should be placed in the lime or manure up to the middle point. At the very least, a third part of it must be outside the manure and exposed to the air, so that what rises into the neck of the circulator from the heat may descend again due to the cold. This can also be done in a **Bain-Marie** <term>balneo Mariae; a water bath used for gentle, uniform heating</term>, or in hot sand, or by placing the material to be circulated in the sun during summer days, or by placing it in fresh grape pomace during the autumn after the clusters have been pressed, as has already been noted quite often in the preceding sections.

Works and Days: God does not give all things to one man, etc. <note>original: "Opera & dies: Non uni dat cuncta deus"; a reference to the poet Hesiod, suggesting that mastery of all skills is rare.</note> And since we see so very few laboring in the aforementioned way—on account of the excessive labor, which always produces weariness and nausea, and because of the great expenses that are not undeservedly applied to this noble work—we have therefore decided to hand down a shorter and easier method, requiring less expense. For I know there are many students of this art who, from the mere reading of our work, might be deterred from this noble labor because of the costs. Therefore, we shall prescribe for them another way of extracting the **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence; also known as the Quintessence, the purest and most potent medicinal spirit extracted from matter</term> of wine, without great labor and expense, and it is as follows: Take the best wine you can find, whether it be white or red, provided it is not very sweet, and you shall distill this through an **alembic** <term>alembic; a classic distillation apparatus consisting of a boiling pot and a cooling cap with a snout</term> four times, just as **burning water** <term>burning water; aqua ardens, or high-proof alcohol that can be ignited</term> or **water of life** <term>water of life; aqua vitae, a concentrated medicinal spirit</term> is commonly made among us; and if it can be done more often, it would be better, so that

### ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE.

When, therefore, this matter has been placed in a vessel that is well **luted** <term>luted; sealed with a specialized paste or "lute" to make it airtight</term> and cemented with strong clay, so that the powers of the matter cannot evaporate, then—through the frequent ascent and descent <note>This refers to the process of reflux, where vapors rise, condense, and drip back down.</note>—the **water of life** <term>water of life; aqua vitae, a high-proof distilled spirit used as a medicinal base</term> itself will be converted into the true **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence; the Quintessence, believed to be a fifth element beyond Earth, Air, Fire, and Water that is pure and incorruptible</term>. And the wine thus distilled, as was said above, although it contains all four elements, nevertheless, through the constant motion and agitation caused by the ascent and descent, it is converted and passes from a corruptible state into one that is, in a certain way, incorruptible.

If, therefore, it is seen through the frequent ascent and descent of distillation that the impure is separated from the pure, and the coarse from the subtle, how much more does this occur in the distilling vessel, where it is agitated upward and downward a thousand thousand times? How much more should it be considered that the **elemented** <note>original: "elementatam"; matter composed of the four base elements</note> matter is purified <note>original: "putreficari"; in alchemy, "putrefaction" often refers to a necessary stage of breakdown and purification rather than simple rotting.</note> and converted into an **unelemented** substance and an incorruptible body. Therefore, just as heaven itself is more incorruptible than the four elements, <margin>The Fifth Essence is incorruptible.</margin> so much more is the Fifth Essence more incorruptible than an elemented body.

When, however, that **sublimation** <term>sublimation; here referring to the repeated rising of vapors in the flask</term> has been performed many times in the aforementioned distiller, let the hole that is at the top of the **pelican** <term>pelican; a specialized circulatory flask with "arms" that feed condensed liquid back into the base</term> finally be opened. From its most precious and fragrant odor, you will be able to know if there is anything of the four elements remaining in the matter which ought to be converted into the Fifth Essence. Because if it has perfectly arrived at the Fifth Essence, a most fragrant odor will come out of the **circulatory vessel** <note>original: "circulatorio"; another name for the Pelican vessel</note>, so precious that we seem to be raised to heaven by its scent and extreme sweetness, and it appears in some way to be a heavenly fragrance. And if that vapor enters any secret room of the house, it will so

# THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTAD.

[It] will fill the entire house, so that you will think you have never smelled anything sweeter, more pleasant, or finally more fragrant, to the point that it is a wonder to speak of and almost incredible. If it is also placed at the top of a tower, it will attract all the birds that are in the neighborhood to itself. If, however, it happens that it does not give off such an aroma, let the **pelican** <term>pelican; a specialized circulatory distillation vessel with two "arms" that return condensed vapors to the body</term> be closed again and well **luted** <term>luted; sealed with airtight paste</term>, and let it be put back for **circulatory distillation** <note>refluxing; a process where vapors rise and fall repeatedly to achieve extreme purity</note> for as long as it takes until the **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence; the Quintessence, the highest and purest state of a substance</term> itself is produced, or—as **Raymond** <note>Refers to Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and alchemist whose works Ulstad frequently cites.</note> says in book one, chapter two—the **Vegetable Mercury** <term>Vegetable Mercury; a term for the highly rectified spirit of wine, considered the "mercury" or life-force of plants</term>, which you will always be able to recognize by the aforementioned odor. 

And it possesses not only that most excellent odor and flavor, but also a certain incorruptible quality compared to other medicines; it certainly has no burning sensation in the mouth like **water of life** <term>aqua vitae; concentrated alcohol</term> does, nor any humidity or **phlegm** <note>original: "phlegma"; here referring to watery, impure residues or the cold/wet humor</note>, because all earthly and elemental matter remains at the bottom. 

<margin>The Fifth Essence is not entirely incorruptible.</margin>
And just as heaven itself is composed of matter and form, so too is the Fifth Essence, and for this reason, it is compared to heaven itself. Yet it is not entirely devoid of all corruption; for if it were most perfect in its incorruptibility, it would, without any obstacle, make our bodies perpetual and eternal. This is not permitted by the Creator of all creatures themselves, since He has established for us a limit of life which we cannot pass, as the **Psalmist** <note>original: "Psalmographus"; referring to the author of the Biblical Psalms, likely citing Psalm 90 or 102 regarding the span of human life.</note> says, and as **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, Roman Stoic philosopher.</note> witnesses: *Nothing is more certain than death, and nothing more uncertain than the hour of death* <note>original: "Nihil certius est morte, & nihil incertius hora mortis."</note>. Therefore, when any substance is converted into the Fifth Essence, this is not a divine act but a natural one; nevertheless, it occurs with the aid and help of Almighty God, without whom nothing is made.

### On the easy method of circulatory distillation, and extracting the Fifth Essence without fire. Chapter Eight.

<margin>
A double way of extracting the Fifth Essence without any fire.
</margin>
If in this most excellent work you desire to avoid all expenses, and (what is greatest) the consumption and loss of time, keep also this twofold path of extracting the **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence; the Quintessence, or the purest spiritual core of a substance</term>; you will be able to do this without any fire or coals. The first way is this: Let horse dung <note>original: "fimus æquinus"; decomposing manure was used by early chemists as a source of gentle, steady heat, often called a "horse's belly" (venter equinus)</note> be taken, and let it be placed in a certain large and deep vessel, or in a pit made for this purpose, and in the middle of the dung let a distillation vessel be placed, filled with the material to be distilled to two-thirds [capacity]. Let a third part of the vessel remain empty above the dung <note>original: "extra fumum"; likely a typesetter's error for "fimum" (dung), or referring to the heat/vapor rising from it</note>, because Nature desires this, so that the matter can have ascent and descent <note>the process of evaporation and condensation within a single vessel</note>, and can consequently be converted into the clearest water. This is done without any labor or fire, although it is necessary to renew the dung every week, at least once. This can also be done in **grape pomace** <term>vinacea; the skins and seeds left over after pressing grapes</term>, as was said above, provided it is taken at the time of the vintage, immediately when the grapes have been pressed in the wine-press; in this you will see a wonderful operation. Or it can even be done during the **dog days** <term>dog days; the hottest part of summer, usually July and August, when the sun's heat is strongest</term> in the sun. And therefore divine providence has provided equally for the poor and the rich, so that they might have a method of practicing this art.

<margin>
From every wine, even the most impure, but not vinegary, the Fifth Essence can be extracted.
</margin>
The Fifth Essence can also be extracted from cloudy, impure, and rotten wine, provided however it is not vinegary <note>original: "acetosum"; wine that has turned to vinegar was thought to have lost its "spirit" or Fifth Essence</note>, because then it must be presumed that the Fifth Essence itself has exhaled and evaporated from such wine, as **Lullius** <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a famous philosopher often cited in alchemical texts</note> says in book one, chapter three. And although the wine may be rotten and of the worst substance, nevertheless the Fifth Essence always remains in it, which can be known from this: For since...

Let the glass be turned over, and let it be placed with the neck downward, as has been mentioned many times above, so that the thickness <note>original: "grossicies"; referring to the sediment or impurities</note> of the material resides in the bottom of the distillation vessel itself. When it has been buried below <note>likely buried in the earth or a similar cool, stable environment to allow for slow settling or natural maturation</note> for a long period of time, let it finally be gently removed from there in the same manner as it was placed.

### ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE.

Then you will see the separation of the pure from the impure, and the subtle from the coarse; all the coarse matter will gather around the neck of the glass. Take, therefore, a fine and sharp **iron stylus** <term>stilus ferreus; a pointed tool used for marking or piercing</term>, and let it be thrust from below into the wax until it is perforated all the way to the water. Afterward, pull out that stylus, and the **coarseness** <note>original: "grossicies"; referring to the solid impurities or sediment</note> of the earthly matter which was residing in the neck of the distillation vessel will flow out. And after you have separated it all until only the subtlety of the **Fifth Essence** <term>Quinta Essentia; the purest, most refined part of a substance</term> remains, let the distillation vessel be turned over while the opening is blocked with a finger; the residue that remains in the glass is the Fifth Essence itself. However, this is not as precious as the one we spoke of above.

In a similar way, **burning water** <term>aqua ardens; a high-proof alcohol or brandy</term> can be taken and placed in a glass—positioned upright so that it is not turned over—and thus buried in **dung** <note>fimus; horse manure was used in alchemy to provide a constant, gentle, "natural" heat for digestion or incubation</note> for some time. Afterward, the coarse matter which resides at the bottom should be strained from the subtler part remaining around the neck of the vessel; yet even this is not as effective as the first method. It does, however, attract all the virtues of herbs and other materials, and therefore it is not to be entirely despised. There are also much easier ways found for the needy and the poor, who cannot always devote **oil and labor** <note>a Latin idiom "oleum et operam perdere" meaning to waste time and resources, here used literally regarding the cost of fuel for furnaces</note> to this work. But I piously advise that you follow the first way, since it is the best and most excellent, and is accomplished with very little labor and little silver. Nor should it trouble you to spend a little time on this or to bestow a small amount of money. For you will find, once it has been perfected, a thousandfold profit in it.

### On the method of extracting the **Quintessence** <term>Quinta Essentia; the "fifth essence" or purest part of a substance, believed to hold life-extending properties</term> from the four elements, in order to dissolve the **Sun** <note>In alchemy, "the Sun" (Sol) refers to gold</note> within it for **potable gold** <term>auro potabili; a legendary medicinal liquid made from dissolved gold, thought to cure all ailments and restore youth</term>. # THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTAD.

<note>sig: C 2</note>
<meta>catchword: proba=</meta>

...for the poor and the rich, and with which not only the powers of gold, silver, copper, and other metals, but also of precious stones and herbs are usually drawn out when they are immersed in it. But nevertheless, I am not unaware that many scholars desire to go further; I am therefore compelled to relate what I have gathered from frequent reading

<margin>Experience is the mistress of all things.</margin>

and from experience, which is the mistress of all things: namely, the method of extracting the **Quintessence** <term>Quinta Essentia; the "fifth essence" or purest spiritual core of a substance, believed to be the life-force of the material</term> from wine, in which indeed gold, silver, pearls, and precious stones, and other metals can be dissolved, in order to make **potable gold** <term>aurum potabile; a legendary liquid medicine made of dissolved gold, prized as a universal cure</term>, which nonetheless is more suitable for **Artists** <note>In this context, "Artists" refers to practicing alchemists</note> who search the streams of **Alchemy** <term>Alchimia; the medieval forerunner of chemistry, focused on transmuting matter and finding the elixir of life</term> than for physicians. And although there are many ancient writers who teach the way to extract the Quintessence of wine through the separation of the **four elements** <note>The classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire</note>, so that they are brought back into a single substance and being—which indeed, having been so refined and as it were extracted from the humors and all superfluity of the four elements, can be called the Quintessence for the dissolving of the **Sun** <note>original: "Solis"; the alchemical name for gold</note>. Through this, therefore, we will not only be able to dissolve the metal itself, but also to extract all its powers so that it can enter into the potable gold. But certainly, whoever has looked into the matter deeply will judge it to belong more to Alchemy than to Medicine. Truly, I do not deny that many of the ancients found methods of this kind for dissolving the Sun; however, they adapted them more for Alchemy than for Medicine. By this art, therefore, many have been deceived, thinking that gold is made from such a Quintessence. And although

<margin>Gold is not made from the Quintessence.</margin>

in many respects it is consistent, and has the weight, luster, and **ring** <note>original: "clangorem"; the distinct metallic resonance or "clink" of true gold</note> of gold, yet in our time it truly...

<summary>Ulstad describes how the Quintessence of wine can dissolve gold and gems to create "potable gold," a potent medicine. He cautions that while this substance mimics the properties of gold, it belongs to the realm of alchemy and cannot actually create the metal from scratch.</summary>

### ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE.

<margin>The false color of gold is tested by Antimony</margin>
...[the gold] is judged to be false by testing, when it is hammered into the thinnest plates and dissolved in **strong water** <term>Aqua fortis; nitric acid, a powerful corrosive used by alchemists to separate gold from other metals</term> along with other metals, or even tested by **Antimony** <term>Antimonium; a metallic element used in refining; it was known as the "wolf of metals" because it "devoured" impurities, leaving only pure gold behind</term> (which is the final punishment <note>original: "ultimum supplicium"; a metaphor for the final, most intense stage of purification</note> of gold), by which a fake and foreign color is precisely recognized. Therefore, **Arnaldus de Villanova** <note>A Catalan physician and alchemist (c. 1240–1311) famous for his medical and alchemical treatises</note> says: Wine in which a plate of gold has been extinguished <note>extincta; the process of quenching a red-hot metal in liquid to transfer its "virtue" or properties to the liquid</note> forty or fifty times is held by some in the place of **potable gold** <term>aurum potabile; a legendary liquid medicine made of dissolved gold, believed to grant longevity</term>. From this also **John of Rupescissa** <note>A 14th-century Franciscan whose works on the "Quintessence" revolutionized alchemical medicine</note> says: That the wine produced by the extinction of gold plates would be made better if the gold itself had been **calcined** <term>calcinatum; the process of heating a substance until it is reduced to a fine, friable powder</term> and brought to a very fine dust, or if it were rolled into very thin sheets and afterwards cut into small pieces, because the virtue <note>virtus; the inherent medicinal power or spiritual essence of a substance</note> would be more easily extracted from it if it is cut so finely than if it is merely in sheets.

<margin>The power of preserving human life is in natural gold, not alchemical gold.</margin>
Let it be, however, natural gold found in the minerals of the earth, and not that which has been made by human subtlety and industry <note>Referring to "synthetic" gold produced through alchemical transmutation</note>. For this [alchemical gold] has no power to preserve human life, but is rather a deception and a clouding of the eyes. For who among the Alchemists would dare to boast that he has ever composed gold without poisonous matter (which is **Mercury** <term>Mercurius; quicksilver, believed to be the "mother" of all metals but considered naturally toxic unless perfectly purified</term>)? And therefore, truly, this gold of the Alchemists—although it may have the color of true gold, as well as the shape, the ring <note>the sound a metal makes when struck</note>, and the weight—nevertheless does not have the same power as mineral gold. Such an **Essence of wine** can therefore be best extracted from the wine itself, according to the opinion and teaching of the ancients. I shall relate their method (omitting other wordy ambiguities). Take gold, and hammer it very finely so that it may be resolved into a potable form of the color of gold. With which, either by itself or with other medicines added, we will be able...

### THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTAD

...we are first to use, as will be taught below. Although another way has also been discovered to dissolve gold with **strong water** <term>Aqua fortis; nitric acid</term>, or with distilled vinegar, or through distilled human urine—of which it is not fitting to speak in a medical context, since these substances are more corrosive to nature than they are healthy. And although something of this sharp quality can be removed from such matter (as some people wish), it is nonetheless not safe, and not truly believable. Even if it could be done, I would still not want to use it. For our Philosophers say: between two uncertainties, the more certain path must be chosen, and the more uncertain must be fled. 

For that reason, we will demonstrate a much easier and more refined way of separating the **Quintessence** <note>The "fifth essence" or purest part of a substance, believed to be the medicinal soul of the matter</note> from the four elements, and how to digest them into one. This is done through the sharpness that is mixed within the Quintessence itself, combined with a subtlety through which gold, silver, and other metals can be dissolved. 

<margin>Two ways of separating the Quintessence from the four elements.</margin>

Furthermore, we will show how to extract the virtue from it, so that it can be reduced into a potable form similar to water. It is through this inherent sharpness that the metals themselves are dissolved, and by nothing else, provided that the four elements are not yet separated and the earth itself is **calcined** <term>Reduced to powder by heat</term>—that is, through burning, it is turned into salt, lime, or ash, in the form of a certain stone called by some of the ancients the **Philosophers' Stone** <note>original: "Lapis Philosophorum"; the legendary substance sought by alchemists to transform base metals into gold or to achieve immortality</note>. 

And there are scarcely two ways to achieve this. Concerning the more common way, a certain substance is extracted with great care from the aforementioned wine, though this requires great labor. I will pass over (as I have taught before) how the subtle substance itself—acting like a soul separated from the "gross" or physical body—is able to use its own subtlety to make another subtle thing even more refined and to penetrate it. By this process, its power and efficacy are multiplied in a wonderful way. Therefore, in such an Essence, the virtue can be extracted...

# ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE.

...of any substance, such as the Sun <note>original: *Solis*; the alchemical name for Gold</note>, the Moon <note>original: *Lunae*; the alchemical name for Silver</note>, and other metals, pearls, precious stones, herbs, and other such things; however, gold cannot be dissolved [in this manner]. There is, however, another way: by separating the air, fire, water, and earth from the **Quintessence** <term>The 'fifth essence' or spiritual core of a substance</term> itself, and afterward—by adding the earth (through **calcination** <term>reduction to ash or powder via heat</term>) to the other qualities and sublimating them into one—gold can be dissolved through frequent circular distillation and **digestion** <term>the process of slow heating to mature a substance</term>. 

You will understand the method of creating such an Essence in two ways. First: how and by what means the four elements are to be separated, and how they can be reduced again into a single body. In this way, according to some, the **Philosophical Stone** 
<margin>The Philosophical Stone, through its hidden power, dissolves all mineral metals.</margin>
appears to be composed; and through its latent and hidden power, all mineral metals can be dissolved for the purpose of reaching, coloring, and **coagulating** <term>solidifying or fixing</term> the Sun, the Moon, and others. Second: how the earth is to be separated once the Quintessence has been extracted (by which the virtue of the earth is drawn out), and how gold, silver, and other metals can also be resolved through it and reduced into a formal and substantial drink—a restorative and preservative of human life <note>This refers to 'Aurum Potabile' or Potable Gold, a legendary medicinal elixir</note>.

### ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE.
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

...is to be distilled, it should absorb as little of a "fiery nature" <term>an over-concentration of heat or harsh thermal qualities</term> as possible. Such a quality, once acquired through many and various distillations, is extremely difficult to remove and consequently is more harmful than beneficial to human nature. As you can clearly see, the pipe of the distilling apparatus, in which the wine [vapor] is contained, passes and travels through the lukewarm water itself. 

However, the other Bath of Mary <note>original: *balneum Mariae*; a water bath or 'bain-marie' used for gentle, even heating</note>, which was mentioned above, has the lower part of the furnace shaped like a cauldron. In this, all the distilling vessels and instruments should be placed with water. The upper part, which is the alembic <term>the cap or head of the still</term>, should not sit in any water; only the cucurbit <term>the rounded bottom flask</term> itself, which contains the wine to be distilled, is submerged. This is the [true] Bath of Mary. 

And whenever something is to be distilled in the first type of water-bath cauldron, care must be taken to use large and small leaden discs or rings. The glass vessels must be tied to these so that they are not tossed about by the movement of the heated water and broken. Its form is as follows:
