<meta>This page continues the technical setup for distilling wine into "water of life," specifically describing the construction of the furnace and the maintenance of the water bath. It follows the previous discussion on the "Bath of Mary" and the importance of temperature control.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTADIUS

And note that in such a bath, the cauldron must be built into the masonry, and on the side of the furnace it should have a long pipe made of copper or iron. Into this pipe, the coals are to be thrown, which must be glowing beneath the cauldron, or positioned so that the heat of the coals themselves reverberates against the cauldron. The coals cannot be entirely consumed within this pipe because of the many ventilation holes in the furnace and because the pipe is enclosed. 

Likewise, the alembic <term>the cap or head of the distilling apparatus</term> that stands in the water must be made of copper, and its interior should be lined with tin. The upper and lower vessels should be cemented together, and on the outer part, where the joints meet, they should be sealed very well with tin so that no water can escape from them. When the water has become heated, the lower tap or drain should be opened and the hot water let out, replacing it with fresh cold water. By doing this, the *aqua vitæ* <term>literally "water of life"; a high-proof distilled spirit</term> is improved during distillation, as it draws less of a hot and fiery nature into itself <note>The author emphasizes cooling to prevent the spirit from becoming too harsh or "burnt" in character.</note>. 

The instrument or vessel in which the water is contained should be made of copper or brass, and it should be about one palm wide <note>roughly 3 to 4 inches</note>, or a little more. In the middle, it should have a hole, and the outer rim should be two fingers thick. This is placed over the cauldron that is in the furnace. The opening of the cauldron should be no larger than the opening in the distilling vessel itself. All parts must be well-sealed with the "lute of wisdom" <term>original: *luto sapientiæ*; a specialized airtight sealing clay used by alchemists</term>, or with clay mixed with the wool scraps discarded by cloth shearers <note>The wool acted as a binding agent, similar to rebar in concrete, to prevent the clay seal from cracking under heat.</note>. 

When you have placed your wine in the cauldron in this manner, let all the sealed and cemented parts remain in place until you have distilled as much wine as is sufficient or desired. If you were to remove any part sooner, you would face the great trouble of having to reseal everything, if perhaps you had not [distilled]...

# THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTADIUS. 22

It is done in such a way that when it is closed, the heat itself, which rises to it, can return again all the way to the foot of the furnace, and let it be closed very well everywhere, so that it cannot easily lose the heat it has gathered. Therefore, let this channel <note>original: *cannale*; referring to the flue or chimney of the athanor furnace which holds the charcoal fuel</note> be above the furnace, and below the channel let there be a grate [spaced] by one palm, so that the ashes can fall down into the bottom. 

<margin>The furnace should be square.</margin>Indeed, the furnace must be square, and in each of its four corners it should have a hole or vent through which the smoke can exit. And the channel itself should have a window distant from the furnace by one palm, through which window the coals must be thrown in; and let it always be closed very well with some instrument made for this purpose, which must be as thick as the channel itself. 

The fire, however, should always be moderated and regulated through those four angular holes with some blocking registers <term>original: *registris obstursoriis*; sliding plates or dampers used to control the airflow and thus the temperature</term> suitable and fit for this purpose; and thus the heat will pass through the channel all the way to the copper cover, and no one will know where that fire can be coming from. 

And let a wooden vessel be made the size and circumference of a wagon wheel, so that it entirely surrounds the copper cover itself. In this vessel, water must be placed, so that the entire cover sits in the vessel full of water up to the middle part of the vessel. The vessel, moreover, should be four or five cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, approximately 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note> high, and two cubits from the bottom of the vessel, let there be a wooden lid with many holes, such that any gourd <term>original: *cucurbita*; a glass or ceramic vessel shaped like a gourd, used as the base of a still</term> can enter into any hole. And upon each gourd let an alembic <term>the "head" or cap of the distillation apparatus where vapors condense</term> be placed, and beneath each alembic a receiver <note>A vessel to collect the distilled liquid.</note> [should sit] upon the lid of the larger vessel; and let everything be very well cemented and sealed with lute—that is, the lid upon the vessel itself, the gourds upon the holes of the lid, and the alembics upon the gourds...

[alem]bic, and when you see certain drops distilling within it, then it is a most certain sign that nothing more of the spirits <term>original: *spiritus*; the volatile alcohol vapors</term> remains. When you wish to know when all the spirits have departed from the wine, it will always be clear to you through the signs mentioned before; or you can also take some of the water of life from the alembic itself, tasting it to see if any wateriness remains, and this method is more certain than certain. 

Therefore, when you have distilled it according to our teaching in a Mary’s bath or in a furnace of sloth, take the "phlegm" <term>original: *flegma*; the watery, non-combustible residue left after distillation</term> itself which remained in the distilling vessels, and let it be distilled eight or nine times. Each time, join the remaining phlegm with the previous portion, and let this be repeated until the water of life itself is converted into a fiery substance. A test for this is: if a cloth is soaked in it and set on fire, the cloth is immediately burned away along with the water <note>Highly concentrated alcohol (ethanol) burns completely; if water is present, the cloth usually remains damp and unburnt.</note>. 

If you also drop in a single drop of olive oil, it will immediately sink to the bottom and never rise again, no matter how much the vessel is moved or shaken; and thus you have the "Fifth Essence" <term>original: *Quinta Essentia*; the pure, concentrated spirit or "quintessence" thought to be the medicinal core of a substance</term> of the wine itself, separated from the four elements.

<margin>On preparing potable gold and dissolving the Sun.</margin>
In this way, you shall distill sixty *canthari*, and from the first distillation you will barely have seven; in the second, barely five; in the third, barely two or three. Thus, the more you distill, the less you will have, and what is distilled at the very end will be the purest, most subtle, and best for preparing "potable gold" <term>original: *aurum potabile*; a legendary liquid medicine made from gold</term>, in which you can also dissolve the Sun <term>original: *Solem*; the alchemical name for gold</term>. When you have distilled it three times, it will be most subtle. Therefore, unless you take great care, all its virtue will easily exhale <note>meaning to evaporate or vanish into the air</note>. 

Therefore, take a strainer <term>original: *colatorium*; here used as a specialized funnel for transfer</term> when you wish to pour it from one vessel to another, and let it be placed in the vessel into which you are pouring the wine, and let it be sealed all around with such wax as will be taught below. Place another vessel on top so that its neck is plunged entirely into the strainer. In the same way, you shall seal <term>original: *lutabis*; to seal with "lute" or airtight paste</term> the neck of the strainer on all sides. Having done this, allow the wine to flow into the other vessel by its own weight, and its virtue will evaporate less. If, however, the strainer is the length of one palm...

to be separated. Take all the aforementioned waters, collected together, and place them in the cauldron of the first furnace, or in a large cucurbit <term>cucurbit: a gourd-shaped glass vessel used as the base of a still</term>. If you are unable to obtain a large cucurbit, place the liquid into several small ones and let it be distilled in one of the previously described furnaces. From this, the phlegm <term>phlegm: in alchemy, the watery, tasteless, and inactive liquid that is the first to be distilled off</term> should be extracted until nothing more comes out. Use a slow fire, just as if it were in a Bain-marie <term>original: *Balneo Mariæ*; a water bath used to provide gentle, even heat</term>.

<margin>Human blood is the air itself.</margin>
And when it has been distilled in this way until the end, remove the receiving vessel, and there you will find the phlegm. In that cucurbit or cauldron, a very black matter remains, like liquid pitch <note>original: *pix liquida*; a thick, dark resin or tar</note>. Some people, seeking a shortcut, place this into a vessel or an iron box over a slow fire of coals until that black substance appears like thin pitch, and then this is placed into the cucurbit. Afterward, place some of the other phlegm into the box, and proceed as you did with the first; then the black matter is placed with the rest. You shall do this as many times as necessary until no phlegm remains. Then, the black matter should be placed back into the cucurbit and set in sand <note>A sand bath allowed for higher temperatures than a water bath</note> to extract the superfluous moisture from it. Afterward, pour in the Quintessence <term>Quintessence: the "fifth essence," the purest and most potent medicinal core of a substance</term> which you previously drew away from it, to a depth of four or five fingers. Mix them well together and place them in a Bain-marie to digest <note>original: *digerendum*; a process of gentle, sustained heat used to soften or mature a substance</note>; afterward, let it be distilled through an alembic <term>alembic: the "head" or cap of the distilling apparatus that redirects vapor into a condenser</term>. When the water has been distilled in this way, pour it back over the dregs <note>original: *feces*; the solid remains at the bottom of the vessel</note>, and let it be distilled again, and again digested and distilled. The more often you do this, the better it will be. That which is distilled in the seventh distillation is called "human blood" <note>An alchemical metaphor for the refined "Air" element of the substance, not literal blood</note>, which Alchemists seek above all else, and it is the Air itself. Thus, you hold two Elements in their power.

After this, when it has been removed from the lime kiln, you will be better able to calcine it in the reverberatory furnace. And if you had done this before you placed it in the lime kiln, it could not have been calcined for a long time. If indeed it is not sufficiently burnt on the first try in the lime kiln, let it be put back into the same. ...consider, when you have taken some of this earth, and placed it in the open air, and caused it to be dried, and afterwards have poured three or four drops of the aforementioned water upon it, if it is not resolved <note>meaning: if the solid does not dissolve back into a liquid state</note>, then you have the most certain sign that it is sufficiently prepared. If, however, it does dissolve, it must again be **calcined**, and **reverberated**, and dissolved in a **bain-marie** <term>balneum Mariae: a water bath used for gentle heating</term> just as was said. And let this be done as many times as necessary, until the earth is no longer 
<margin>The philosopher's stone fixes all spirits.</margin>
dissolved, and then it will be truly prepared. It is called **Water of Life** <note>original: "Aqua vitæ"</note>, because by it and through it, **gold** is dissolved and refined. But in truth, this is that very **stone**—and not a salt, as some claim—which is called the **rectified philosopher's stone** <note>original: "lapis philosophicus"</note>, and the **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence: the Quintessence, the pure, incorruptible essence of a substance</term> itself, separated from its four elements. Within it lies hidden the spirit separated from spirit; that is, from its own phlegm (which is the water), and from its own oil (which is the air), and from the fiery water (which is properly the water itself), and from its own burnt dross, which is the earthy element. And that Essence, thus reverberated, calcined, and resolved, and again re-calcined in the form described above, is turned into a stone, with which all spirits are **fixed** <note>in alchemy, "fixing" means turning a volatile substance into a stable solid that can withstand heat</note>, because it possesses the highest power of spirituality and the lowest power of corporality. And for this reason, **Hermes** says: *That which is above is as that which is below, and that which is below is as that which is above* <note>A famous maxim from the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note>. And it is a unique essence for perfecting **Gold** <note>original: "Solem" (the Sun), the alchemical name for gold</note>, and for resolving any other metals, because it always maintains its own force and power. It contains the entire substance of the four qualities or elements within a single complex essence, in which the **secrets of the secrets of nature** are contained. And therefore the Philosopher says: The stone comes from the earth just as from four corruptible qualities, toward heaven,

<margin>The elixir stone is like crystal.</margin>
...that which is incorruptible ascends, and likewise descends again from heaven to earth. This he signified by the words mentioned before, when he said: "The highest is that which is lower, and the lower is that which is highest" <note>original: "Supremum est, quod est inferius, & inferius est, quod est supremum." This is a famous passage from the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus, expressing the correspondence between the celestial and earthly realms</note>, so that it possesses the power of the **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence: the Quintessence, the pure, incorruptible core of a substance</term>, to **fix** <note>meaning: to make a volatile substance solid and resistant to fire</note> those very metals which arise from the earth. For it is the elixir stone, and it is like a crystal, which holds most perfectly to **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio"</note>, the mother of all metals. And when this Fifth Essence is mixed with the aforementioned stone, and gold leaves are added, they are dissolved through the powerful penetration found within the stone; and the hidden, overflowing subtlety that lies within the Fifth Essence is converted into **drinkable gold** <note>original: "aurum potabile." Alchemists believed that because gold was the most perfect and incorruptible metal, making it into a liquid medicine would grant health and longevity</note>.

<margin>The utility of the Water of Life.</margin>
We promised above that we would demonstrate a twofold path for how the four elements are to be separated and reduced into a single form, in order to dissolve the **Sun** <note>the alchemical name for gold</note>, the **Moon** <note>the alchemical name for silver</note>, and all other metals. Now, however, we must proceed to the second path: namely, how the earth is to be separated once the Fifth Essence itself has been extracted, through which all metals can be dissolved and reduced into the substance of drinkable water. For all philosophers say with one voice that the **Water of Life** <note>original: "aqua vitæ," usually referring to highly concentrated alcohol or a potent chemical solvent</note> can be made to sustain human nature and to ward off every illness. Therefore, make the Water of Life clear and **rectified** <term>rectified: purified and strengthened through the process of repeated distillation</term>, so that it burns when set on fire, just as we have said above:

we said: And by no means make human blood,
<margin>Human blood, that is, the air itself.</margin>
<note>In alchemy, "human blood" was often used as a code name for the element of air or a specific red-colored stage in the distillation process.</note>
because if any herbs or other material substances are added into it in excess, they will lose their powers and lose every good taste and smell; it would even be horrific for human use. Nor should "fiery water" <note>original: "aqua ignea"</note> be made, because it would contain such heat and sharpness in itself that all materials (except for metals) would draw such excessive and fiery heat to themselves. Thus, when you thought you were making the **Water of Life** <note>original: "aquam vitæ"</note>, you would instead be making the **water of death**. Since, however, all virtue and perfection is in the earth itself, reduced into a stone, as we have explained copiously above, we therefore assign this "earth" to our present water, making neither human blood nor fiery water; and then it is called the true **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence: the Quintessence, the pure, incorruptible core of a substance</term>, or the Water of Life, and not the water of death. And the more often it is distilled through the earth, the more effective and better it becomes. And it should be noted that a small amount of this earth holds the highest power in that work. For just as a small amount of leaven <note>original: "fermenti"; used here like yeast or a sourdough starter</note> makes a large and plentiful dough for baking bread, so even the smallest part of this earth raises up the greatest perfections and virtues suited for the human body. And therefore, one must beware of those who make "earth" from tartar <note>tartar: the potassium bitartrate crust found in wine barrels</note>, or those who burn grape skins into ash, or those who take powdered lime, or the ash of vines and willows, and claim this to be the "earth" of which we speak. ## Chapter Twelve.

**N**ow we must proceed to the Fifth Essence <term>Fifth Essence: also known as the "Quintessence," the purest and most potent medicinal core of a substance</term> of honey itself, and how it ought to be extracted. When, therefore, you wish to extract it, you should have a large quantity of good and excellent honey, from which the honeycomb has not yet been separated, just as it is when it is first taken from the hive. Squeeze the honey from the combs without using fire, by making three or four bags out of straining cloth, two cubits <note>a cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</note> in length. The width should be as wide as the cloth extends. Once these are filled, put the honey with the comb into the aforementioned bags, and during summer days, let them be placed and hung in the sun. Under each bag, you should place a clean vessel to receive the honey dripping from the bags. 

And if it should happen to be during winter days, let a steam bath <note>original: "balneale vaporarium," a heated room or stove-room common in Central Europe</note> be heated, such as the wealthier people have in Germany, and let the bag be hung against the wall so that the honey may melt from the heat of the bath. When all the honey has been liquefied, let more be put into the bag, and let it be done as with the first. You shall do this as many times as necessary until you have a sufficient quantity. From the dregs <note>the remains of the honeycomb</note>, let wax be made, as is the custom. 

Afterward, obtain six or eight earthen vessels made of the best clay you can find, which are shaped like a gourd <term>cucurbita: a round, long-necked vessel used for heating substances</term>. Each of these vessels should be large enough to hold fifteen or sixteen pounds. They should be narrow at the neck but wide in the belly, so that you can place an alembic <term>alembic: the "cap" of the still that catches and condenses vapors</term> on top of them. About a palm's breadth from the opening, the vessel should have two handles the length of a little finger, so that it can be placed into and removed from the furnace by them. From the bottom, let them be very well sealed with lute <term>lute: a specialized clay or paste used to seal alchemical vessels against high heat</term>, of which...

The shape is this.

And when all these things have been prepared, let the master of the work fill each of the said vessels up to a third part with the honey, extracted as we have already described <note>that is, extracted by the sun or a steam bath without direct fire, as mentioned on the previous page</note>, and let the remaining two parts of the vessel be empty, so that the honey can rise and fall; and let it be placed in a furnace of laziness <term>furnace of laziness: also known as an "athanor," a slow-burning furnace designed to maintain a steady heat for a long time without constant tending</term>. And let an alembic <term>alembic: a cap or lid used in distillation, shaped like a head with a long beak to channel vapors</term> be placed on top, and all the little cracks all around should be very well sealed with a linen cloth, smeared with thin clay, and perform your distillation with a slow fire. 

For if you heat the furnace too much, the honey itself would rise up into the alembic. And therefore, with constant care, always observe if the vessels are too hot; if so, let linen cloths be dampened and wrapped around the vessels near the alembic, but do not let them touch the alembic itself, nor let any drop of water fall upon it, because the alembic would shatter very quickly <note>the sudden temperature change on the glass or ceramic would cause it to crack</note>. And let the linen cloths be wrapped around the vessel itself until it cools down, and thus the honey itself will not be able to rise or distil through the alembic. 

And the first water which is distilled from this honey contains within itself the spirits which enter into potable gold <term>potable gold: a legendary medicinal liquid made from gold, believed to restore youth and health</term>, and when they sense even a little heat, they ascend and do not remain. And therefore, honey that has been extracted from its comb by means of fire is of no use at all for this present work of ours, because all the spirits have been taken from it; they have expired and departed. And you shall consider this carefully.

# ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE.

And when you see that no more spirit <note>original: "spiritus", referring to the volatile essence or vapor being extracted</note> appears in the alembic, let the vessels be removed. You can tell this is happening when small droplets appear around the rim of the alembic; then, remove the vessels from the furnace and set the honey aside in its own container, for it is no longer useful for this particular work. 

And immediately pour boiling water into your cucurbit <term>cucurbit: the lower, gourd-shaped vessel of a distilling apparatus that holds the substance to be heated</term>, and wash it thoroughly. You should use a wide iron spatula, about two fingers' breadth across, to clean the cucurbit completely, so that if any burnt honey has settled inside, it can be scraped away and removed. Do this every time you wish to put fresh honey into the vessel. 

If you wish to distill your honey safely, without the worry that it might boil over and rise up into the alembic, take a handful of ivy <note>original: "hederæ"</note> leaves and one pound of honey. Mix them together and distill them; in this way, your honey will never rise too high. Distillation can also be performed in this manner using a bell-shaped vessel <note>original: "per campanam", referring to a specific bell-like glass cover used in certain distillation setups</note>.

## How the Fifth Essence is to be extracted from Celandine.

### Chapter Thirteen.

<margin>Lullius, Book 1, Chapter 4.</margin>
According to Raymund Lull and John of Rupescissa <note>Johannes de Rupescissa, a 14th-century alchemist influential in the study of distillation</note>, Celandine is called *Chelidonia* as if it were a "gift from heaven" <note>original: "coeli donum"</note>. However, if we wish to examine the etymology of the word and its Greek derivation, we must speak differently of its origin. For Pliny and Aristotle say that Celandine is named after the swallow <note>original: "hirundine"; the Greek word "chelidon" means swallow</note>, or from...

<meta>catchword: swallows (hirundine)</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the instructions for extracting the Fifth Essence of honey using pebbles to regulate distillation and begins a new chapter on Celandine, debating its etymological origins as either a 'heavenly gift' or 'swallow-herb.'</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the distillation of honey to the medicinal and alchemical properties of the herb Celandine (Chelidonia), continuing the author's survey of substances capable of yielding a "Fifth Essence."</meta>

# ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE.

...of the sight of the swallow. For in Greek, the swallow is called *Chelidon*. From this comes the proverb: **one swallow does not make a spring** <note>original Greek: "μία χελιδών ἔαρ οὐ ποιεῖ"; Latin: "Vna Hirundo non facit ver"</note>. From this bird, the plant took the name **Celandine** <note>Latin: "Chelidonia"</note>, having been revealed to mankind by the swallow. For with this plant, swallows restore the sight of their chicks in the nest <note>A common medieval belief held that parent swallows used the caustic sap of the celandine plant to heal the eyes of their young, leading to its use in human ophthalmology</note>.

There are two kinds of it. The **Greater** has a shrub-like stem, a leaf like a parsnip, a thicker bark, stands about two cubits high <note>a cubit is roughly the length of a forearm, or 18 inches</note>, is whitish in color, and has a yellow flower. The **Lesser** has leaves somewhat smaller and rounder than ivy, and is less white; its juice is sharp like saffron, and its seed is like a poppy’s. They bloom at the arrival of the swallows and wither at their departure. While they are blooming, the juice is pressed out and boiled in a bronze vessel with honey over gently glowing ashes. This is used as a singular remedy against the dimming of the eyes. The juice is also used by itself in eye-washes <note>original: "colliriis"</note>, which are called "Chelidonia" after the plant.

<margin>The virtues of celandine.</margin>

It also possesses countless other virtues, and therefore, not without reason, did we wish to include in this work of ours the method by which its **Fifth Essence** <note>the most refined and potent spiritual core of the plant, isolated through distillation</note> is to be extracted. This essence even enters into the composition of **potable gold** <note>original: "aurum potabile," a legendary alchemical medicine consisting of gold dissolved in a liquid state, believed to grant longevity and perfect health</note>.

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific laboratory instructions for the preparation of the herb Celandine. Having shredded the plant on the previous page, the author now describes the processes of fermentation, primary distillation, and the transition to "circulation" using a blind alembic.</meta>

### ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE.

The material is processed finely and ground in a stone mortar. Afterwards, let it be placed into a glazed earthenware <term>cucurbit<gloss>a vessel shaped like a gourd, used as the base of a still</gloss></term>. The vessel is to be filled completely, closing it and sealing it well with <term>lute<gloss>a paste or clay used to make laboratory seals airtight</gloss></term>. Then, let it be placed in fresh horse dung <note>original Latin: "fimum equinum"; horse manure was commonly used by early chemists as a source of gentle, steady heat for weeks at a time</note> to ferment and digest for a period of three weeks. 

From there, place an <term>alembic<gloss>the "head" or cap of the still that directs vapors into a condenser</gloss></term> on top, and let it be distilled in a <term>Mary’s bath<gloss>a bain-marie or water bath, used for gentle heating</gloss></term>. The fire must be very slow, and all the joints should be perfectly stopped up. From this process, the <term>phlegm<gloss>in early chemistry, the watery, tasteless distillate that emerges first</gloss></term> will come forth, while the other three elements will remain at the bottom of the still. Afterwards, the dregs <note>original Latin: "feces"</note> are to be extracted and ground upon a marble slab as finely and delicately as you are able. Then, place them back into the vessel and pour the phlegm—which was previously separated from the other three elements—back over them. Finally, place a <term>blind alembic<gloss>an alembic head without a delivery spout, used to force vapors to condense and fall back into the base, a process called "circulation"</gloss></term> on top.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a large bulbous distillation vessel, likely an alembicus caecus (blind alembic), which has no delivery spout. The vessel rests on a narrow base or neck. The background depicts a courtyard-like setting with two tall rectangular windows and decorative balustrade posts connected by draped cords or chains.</image-desc>

Everything is to be sealed very well and placed in a <term>Mary's bath</term> for seven or eight days, in order to rot <note>original Latin: "putrefiendum"; in alchemy, "putrefaction" or rotting was a necessary stage of breaking down a substance to release its inner essence</note> and

to be digested. If, however, you fear the expense <note>original: "expensas"; likely referring to the cost of maintaining a furnace for three weeks</note>, let it be fermented in horse dung. Afterward, let it be placed in another furnace to be distilled through ashes <note>original: "per cineres"; a method of heating where the vessel sits in hot ashes to provide a more even, higher temperature than a water bath</note>, and let a beaked <term>alembic<gloss>the cap of a still with a long tube or "beak" to lead vapors away</gloss></term> be fitted to it. Then a clear water will come forth, having the color of oil, which contains two elements within itself—namely, air and water—and in the bottom <note>original: "infundo," a contraction of "in fundo"</note> the other two elements will remain, which are fire and earth.

When you wish to separate the **phlegm** <note>original: "phlegma"; the watery, inactive part of a distillate</note> itself from the air, place the oil in a fresh distilling vessel, and let it be distilled in **Mary’s bath** <note>original: "balneo Mariae"; a water bath or bain-marie</note> with a gentle fire. You will see the phlegm itself ascend, and the oil will remain in the distilling vessel. In this way, you will have two elements—namely, air and water—each one separated from the other. For the oil cannot ascend or be sublimated in **Mary’s bath**, because the fire is not powerful enough. 

When you truly wish to separate the other two elements, take the dregs <note>original: "feces"</note> from which you distilled the oil, and let them be ground upon a marble slab as before. Afterward, take four parts of the phlegm and one part of the dregs; let them be mixed and incorporated together. Then, let it be placed in the furnace of the [water] bath for seven days. Afterward, let it be distilled through sand <note>original: "per arenam"; a sand bath, used for higher temperatures than a water bath</note> with a strong fire, until a reddish water comes out; let it be continually distilled until it is entirely converted into 그 water.

### ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE.

Now that the rectifications of each of the elements have been demonstrated, it is worthwhile to know the nature and virtue of each of these elements.

The power of the **Watery quality**, then, is to expel all heats and poisons from the chest itself, and also to purify the blood. It is effective against obstructions <term>obstructions<gloss>referred to as "opilationes," these were blockages in the internal organs thought to cause disease in Galenic medicine</gloss></term> of the lungs and liver. It destroys every superfluous humor and phlegm. Finally, it frees a human being from every infirmity in nine days.

The **Oil of Air**, however, preserves youth and strength along with beauty and the finest grace. It does not permit the blood to rot, nor melancholy to dominate, nor choler to burn <note>original: "coleram adurere," referring to 'adust' or scorched humors which were thought to become toxic</note>, nor does it allow phlegm to abound in the human body; it increases the blood and distributes it through every limb. 
<margin>The natures and virtues of the oils from the four elements.</margin>
And for this reason, those who use this Oil of Air often undergo bloodletting <term>bloodletting<gloss>phlebotomy; the practice of withdrawing blood to maintain the balance of the four humors</gloss></term>, because it is highly penetrative. It sustains and nourishes the sick to the greatest degree. It is also valuable if anyone should fear being in danger of losing an eye: if they place one or two drops into it every day for the space of thirty days, it helps miraculously.

The efficacy of the **Fiery quality** is much greater than the other two. For it works in those cases where

Indeed, the earthy matter, rectified through dissolutions, coagulations, and calcinations <term>calcinations<gloss>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder or ash</gloss></term>, is the subtle salt of the earth <note>original: "sal terræ"</note>, with which all metals can be transmuted into stone, and with which all spirits—those possessing radical moisture—are fixed <term>fixed<gloss>in alchemy, "fixing" a spirit means turning a volatile or liquid substance into a solid form that can withstand fire</gloss></term>. It even nourishes lepers. From this, the ancient Philosophers made the stone, which they called the Philosophers' Stone <note>original: "Lapis Philosophalis"</note>. 

<margin>
A stone for fixing all spirits.
</margin>

The method of taking this medicine is as follows: Take three drops of the fire of celandine <note>the "fiery" element or essential oil of the Celandine plant</note>, three spoonfuls of rose water, and a small spoonful of the blood-red air <note>referring to the "Oil of Air" described previously</note>; let them be taken mixed together on an empty stomach. If the illness is "hot" <note>referring to the Galenic classification of diseases by temperature and moisture</note>, let it be given with wine; and if the person has exceeded their twenty-fourth year, let it be given with aqua vitae <term>aqua vitae<gloss>literally "water of life," referring to concentrated alcohol or distilled spirits</gloss></term>. However, if the fevers are hot, it should by no means be administered to the patient.

### On the method of extracting the Fifth Essence from human blood, eggs, meats, and similar things.

### Chapter Fourteen.

If you wish, therefore, to extract the Fifth Essence <note>original: "Quintam essentiā," the pure, celestial medicinal core of a substance</note> from human blood, let the blood of a person of a sanguine complexion <term>sanguine complexion<gloss>one of the four temperaments, characterized by an abundance of blood, a ruddy face, and a cheerful disposition</gloss></term>, or a healthy choleric person of middle age and a drinker of good wine, be taken after they have undergone bloodletting <term>bloodletting<gloss>referred to as "phlebothomia," the medical practice of withdrawing blood to balance the body's humors</gloss></term>. When the blood residing at the bottom of the vessel has been separated from the water <note>the serum or plasma</note> itself, let it be ground in some vessel with a tenth part of common salt, and let it be mixed well by shaking it together, and place it in a closed pelican <term>pelican<gloss>a specialized alchemical vessel with two curved "arms" that allow vapor to circulate and condense back into the body, used for long-term digestion</gloss></term>. And afterwards, let it be placed in a pit five feet deep and two feet wide, having one layer of quicklime <term>quicklime<gloss>calcium oxide, which generates heat when in contact with moisture</gloss></term> and another of horse manure, just as...

### *On the method of extracting the Fifth Essence from*
### *apples, pears, and other fruits.*
### *Chapter Fifteen.*

If you wish to extract the Fifth Essence from apples, pears, plums, cherries, chestnuts, and other kinds of fruit, let them be chopped into very small pieces with a knife, and afterward let them be ground in an earthenware mortar, and very well blended with a tenth part of common salt. Afterward, let them be placed in a cucurbit, and a blind alembic be placed on top; let it be very well sealed with lute <term>lute<gloss>a clay or paste used to seal the joints of chemical apparatus to make them airtight</gloss></term> and placed in manure <note>original: "fimum"; horse manure was used as a natural, steady source of gentle heat</note> to putrefy, just as was already said concerning human blood. And then it must be observed whether any of the fruits possesses the virtue for that purpose for which you wish to use it, because that virtue and excellence of the fruit itself comes from its Essence, which is latent and hidden within the matter itself. And when that essence is separated from its dregs and from the coarser matter, and is led back to immateriality and a certain state of incorruption...

## On the method of extracting the Fifth Essence from flowers, herbs, and roots.

### Chapter Sixteen.

If you wish to separate the **Fifth Essence** from the four qualities of flowers, herbs, and roots, take them while they are well-matured, in their whole substance, during clear and serene weather, while the moon is waxing and already nearly full. At that time, all flowers, herbs, and roots are in a sense free

...manure for one month, renewing the manure once every week. Let it be distilled in the Mary's bath, decreasing the heat by a medium degree. After this, let the dregs be pulverized again, and with the water that was distilled from them poured back over, let it be digested as above, and distilled a third time, and allowed to putrefy <note>putrefiat; in this context, "putrefaction" refers to a controlled biological breakdown or fermentation intended to release the "spirit" of the substance</note>, always decreasing the heat by a medium degree. ...or it may even be hidden in grape marc <term>grape marc<gloss>vinacea; the skins, seeds, and stems left after pressing grapes</gloss></term>, and circulated there for one full month, or a little more, because the more it is circulated, the nobler it becomes, while the superfluous humidity of the four qualities <note>The Aristotelian qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry</note> is thoroughly digested, consumed, and resolved in the circulation vessel <term>circulation vessel<gloss>circulatorio; a vessel such as a 'pelican' designed for continuous refluxing where vapor rises and falls back into the liquid</gloss></term> through frequent rising and falling. And thus you have the Fifth Essence <term>Fifth Essence<gloss>Quinta Essentia; the celestial or purest part of a substance, distinct from the four earthly elements</gloss></term>, in which the greatest virtue of herbs, flowers, and roots lies hidden. It is even more effective, however, if you take the herb, seed, or root, and distill the water from them, and that water so distilled is poured back again over its own dregs <note>original: "feces"; the solid residue remaining after a liquid is poured off or distilled</note> from which it was distilled, digesting it in horse manure for seven days. Afterward, let it be distilled through ashes <term>ash bath<gloss>per cineres; a method of heating a vessel by nesting it in hot ashes, providing a more intense and even heat than water</gloss></term> in every way, manner, and form, just as was done above with Celandine <note>original: "Chelidonia"; a plant in the poppy family highly prized by alchemists for its golden sap</note>. Because through this you will separate the four qualities from them, and the material is rendered so much more noble and excellent. And let each part be reserved separately, for otherwise the dregs would be of no value. Now someone might ask: what is the Fifth Essence, since it seems to be nothing more than any other distilled water? To this I reply that in form and appearance it is nothing other than another distilled water. But it is much greater, subtler, and nobler than other simple distilled water, since through many and various digestions, circulations, and distillations, all phlegmatic <note>In this context, "phlegmatic" refers to the dull, watery, and inactive parts of the substance</note> and watery humidity in it has been consumed. In like manner, through its excellent subtlety, while it is poured over the dregs, and digested again, and distilled again from those very dregs, it has recovered all its power and virtue which lay hidden within it. And therefore it is compared to burnt wood, which has now been reduced to coals, and yet the coals themselves are no longer flam-

body. <note>The text begins with "ma," the completion of "anima" or "soul" from the previous page.</note> It is also compared to a corpse. For while the soul itself is separated from the body, the corpse is good for nothing except for rotting. And for this reason, I wanted to reveal everything perfectly <note>original: "ad vnguem"; literally "to the fingernail," a Latin idiom meaning with high precision</note>, so that if you wish to have something subtle and of great efficacy, you might lead the work to a result according to your heart's desire by taking some of this **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence<gloss>Quinta Essentia; the purest, celestial part of a substance, believed to be the "soul" of the matter</gloss></term> in place of other waters. Then, add whatever botanical species <note>original: "species"; here referring to herbs, minerals, or other raw medicinal ingredients</note> you wish, and let it be distilled in the same way as has been heard many times now—namely, by taking six ounces of the Fifth Essence and four drams of whatever other species you choose. These should be ground into a very fine powder and placed in a **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit<gloss>cucurbitam; a gourd-shaped chemical vessel used for distillation</gloss></term>, placing a **blind alembic** <term>blind alembic<gloss>alembicum cæcum; a still-head without a spout, used for digestion or refluxing rather than collecting a separate distillate</gloss></term> on top. Let it be digested in **horse manure** <term>horse manure<gloss>fimo equino; a common alchemical method for providing a gentle, constant heat via the natural fermentation of the dung</gloss></term> for a whole month.

Afterward, let it be distilled by making a fire up to the end of the **third degree** <note>Alchemists typically recognized four degrees of heat: 1st (lukewarm), 2nd (boiling water), 3rd (sand/ash bath), and 4th (red hot/direct flame)</note>. Afterward, let the dregs <note>original: "feces"; the solid residue remaining in the vessel</note> be powdered, and with the water distilled from them poured back over, let it be placed again in a blind alembic, digesting for three weeks. Then let it be distilled again and digested for fourteen days, always decreasing the fire itself by one degree. 

And when you have distilled and **putrefied** <note>original: "putrefeceris"; in alchemy, the process of breaking down and rotting matter to release its internal virtues</note> it four times, let the dregs be powdered again; finally, let a fire be made in the first degree of heat, and let the digestion take place for eight days. Afterward, let it be placed in a **circulation vessel** <term>circulation vessel<gloss>circulatorium; a vessel designed for continuous reflux, such as a 'pelican,' where the substance evaporates and falls back into itself</gloss></term>, and let it be circulated there as much as you wish; then you will find a miraculous operation in each thing for which the material itself was intended. 

If, however, you think that an incorruptible thing can be made from a corruptible one, you are deceived, as <term>John of Rupescissa<gloss>a 14th-century Franciscan alchemist famous for his work on the 'quintessence' of wine</gloss></term> says: "For although it may make a corruptible thing close to being incorruptible, it is nevertheless not led to true perfection," as <term>Lullius<gloss>Ramon Llull, a major figure in medieval logic and alchemy to whom many 16th-century texts were attributed</gloss></term> says. Because whatever is beneath the heavens is corruptible. And therefore

...therefore, it is highly absurd to prepare or make various things so that any matter might be entirely preserved from corruption. As we see in the sick, who are sometimes relieved by doctors by a stroke of luck, even though no proper care preceded it. But yet (as the poet Ovid says), *It is not always in the power of the physician that the patient be relieved.* <note>original: "Non est in medico semper releuetur vt æger"; from Ovid's *Ex Ponto*.</note> For if this were the case, people who died a hundred or two hundred years ago would still be alive today. Therefore, if you were to use true **potable gold** <term>potable gold<gloss>auro potabili; a legendary alchemical life-extender consisting of gold in a liquid, drinkable form</gloss></term>, just as was in use five hundred years ago, and thought you would live three hundred or five hundred years (as is read regarding many of the ancients), this is a frivolous and most ridiculous notion in this age of ours. This is because the nature of men and their **vegetative power** <note>original: "virtus vegetatiua"; the fundamental biological force of growth and nutrition</note> decrease from day to day, and because there is no good regimen of eating and drinking among men. For in our time (which even Saint Paul <note>original: "diuus Paulus"</note> foretold would be common), there is no one who doubts that more die from gluttony <note>original: "crapula"; refers to excessive drinking or overindulgence</note> than by the sword. Finally, we do not observe any worship of God, fear, or ceremonies in the right path, as our ancestors did. And therefore "the just man is taken away, lest wickedness should alter his understanding," <note>A reference to the Wisdom of Solomon 4:11.</note> and "men of blood and deceit shall not live out half their days." <note>A reference to Psalm 55:23.</note> And so, whatever we strive for is in vain.

## How the Fifth Essence is to be extracted from Antimony. Chapter Seventeen.

The **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence<gloss>Quinta Essentia; the purest, celestial part of a substance, believed to be its medicinal "soul"</gloss></term> extracted from **antimony** <term>antimony<gloss>antimonio; a brittle, silvery-white mineral used in early medicine for its purging properties and in alchemy for purification</gloss></term> is very effective for drying out wounds. **Lead of the Philosophers**. It is also named by certain Artists **virgin’s milk**. <margin>From lead: The Essence.</margin>

It is effective against all burns from fire and hot water, and against **shingles** <note>original: "impetiginem"; refers to skin eruptions, rashes, or inflammatory conditions</note> and **choleric blisters**. ...repeatedly washed with rose water, straining it through a linen cloth until no trace of the **white lead** <term>white lead<gloss>cerusa; a white pigment and medicinal powder made from lead</gloss></term> powder remains in it. Then let it be dried and reserved for use. This is the method favored by **The Servant** <note>original: "Seruitor"; a reference to the *Liber Servitoris* by the 10th-century physician Al-Zahrawi (Albucasis)</note> and **Johannes de Sancto Amando** <note>a 13th-century theologian and philosopher known for his medical commentaries</note>, in his commentary on the dispensatory of **Johannes Mesue** <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh, a celebrated Persian physician; his "antidotarium" was a standard pharmaceutical text</note>.

### ¶ On the method of extracting the Fifth Essence from Gold. Chapter Eighteen.

Many have handed down various ways regarding the **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence<gloss>Quinta Essentia; the purest, most potent medicinal extract of a substance</gloss></term> of gold; however, for the sake of brevity, we shall demonstrate a more subtle and shorter method. When, therefore, you wish to extract the Fifth Essence from **Gold** <term>Gold<gloss>referred to here as "Sol" (the Sun), its planetary name in alchemy</gloss></term> itself, as the most precious and perfect of all metals: Take 4 ounces of the best Gold and 24 ounces of living and well-washed **Mercury** <term>Mercury<gloss>quicksilver</gloss></term>; let an **amalgam** <term>amalgam<gloss>a mixture or alloy of mercury with another metal, in this case, gold</gloss></term> be made. 

And you should have three or four, or more, earthen instruments made by a potter from the best clay you can find. Let them be made in the shape of a **box** <term>box<gloss>pixis; a cylindrical container</gloss></term>, of a uniform width and breadth from one base to the other. Let them be round at the bottom and one **palm’s length** <note>approximately 3-4 inches</note> wide, so that a goldsmith’s **crucible** <term>crucible<gloss>tigillum; a ceramic or graphite container used for melting metals at high temperatures</gloss></term> can fit inside. Let them be glazed on the inside and around the rim or opening. Their form is this:

<margin>The Raven's Head.</margin>
[What] we have already mentioned is called the **Raven’s Head** <term>Raven’s Head<gloss>original Latin: "Caput corvi"; the alchemical term for the "nigredo" or blackening stage, signifying the breakdown and purification of a substance</gloss></term> by the Philosophers, and it is the beginning of all true and perfect science. And although it may be black and dark, it is nevertheless **Gold** <term>Gold<gloss>original: "Sol" (the Sun); the alchemical symbol for gold</gloss></term>, which you will be able to prove by smelting. And when you see that the **Mercury** <term>Mercury<gloss>quicksilver</gloss></term> itself yields no more of such black matter, then it is a most certain sign that no more Gold is contained within it. Therefore, when you have collected it in this way, it must be washed very well, so that all the Mercury is withdrawn and separated from it. This washing, however, is done in this manner:

Distill spring water once through an **alembic** <term>alembic<gloss>a hooded apparatus used for distillation</gloss></term>; place the black matter in a glass jar, pouring this distilled water over it, and let it be rubbed here and there with a clean finger, and you will see the Mercury, separated from the matter itself, settling at the bottom. Afterwards, strain the water and the matter away from the Mercury itself into another glass jar, pouring more distilled water over it and doing as before. And do this as many times as necessary until all the matter is segregated from the Mercury itself.

Afterwards, take the jar with that black matter and place it in a safe location, covering it very well so that no impurity can fall in, and let it rest thus for a day and a night, and all the matter will settle at the bottom. From there, let the water be strained from the matter itself with a clean, white linen cloth, and let that matter be dried over a small fire made of coals, or let it be placed in hot sand or in ashes. Afterwards, let it be placed in a **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit<gloss>a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation</gloss></term>, sealing it very well with **lute** <term>lute<gloss>original: "lutando"; a clay or paste used to hermetically seal laboratory vessels</gloss></term>, and let it be reserved for use until you wish to employ it. ->*On the method of distilling by descent.*<-

### Chapter Nineteen.

I believe it escapes no one's notice that there are two ways of distilling: namely, by ascent <note>distillatio per ascensum; the standard method where vapors rise and then condense</note> and by descent. The former is easily known by everyone, but the latter by only a few. For this reason, I thought I would be doing something worthwhile if I introduced the way of distillation by descent to the apprentices of this art. For many things are distilled by this method, such as is done in oil of juniper, in blessed oil <term>blessed oil<gloss>original: "oleo benedicto"; in this context, usually refers to "oil of bricks," a substance produced by distilling bricks soaked in oil, used for medicinal purposes</gloss></term>, in oil of nutmeg, in oil of egg yolks, and in many other substances. Such a distillation can be performed in a square furnace made of unbaked bricks. It should be made with a width and breadth of three cubits <term>cubits<gloss>an ancient measurement based on the length of the forearm, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</gloss></term>, or a little more, by placing one stone after another, as is done in **Germany** in the construction of hearths upon which food is usually cooked. It is made of earth, at a height of one and a half cubits on all sides, except for the front, upon which iron fittings are placed; upon these are other bricks—thin ones which we call tiles...

<meta>catchword: ...we call [tiles].</meta>

...[little by little] water will drip <note>original: "stillabit aqua"</note>, yet one [material will drip] more than another, as a diligent follower of this art can see from the distillation of various materials. Therefore, let a glass **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit<gloss>original: "cucurbita"; a gourd-shaped laboratory vessel used for distillation</gloss></term> be placed below the orifice itself, through the upper part of the foundation into the lower part of the furnace, to receive what you have distilled. 

And when you see the oil distilling, the vessel itself ought to be emptied and placed underneath again; and you shall always increase the fire, moving it closer to the distilling vessel or **cucurbit**, until you see no more oil distilling. Then, indeed, the fire is to be moved away and cast back—and the further the better—until it is extinguished and the upper part of the **cucurbit** has cooled down. And then you will remove the lower part, in which the oil is, and keep it separately. 

However, the reason that the fire ought to be very small in the beginning and ought to be gradually increased so that it reaches the **fourth degree** <note>in traditional alchemy, heat was measured in four degrees, from a gentle "lukewarm" heat to the intense heat of a blast furnace</note>, is for two reasons. 

The first is that from that abundant and vehement heat, all moisture from which oil can be made is consumed; and this happens especially in the oils of egg yolks and **nutmeg** <note>original: "nucis muscatæ"</note>. But in others, such as in oil of **juniper** <note>original: "Iuniperi"</note>, you can make a sudden fire, as much as you like. 

The second reason is that the burning wood itself might harm the **cucurbit**, so that it might be scorched, or broken, or the oil might attract some foul smell <note>original: "fetorem"</note> from it; and therefore the fire in the beginning is to be made according to the course of nature, since nature itself suffers nothing violent, as has already been proven from the opinions of **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina, the Persian philosopher and physician</note> and **Aristotle** <note>the Greek philosopher whose physical theories formed the basis of medieval science</note> in the **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence<gloss>original: "Quinta Essentia"; the purest element, often identified as the medicinal spirit of a substance</gloss></term> of wine. 

But in simple **blessed oil** <note>original: "oleo benedicto"; a common name in early pharmacy for "Oil of Bricks," used for joint and nerve pain</note> you ought at least to increase the fire at the end of the distillation. You can also make a furnace in this way: such that there is a pit dug in the earth, in length, width, and depth...

[depth] <note>original: "profunditate"</note>, as the material to be distilled requires, in which a **glazed pot** <note>original: "olla vitreata"; a ceramic vessel coated with a glass-like glaze to make it non-porous</note> is placed, well washed with water to prevent the distilled oils from entering its pores. Over the mouth of this pot, a thin iron plate should be placed, perforated with many holes, so that the pot rises above the plate by the width of one oblique or transverse finger; and over this earthen vessel, another is placed, turning one mouth against the other.

In that upper vessel should be the **material to be distilled**, sealing the vessel well so that the material cannot escape. Then everything should be **luted** <term>luted<gloss>original: "lutentur"; sealed with "lute," a paste of clay, dung, or other materials used to make laboratory joints airtight</gloss></term> very well, and a fire should be made around the upper pot, which must be entirely outside the pit, except for the mouth, which should match the size of the pit. 

But this second method cannot be performed as well as the first, for two reasons: 

The first of these is that on the first run you cannot separate the water from the oil. The second is that one cannot easily know when all the material has been distilled, unless you learn through frequent use and experience. 

A furnace for **distilling by descent** <term>distilling by descent<gloss>original: "distillandi per descensum"; a method where the substance is heated from above and the distilled liquid drips downward into a receiver, often used for heavy oils</gloss></term> can also be made another way, in this manner: 

Let a pit be made in a steep mound <note>original: "colle præcipiti"; likely referring to a bank of earth or ash used to insulate and support the furnace</note>, and let a distilling vessel be placed below in the manner described before, always placing another above it, in the bottom of which are many holes. And over this distilling vessel or pot, a cover should be placed, sealing or **luting** it very well everywhere from the lower to the upper part. And the circumference of the upper pot should be lower than the mound, so that the mound rises above the pot itself; then let a fire be made in the manner described above. You may also place a glass vessel beneath this pot. For the front part of the furnace belonging to the lower pot ought to be open, so that you can recognize when the entire material has been distilled.

### ¶ On the method of closing the Seal of Hermes.
## Chapter Twenty.

Often, oil or noble water <note>original: "aqua nobilis"; likely referring to highly purified distillates or potent chemical waters like aqua fortis</note> requires an exceptional closure, so that the spirit and **vegetative power** <note>original: "virtus vegetativa"; the vital or life-sustaining force believed to be contained within organic substances</note> cannot evaporate or expire from it; and quite often this is required by the material to be **circulated** <term>circulated<gloss>original: "circulanda"; a process where a liquid is distilled and condensed repeatedly in a closed loop within a single vessel</gloss></term> or fermented, so that the power of the matter is not diminished. This closure is called the **Seal of Hermes**, and among all other methods of sealing, it is the most noble and excellent. It is typically done in this manner:

Let the glass or distilling vessel, filled up to a third part with the material to be digested and circulated, have a long neck. It should be inserted from below into the opening of a small furnace, just as was described in the preceding chapter, so that the vessel itself passes through the furnace opening by **four oblique fingers** <note>a traditional measurement based on the width of four fingers held at an angle</note>. If, however, the opening is wider than the neck of the glass, it must be obstructed with clay on all sides, so that nothing around the glass can fall into the lower furnace; but let the mouth of the glass be free within the furnace, and in no way surrounded by clay. 

Afterward, let a small fire of coals be made in the upper part of the furnace, as far removed as possible from the glass or distilling vessel itself. Then, you shall frequently bring the fire closer to the **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit<gloss>original: "cucurbita"; the gourd-shaped lower part of the distilling apparatus that holds the raw material</gloss></term>, gradually increasing the heat until you see the glass itself grow red-hot, as if it were about to melt. Take then a blacksmith’s **tongs** <note>original: "forcipem"</note> that is glowing hot at the front, with which you shall squeeze the uppermost red-hot part of the glass, gradually heating and turning the tongs, and slowly drawing it away from there. You will see the glass itself in that part becomes whole <note>that is, fused into a single, airtight piece of glass</note>, just as it is in the other parts. After this, therefore...

-># ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE<-

the fire is to be moved away slightly and lessened, in such a form and manner that it is done gradually, until the vessel itself is completely cooled; because if a sudden cold should follow after the heat has expanded the glass, it would break immediately, and thus all the labor, diligence, and expense would be in vain. Therefore, the fire must be diminished with no small care, and the glass itself cooled. In this way, you can keep the instrument or closed vessel sealed for as long as you wish. 

When, however, you wish to open it again, take a **sulfur-treated string** <note>original: "filum sulphuratum"; a cord or wick dipped in sulfur, used here to create a localized line of intense heat to crack the glass precisely</note>, with which you shall wrap the neck of the glass six or seven times. Let the sulfur itself be lit little by little with a wax candle, and when the whole string has been burned, the glass itself will break <note>This technique uses "thermal shock"—the rapid expansion and contraction of the glass—to create a clean break</note>. Therefore, remove the neck and put in or take out whatever you wish. 

Alternatively, you may have three or four iron instruments, the length of two **cubits** <note>a cubit is an ancient unit of measurement based on the length of a forearm, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</note> or a little more, at the ends of which are two rings, one of these—

### ¶ On the method of distilling vinegar and human urine,
### in which all calcined metals <note>original: "calcinata"; metals that have been reduced to a powder through heating or oxidation</note> can be dissolved.
### Chapter Twenty-one.

In the previous chapters of this work, we stated that **Sol** <note>original: "Solem"; literally "the Sun," the alchemical name for Gold</note> can be dissolved in distilled vinegar, and also how the **Quintessence** <note>the "fifth essence" or purest medicinal extract of a substance</note> is extracted from lead and antimony by it. It is therefore necessary to describe the method of distillation for beginners <note>original: "tyrunculis"; literally "little recruits" or novices</note>. For there is a great difference between the distillation of **aqua vitae** <note>original: "aqua vitæ"; "water of life," referring to concentrated alcohol or spirits of wine</note> and vinegar. 

This is because in the "water of life," the better substance is distilled first. In vinegar, however, it is the last. Thus, the water or **phlegm** <note>original: "phlegma"; the watery, tasteless, and medically inactive byproduct of distillation</note> in wine comes out later, but in vinegar, it comes out first. 

Therefore, so that the better substance of the vinegar may flow down into the receiver through distillation, you should take the best vinegar you can obtain and place it in a <term>cucurbit</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass or ceramic vessel used as the base of a still</note>, placing an <term>alembic</term> <note>the "cap" or head of the still that directs vapors into a cooling tube</note> on top. It is then distilled through ashes <note>a "sand bath" or "ash bath" used to provide even, indirect heat</note>, or in a **Mary’s bath** <note>original: "balneo Mariæ"; a bain-marie or water bath</note> with a slow fire, until no more water comes out. 

Afterward, the liquid should be tasted again and again upon the tongue. If it is strengthened in sourness with a certain sharpness or "bite," then it is time to remove the receiver and place another in its place, which must be well **luted** <note>original: "lutatum"; sealed at the joints with a paste made of clay, lime, or flour to prevent vapors from escaping</note>, and the fire should be increased slightly. And when you see small white lines, like little clouds, in the alembic, you shall continue distilling until the spirits come out,

<summary>This page concludes instructions on using thermal shock to open glass vessels and begins Chapter 21, which explains the specific technical differences between distilling alcohol and vinegar for alchemical use.</summary>
<keywords>glassblowing, distillation, vinegar, human urine, gold, lead, antimony, cucurbit, alembic, bain-marie, luting, quintessence</keywords>

# DE SECRETIS NATVRAE
<meta>catchword: De modo</meta>

...the vapor of which you will see rise up into the top of the **cucurbit** <term>cucurbita: a gourd-shaped glass vessel that forms the lower part of a still, named for its gourd-like shape</term>, and exit from the **alembic** <term>alembicus: the cap or "helmet" of the still that redirects vapor into a cooling tube</term> into the receiver itself. But when you see bloody drops appear in the alembic, another receiver should be placed before it, and let it be distilled just as much until all the bloody matter has passed out into the receiver. And this matter is very foul-smelling, with an odor of burning; therefore, this vinegar is not good for dissolving **calcined Sol** <note>original: "Solem calcinatum"; Sol is the alchemical name for Gold. Calcination is the process of heating a metal until it becomes a fine powder.</note> for the purpose of making **potable gold** <term>aurum potabile: a legendary medicinal oil of gold believed to grant near-immortality</term>. However, it is quite good for tinting metals, because that stench of burning would cling to the Solar substance, by which the potable gold would be corrupted rather than improved. 

If, however, you wish to distill human urine, in which gold leaf or even calcined gold can be dissolved—from which the distilled urine itself can extract the color to make the already oft-mentioned potable gold—it also has the greatest power against **gout** <margin>for gout</margin> <note>original: "podagram"; a painful form of inflammatory arthritis, historically associated with rich diets</note>, provided that the feet are smeared with it two or three times every day and allowed to dry on their own.

It is also useful for **consumptives** <note>original: "ptificis," referring to phthisis or tuberculosis</note>, and can be adapted for many medicines which we shall omit for the sake of brevity, as it is not our purpose here. Therefore, you shall distill human urine in this way: Take the urine of a healthy man of a **sanguine or choleric temperament** <note>In medieval medicine, these humors suggested a person was warm, active, and had good circulation</note>, who drinks good wine and has not yet passed his thirty-fifth year. Let it be distilled four times through an alembic in a **Bain-marie** <term>Balneo Mariæ: a "bath of Mary," or water bath, used to provide gentle, even heat</term>. Afterward, let it be circulated in a **pelican** <term>pellicanus: a specialized circulatory distillation vessel with "arms" that feed condensed liquid back into the main body</term> for forty days, and keep it for use. Others distill it seven times, which is even better.

## On the method of distilling honey, which is an ingredient in potable gold itself.

### Chapter Twenty-Two.

<margin>On these matters see Pliny, book 11, Chapters 14 & 16. <note>original: "Plin. lib. 11. Cap. 14. & . 16."; Ulstad refers to Pliny the Elder’s *Natural History*, the foundational Roman encyclopedia of science.</note></margin>
<image-desc>Woodcut illustration of a bulbous distilling vessel, likely a cucurbit, with two curved handles and a small spout at the top, resting on a flat base or furnace decorated with diagonal hatching.</image-desc>
The quality of honey depends on its nation of origin. In some places, the honeycombs produced are remarkable for their **wax**, such as among the Peligni <note>an ancient people of central Italy</note> and in Sicily. In other places, there is an abundance of honey, such as in Crete, Cyprus, and Africa. In others still, it is notable for its size, as in the Northern regions; indeed, honey has been seen in Germany eight feet in length, found in a bundle <note>likely referring to a massive wild honeycomb</note>, and black in its hollow part. However, in whatever region of the earth it is found, there are three main types of honey.

The first is **Spring honey**, built from flowers, which is therefore called *Anthinum* <term>Anthinum: from the Greek "anthos" meaning flower; flowery honey</term>. The second type is **Summer honey**, which is called *horaeum* <term>horaeum: from "hora," referring to the ripe season</term> because of its particular timeliness. The third type of honey is **Wild honey**, which is gathered after the first rains of autumn. This they call *Ericaeum* <term>Ericaeum: heather honey</term>, named after the **heather** shrub (*Erica*), which is also called *sisarum* <note>original: "sisarum"; likely referring to skirret or a similar wild root, though here associated with heather</note>. This plant is the only one flowering in the woods at that time, and thus gives its name to the honey.

But because here in our lands neither **Attic honey** <note>original: "mel Atticum"; honey from Mount Hymettus in Greece, considered the highest quality in antiquity</note> nor the types described above are commonly found, one must take our local honey—the best that can be found. It ought to be reddish, thick, and contain no watery thinness. Let it be placed in a **cauldron** <term>caldare: a large cooking kettle or boiler</term> or an iron chest in whatever quantity you wish; add pure spring water in an amount equal to the honey, and let it be cooked over a slow...

<summary>This page categorizes honey by region and season, citing classical authorities like Pliny, before providing instructions on selecting and preparing local honey for distillation.</summary>
<keywords>honey, distillation, Pliny the Elder, Anthinum, heather honey, Attic honey, alchemy, potable gold, Germany</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for preparing honey as an alchemical base. The previous page discussed the varieties of honey (Spring, Summer, and Wild) and began the process of purification by fire.</meta>

fire, always straining the foam until the water is consumed, and this should be done nine times. Finally, let it be cooked until it is reduced to the proper thickness of honey. After this, let it be circulated in a **Mary’s bath** <term>balneum Mariae: a water bath used for gentle, even heating, similar to a modern double-boiler</term>, heated to the first degree of heat, for forty days. 

Once this is done, place it in a **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit: a gourd-shaped glass or earthenware distilling vessel</term>, which should be longer and taller than those used for distilling other materials. It should be made of earthenware, glazed on the inside. Place it in the Mary’s bath and distill it through an **alembic** <term>alembic: the "still-head" or cap of the distilling apparatus that collects vapors</term>. 

If the honey itself begins to rise or swell, have cold, wet cloths ready to be wrapped around the cucurbit from the inside through the alembic. First, a clear water will come out, which must be stored separately, because it is useful for lengthening and coloring **hair** <margin>Hair. Artificial balsam.</margin>. After this, a yellow and golden water will come out along with an oil; keep this second liquid separately as well. This second water is held by some surgeons in place of an **artificial balsam**. It is effective for washing and surgically treating internal areas, for cleansing deep wounds, and also for **coloring hair** <margin>Coloring hair.</margin>. 

Third and finally, it shall be distilled through ashes <note>original: "per cineres"; a method of applying higher heat by placing the vessel in a bed of hot ashes or sand</note>, and a reddish water will come out, leaning slightly toward blackness. This is useful for cleansing and healing **wounds** <margin>For wounds.</margin> <note>original: "incarnandum"; a medical term meaning to promote the growth of new flesh to close a wound</note>. 

If you wish to obtain a larger quantity of the first water, let that first water be placed back over its own dregs <note>original: "feces"; the solid residue or sediment left after distillation</note> and distill it just as you did the first time. You shall enclose and keep each water or oil separately in its own vial.

In the previous chapters, we have discussed the method of extracting the **Fifth Essence** <term>Fifth Essence: also known as the Quintessence, this was believed to be the purest, most incorruptible part of a substance, capable of transmitting its medicinal virtues</term> from all things that have form and appearance, which are primarily administered for the preparation of **potable gold** <term>potable gold: a liquid form of gold believed by alchemists to be a universal medicine and an elixir of life</term>. Now, it would not be inopportune if we were to discourse briefly on the various methods and ways of making **potable gold**, through which human life might be preserved, as it were, from corruption—that is, until the term of life prescribed for us.

For different practitioners work in different ways, and each praises his own method, even though it may not always be praiseworthy. I find, in fact, four principal ways by which gold was anciently reduced to a drinkable substance. <margin>Four ways of reducing gold into a drinkable substance.</margin> However, so as not to overwhelm the sensitive ears of beginners, I will explain a shorter and more common way; for there is a common proverb: *On a well-trodden path, one cannot go astray.* 

Therefore, I will show a sufficiently trodden and well-traveled path, from which you can obtain the best practical results. It is, indeed, a secret extracted from the **Fifth Essence** itself, upon which the ancient Philosophers spent the greatest labor. Nevertheless, they hid everything from us for a reason: because it possesses a hidden power (which Divine Providence instilled in it) through which youth itself can recover lost beauty and strength; through which <margin>The old become young.</margin> even all the effects of old age are driven away. This does not necessarily change the actual age a person has reached, but it makes an old man appear and feel as if he were young, provided he makes use of it, as will be taught more extensively below. 

And this is the substance and "first matter" in which the ancient Philosophers labored so much, so that they might in some other way preserve human substance and nature from premature corruption. For every kind of man (as the philosopher **The Stagirite** <note>original: "Stagirita"; a common nickname for Aristotle, who was born in the city of Stagira</note> testifies) is desirous...

...can be preserved from corruption. But it has been foreknown and predestined by God, the Best and Greatest, that we must die once. Wherefore our Seneca of Cordoba <note>Seneca the Younger, the Roman Stoic philosopher born in Spain</note> not ungracefully says: *Nothing is more certain than death, and nothing more uncertain than the hour of death.* <note>original: "Nil certius esse morte, & nil incertius esse hora mortis"</note> And Saint Paul [says]: *It is appointed for all to die once.* <note>Hebrews 9:27</note> And the same: *In Adam all die.* <note>1 Corinthians 15:22</note> It is therefore impossible for us to be able to find a corruptible matter <term>corruptible matter: physical substance subject to decay, as opposed to the eternal heavens</term> which might preserve us from corruption, since there is nothing under heaven (according to Aristotle) that is free from corruption. And for that reason, we must find something close to the incorruptible. 

And I judge that many have labored in this pursuit so that they might live eternally, those who have written that the **Fifth Essence** itself is so perfect and incorruptible that it lacks nothing; which is indeed most false. For if it were so perfect that it lacked nothing, it would make us eternal, which is against the will of God, as has already been said quite often. We can, however, find a way and an art of prolonging our life until the term prescribed for us, provided that we fear and revere God as is just and worthy, and that we maintain a regimen <term>regimen: a systematic lifestyle or dietary plan to maintain health</term> that is not harmful to our life.

<margin>Hugo’s Potable Gold.</margin>
Therefore, Johannes of Toledo and Hugo—both Cardinals—made **potable gold** <term>potable gold: "aurum potabile," a liquid gold preparation believed to be a supreme medicine</term> in this way, which they indeed always used in all their foodstuffs and held as a hidden treasure. 

And it is made thus: First and before all things, you must have a water in which gold, silver, and all precious stones can be dissolved. And it is done in this manner: Take one pound each <note>The text uses "añ. lib. i." meaning *ana libra 1* or one pound of each</note> of **sal ammoniac** <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, used by alchemists to dissolve metals</term> and **saltpeter** <term>saltpeter: potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in "strong waters" or acids</term>. Let them be crushed and pulverized in a mortar. Afterwards, add an ounce of **native sulfur** <term>native sulfur: "sulphur vivum," or naturally occurring sulfur that has not been treated by fire</term>...
