An **amalgam** <term>amalgam: a mixture of mercury with another metal, often creating a paste-like consistency</term> can also be made with the grease that remains in **cannons** <note>original: "bombardis"; while referring to artillery, alchemists often sought out the chemical residues left in these weapons after use</note> after they have been frequently strained. ### THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTAD ###

...give it sweetness, such as honey or sugar <note>original: "zuccarū"</note>, and this ought to be taken in food or drink whenever you wish. For human nature is best sustained by it. It also creates a good memory.
<margin>for memory.</margin>
If indeed a leper washes with it, it will benefit him wonderfully; in a similar way, it also helps those suffering from paralysis. For it is a spirit capable of recovering all lost heat <note>In the medical theories of the time, "vital heat" was the spark of life; its loss resulted in aging, fatigue, or chronic illness</note>. And in this manner, all precious stones can be reduced into a potable form, but you must not add Mercury, but only sulfur.
<margin>The method of preparing precious stones for potable gold.</margin>
And therefore, you may take any precious stone you like and pulverize it thoroughly. After this, you shall mix in an equal amount of purified sulfur, and let them all be ground together excellently until they are reduced to a very fine powder. Afterward, let them be placed in a crucible <note>original: "tigillo"</note> over hot coals until the sulfur catches fire and is entirely burned away, and all the stench has departed from the crucible. Then, let it be ground again upon a marble slab into a very fine consistency, adding just as much purified sulfur as the first time, and let it be burned again as described above. And let this be done a third time, pulverizing it very finely, and then it will be prepared. 

Take then half an ounce <note>original: "vnc. sem." (uncia semis)</note> of the aforementioned stones, thus purged and cleansed, and three ounces <note>original: "vnc. iij."</note> of the aforementioned strong water <term>strong water: "aqua fortis," a powerful solvent like nitric acid</term>. Let them be put together into a glass vessel with a long and narrow neck, closing it well, and immediately the stones will be dissolved. When they have been dissolved, let the glass be opened so that the strong water may evaporate and lose its whole substance and power. You will then see the precious stones at the bottom like butter, which you will work with in the same manner as you did with the golden butter. And if you add "water of life" <term>aqua vitæ: high-proof distilled alcohol, believed to have life-extending properties</term> and the aforementioned spices, you have true potable gold, which has great effect in various and most serious illnesses.

CONCERNING THE SECRETS OF NATURE

## ¶ Concerning potable gold without "strong water."
<note>Potable gold (aurum potabile) was a legendary alchemical medicine believed to grant longevity and cure all ailments. "Strong water" (aqua fortis) refers to nitric acid, which was the standard solvent for gold but was considered too toxic for internal medicinal use.</note>
### Chapter Twenty-Four.

<margin>From which the philosopher's stone is made.</margin>
You can also make potable gold <note>original: "aurum potabile"</note> without strong water, and this is better suited for medicine than the method previously mentioned. This is done by dissolving your gold through the "water of life" <note>original: "aquam vitæ," likely referring here to highly distilled alcohol or a specialized solvent</note> to obtain its power from this stone, which is called "Philosophical." This stone is made from the Fifth Essence <term>Quinta Essentia: The "fifth element" beyond earth, air, fire, and water; a pure, celestial substance that alchemists sought to extract from physical matter.</term> through the separation of the four elements, as has already been heard many times above. For the Fifth Essence itself possesses such great power that whatever you place into it, it draws that thing's efficacy and force into itself. For if it can preserve dead flesh from decay, how much more so will it preserve human flesh that is still living and contains the spirit of life? It also digests everything that is indigestible, and by its own nature, it leads out everything that has been digested. Therefore, it continually expels all bad humors <note>In medieval medicine, health was governed by four "humors" or bodily fluids; an imbalance caused disease.</note> and complexions of human nature from the body, since it is not a mere element, but a most subtle substance extracted from the elements. And because the four elements themselves are corruptible <note>perishable or subject to decay</note>, it is therefore necessary to have an incorruptible substance—or one most similar to the incorruptible—and this is the "fifth being" itself, in which gold can be dissolved and all its power extracted.

<margin>Gold does not suffer decay by nature, but only through artifice.</margin>
For gold suffers no decay from the four qualities <note>The qualities are heat, cold, moisture, and dryness, which compose the material world.</note> except by artificial means, and not by nature. For who has ever seen gold rot in water? Who has seen it burned by fire? Who has seen it draw rust from the earth? Finally, who has known it to be consumed by the air? But these things are possible in all other substances. And therefore, since gold itself [is of] the Fifth Essen-

<meta>catchword: -tial [completing the word "Essentiae" or "Essence"]</meta>

<summary>This page explains why gold should be dissolved in the "Fifth Essence" rather than nitric acid for medical use, arguing that because gold and the Quintessence are both incorruptible, they are the perfect agents for preserving human health.</summary>
<keywords>potable gold, aurum potabile, Quinta Essentia, Fifth Essence, four elements, humors, alchemy, corruption, incorruptibility</keywords>

...of the essence <note>original: "essentiæ"</note> is added and mixed, it is called potable. Therefore, it is a matter of great science to find a refined method for dissolving gold and bringing it into a potable form, lest perhaps, when you think you have made the "water of life," you instead make the "water of death." 

This potable gold <note>original: "aurum potabile"</note> must therefore be prepared with the water of life <note>original: "aqua vitæ"</note> that has been rightly and duly rectified, as is fitting, by extracting the four elements from the wine itself in which they lie hidden. Do not let yourself turn human blood <note>Human blood was occasionally used in alchemical preparations as a source of "vital spirit," though Ulstadt here warns against its improper use leading to decay.</note> into a foul and stinky-smelling matter, for in such a state it would lose its powers and absorb all the stench from the oil of the human blood. 

And if it were not made from that very "earth" from which the Philosopher's Stone is composed, you would not be able to dissolve the Sun <note>In alchemy, the "Sun" (Sol) is the universal symbol for gold.</note> itself. Because if you thought to make a "fiery water," it would be clearly absurd and harmful to the user; therefore, do not worry if there is only a tiny amount of this earth, for however small it may be, it possesses the greatest power. 

You must also beware of taking "foreign earth" <note>In this context, "earth" refers to the solid base or mineral reactant used in the distillation or transmutation process.</note> as some do, who use lime, or others who use willow ashes, or others burnt tartar, or finally others the ashes of grape-vines, as we have already described at length above. With this "foreign earth," they imagine they are making the water of life, when in fact they do not realize they are preparing the water of death. 

As Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century scholar often called the "Father of Chemistry."</note> says: the earth and the medicine of a different mixture are incompatible, as we have already said quite often. For this [correct] earth confers its power upon the water of life itself, provided it has been prepared as we taught above. 

<margin>In the Fifth Essence, gold is dissolved in two ways.</margin> 

It is necessary, therefore, to have a method for separating the four elements so that you can make the true water of life—or rather, the Fifth Essence <term>Quinta Essentia: A highly concentrated medicinal spirit believed to be the pure "soul" of a substance, free from the impurities of the four physical elements (earth, air, fire, water).</term>—in which gold can be dissolved in two ways. First, with the water of life, which draws its power from the Philosopher's Stone.

<summary>Philip Ulstadt warns against the dangers of improper alchemical preparations, distinguishing between the "water of life" and the "water of death." He emphasizes that "potable gold" must be dissolved using the Fifth Essence and the correct "philosophical earth" rather than common caustic ashes or lime.</summary>

<margin>Method of separating the four elements.</margin>
Secondly, indeed, by reducing gold into a powder, or by calcining <note>original: "calcinando"; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in order to oxidize it or reduce it to a fine powder</note>, as will be taught below. When you wish, therefore, to separate the four elements from the water of life <note>original: "aqua vitæ"; a term for concentrated alcohol or medicinal spirits</note> itself, or from the wine from which the water of life is made, you must take the best possible wine in a great quantity, placing it into many cucurbits <note>original: "cucurbitas"; gourd-shaped glass vessels used as the base of a still</note>, so that a good quantity can enter into each cucurbit. And when all the wine has been distilled, set it aside, and you shall also keep the phlegm <note>original: "phlegma"; the watery, non-alcoholic portion of the distillate</note> separately. And the distilled wine should always be placed back over its dregs <note>original: "feces"; the solid residue or sediment remaining after distillation</note>, and when the phlegm has been extracted from the earth itself as if from its dregs, the earth itself remains at the bottom. When, therefore, you have obtained the earth—which is the burnt matter, as was said above—let it be washed thoroughly with the phlegm, as has already been shown, so that nothing of the substance smelling of combustion remains; and through this, the water of life itself is justified <note>To "justify" in this alchemical context means to purify, rectify, or bring a substance to its true, perfected state</note>. There is also another more subtle method:

<margin>Another method of separating the four elements.</margin>
When the wine itself has been extracted from its dregs, let the phlegm be taken along with that which remained in the distilling vessel, from which you separated the distilled wine, and let it be cooked in a cauldron over a tempered fire for so long until it is reduced to the thickness of melted honey. Afterward, let it be removed from the fire and allowed to cool until the following day, and you will see a little stone <note>original: "lapillum"; likely referring to the formation of crystals, such as potassium bitartrate (cream of tartar), common in wine processing</note> as if residing at the bottom, as clear as if it were saltpeter <note>original: "sal nitrum"; potassium nitrate</note>. Then let the water be strained, and the oil floating on top and the phlegm itself should be poured over the aforementioned stone or earth, until it is clarified from its dregs. This being done, let it be boiled a second time and cooled as above. And do this so many times until the earth is separated from it. Then you shall dry it in a reverberatory furnace <note>original: "furno reuerberationis"; a type of furnace where the flame and heat are deflected or "reverberated" off the roof onto the material being heated</note> in a clean vessel,

<margin>Another way of making potable gold.</margin>

Potable gold is also made by separating each of the four elements individually and then extracting the Quintessence <term>Quinta Essentia: the "fifth essence," considered the most purified and medicinal core of a substance, superior to the four physical elements</term>. In this Quintessence, you can dissolve the Sun <note>original: "Solem"; the common alchemical name for gold, reflecting its association with the celestial body</note> and transform it into potable gold. It is required, however, that the gold be hammered into the thinnest possible leaves. 

And so, when the spirit (which is like the soul) has been drawn through the alembic away from its phlegm (which is like the body), take the dregs or "earth" that ultimately remained in the distilling vessel. Add four times as much of its own phlegm and place it in the Mary's bath for nine days until it is well putrefied. Then distill it through the alembic, and a lemon-yellow oil <note>original: "oleum citrinum"</note> will emerge. When no more can rise into the alembic, know that three elements still remain in the distilling vessel. For you will see the [itself]...

### ¶ Concerning potable gold without earth or strong water.
### Chapter Twenty-five.

The method for making potable gold <note>liquid gold intended for consumption as a medicine</note> without this sharpness of the [philosophers'] stone or strong water is as follows: Take honey, and pour it drop by drop onto a marble slab, adding and placing the gold leaves themselves upon it. Rub them frequently—just as if you wished to prepare gold for writing—until no trace of the gold is visible at all <note>the honey acts as a grinding medium to reduce the gold to microscopic, ingestible particles</note>. And when you have rubbed two or three leaves of gold in this way, you shall let it stand for twenty-four hours. You may also add some prepared common salt, but this is not necessary except to grind the material more quickly. Once the material has settled for the stated period, let it be placed in a silver vessel or in a wide cup, pouring warm water over it to [wash away] the honey, or [using] spring...

## SECTION ON THE SEPARATION OF GOLD

...distilled <note>The word likely completes "purified" or "cleansed" from the previous page</note>, and the honey will be separated from the gold itself. If, however, you have added salt, it will also be separated in this way. Then you shall strain the salt itself by pouring another distilled water over it; let it settle as described above, and it will be dissolved in the same manner as was stated. And when you have mixed in the salt, you will perform this more often than if you had mixed in none, until the whole is separated and dissolved. Then pour more water over it again in the aforementioned manner, and place it in a <term>cucurbit: a gourd-shaped glass vessel used for distillation</term>, distilling it in a <term>balneum Mariae: a "Mary's bath" or double boiler, providing gentle and even heat</term>, so that only the gold remains in the distilling vessel, and the salt and honey are separated from it. If, however, you did not mix in salt, the gold must be ground for a much longer time upon a marble slab. 

When all the honey has been distilled from the gold, let [fresh liquid] be poured over it and distilled again; you shall do this two or three times. Finally, when you know with certainty that all the honey has been separated from the gold, let there be poured over it some of the best distilled vinegar—as we taught above—and let it be made from the best wine you can obtain, or that which is distilled from "cooked" wine <note>likely fortified or reduced wine</note>. Let it be distilled four times. 

When you have distilled the vinegar for the last time, let three parts be distilled away, and let the fourth part remain in the bottom of the distilling vessel. Because (as was said before) the distillation of vinegar and wine are the opposite of one another: for in wine, the better substance is distilled first, but in vinegar, the better part is distilled last <note>The author refers to the fact that alcohol (the "spirit") evaporates first from wine, whereas in vinegar, the water evaporates first, leaving the concentrated acid behind in the vessel</note>. 

This fourth part, therefore, which remained in the bottom, you ought to pour over the gold itself, and let it rest for three days; afterward, let them be distilled together. Then you shall pour on as much distilled vinegar as you did at first, but it is necessary that the vessel be placed in <term>fimus equinus: horse dung, used by alchemists as a natural, steady heat source for digestion and fermentation</term> or in a Mary’s bath. In this vinegar, therefore, the gold is most refined...

...is most easily <note>original: "liffime", likely completing "subtilissime" (most finely) or "facillime" (most easily) from the previous page</note> dissolved; then, it should be separated from the gold again by distillation in a <term>balneum Mariae: "Mary’s bath," a double boiler or water bath used for gentle heating</term>. Afterward, pour the <term>phlegma: the watery, inactive part of a distillate</term> of distilled vinegar over it, and distill it again in the water bath; finally, pour the phlegm over it again and distill it in the same way. Do this until you have reached the number seven <note>Alchemists often repeated processes seven times to align with the seven known planets or for spiritual perfection</note>. 

After it has been distilled seven times, add some <term>aqua vitae: "water of life," or highly concentrated alcohol</term>, and let it be separated again by distillation. Finally, you shall pour over it another "water of life" which contains no phlegm within itself, and let it be separated in the same way; let this be done in two stages. In this manner, you will most finely distill the honey, vinegar, and phlegm away from the gold itself, together with the water of life. It is then excellently digested and prepared to be dissolved in a <term>digestive or circulatory: specialized alchemical vessels for long-term heating and recycling of vapors</term> within that same water of life, which is the <term>quinta essentia: the "fifth essence" or the purest, most potent core of a substance</term>.

When you wish to make <term>aurum potabile: drinkable or "potable gold," believed to be a universal medicine</term>: take the aforementioned resolved gold and pour over it the prescribed water of life—twelve parts of which have been distilled from one portion of the <term>lapis philosophici: the Philosopher’s Stone</term>. Place it to circulate in the water bath for forty days, or longer. In this way, the gold will be resolved into a lemon-yellow water. 

But since all things must be judged fairly, this liquid possesses a certain "bite" or sharpness from the distilled vinegar and the fifth essence, which is distilled through and from the Philosopher’s Stone itself. By means of this liquid, as if by <term>aqua fortis: "strong water," or nitric acid</term>, gold can be dissolved. However, it is not as harmful as it would be if made with [common] nitric acid, although some are of the opinion that it is not very useful for healthy people. For we sometimes see that some things are useful to the sick...

# PHILIP ULSTAD'S BOOK

But since **potable gold** itself ought to be drunk by the healthy so that they may be preserved in their former health, our more recent philosophers have taken the aforementioned gold, pulverized as has already been described, which was prepared without the **Quintessence** <note>The "fifth essence," or the most purified and potent form of a substance</note> of distilled vinegar, by pouring over it true, rectified <term>aqua vitae: "water of life," or concentrated alcohol</term> that has been separated from all its <term>phlegm: the watery, inactive part of a distillate</term> without using the aforementioned stone. This is done through three or four cycles, always distilling the liquid away from it again, so that at the end of the distillation some moisture from the gold always remains.

Finally, they took one part of this same moisture and twelve parts of the quintessence, and placed them in a <term>cucurbit: a gourd-shaped chemical flask</term>, placing a <term>blind alembic: a lid used in distillation that has no spout, forcing vapors to circulate back down</term> on top. They then circulated it for at least forty days in a <term>balneum Mariae: "Mary’s bath," a gentle water bath or double boiler</term>; then you shall have a true and justified **potable gold** <note>original: "aurum potabile"</note>, both to preserve the healthy from infirmity and to free the sick from disease.

However, some harm could arise from such a drink for the reason we alleged in the Ninth Chapter written above, because over a long period of time the human stomach could become **gilded** <note>original: "deaurari." The author fears that microscopic gold particles might plate the lining of the stomach like a piece of jewelry</note> by this. Through this "gilding," the stomach could lose its accustomed digestion; anyone, even those minimally skilled in medicine, can easily judge how much harm could arise to the stomach from such a condition. Moved by this reasoning, we wished rather to benefit than to harm men. And although I might be attacked by many of those more skilled in this science for making such great knowledge known to the common public, we respond to them that this was done because we wish to benefit everyone equally, since <insert>Solomon</insert> <note>The text cuts off here, but refers to the biblical King Solomon, often cited by alchemists as a source of wisdom</note> says...

### ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE
<meta>catchword: while,</meta>

Solomon says: Hidden wisdom and a hidden treasure are of no use <note>original: "Abscōditam sapientiam & absconditum thesaurum nihil prodesse," a reference to the biblical book of Sirach 20:30</note>. Therefore, I will teach the truest way of making **potable gold** <note>Gold in a liquid, drinkable form, believed in alchemy to be a universal medicine</note> according to the capacity of my humble intellect, although, by the god of truth <note>original: "Mediusfidius," an ancient Roman oath to the deity of honesty</note>, it may not be entirely fitting to reveal such a great treasure; yet this has been published more for the learned and lovers of the good arts than for the ignorant. And so I would ask all those into whose hands this little work of ours may come, that if I have said anything more secret (which it is neither lawful nor right to reveal to the unlearned) than perhaps should have been said, they should judge it with an open mind. For this thought of Cicero's was before my eyes day and night:

> We are not born for ourselves alone, but our country claims a share of us, and our friends another. <note>original: "Non nobis solū natos esse, sed & partem de nobis sibi Patriam vendicare, & partem amici." From Cicero's *On Duties* (De Officiis), 1.22</note>

And since the most wise Solomon says, "Hidden wisdom," etc., I immediately wished to bring this book into the light so that it might be an honor and a benefit to both my country and my parents. For although I have published it for the unlearned as well as the learned, let any envious person know that this was not done without reason, since the highest creator and father of all creatures visits both the upright and the wicked with the same solar light. For cursed is he who prefers his own profit to the advantage of the commonwealth. And although it may be held of little value <note>original: "floccipendi," literally "valued at a tuft of wool"</note> by the unskilled, I know nevertheless that some of the more learned will praise my small effort to the heavens. Therefore, I will teach you the truest **potable gold**, against which (in my opinion) none of our native philosophers can argue, provided they have examined the natural reasons set down below: for I know that there is one way of speaking among **alchemists**, and another among **physicians**. But since the certain must be chosen over the uncertain, and the profitable over the harmful,

# THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTADIUS. 59

...while, and therefore I will stick with the physicians themselves, since Marsilio Ficino <note>A famous Italian philosopher and physician (1433–1499) who wrote extensively on health and longevity</note> says: "None of the ancient philosophers ever followed this way of making **potable gold** <note>Gold in a liquid, drinkable form, believed to be a universal medicine</note> which our local philosophers now pursue." For who would doubt concerning **Sun**? <note>The alchemical name for gold</note> which they dissolve in **aqua fortis** <note>original: "aqua forti"; literally "strong water," referring to nitric acid</note>; who doubts concerning **Mercury**, with which they amalgamate (to use their own terms), that these things are not harmful? No one, by Hercules! No one, unless they were blinder than a mole. Although I do not deny that these substances can enter into potable gold for the sick, such as those with leprosy—for poison must be driven out by poison <note>original: "venenum enim veneno pellandum est," a traditional medical proverb</note>—yet by the god of truth <note>original: "Mediusfidius," a Roman oath</note>, I would not advise using them, since they are highly corrosive (as even anyone moderately learned can guess).

It is similar regarding that which is produced by distilled vinegar, or that which is dissolved through the stone. However, it is not as harmful as that which is dissolved through aqua fortis; yet it possesses a certain violent sharpness which the **Fifth Essence** <note>The "Quinta Essentia," a highly purified spirit or alcohol used as a solvent in alchemy</note> itself received from the stone.

If indeed this reasoning should seem insufficient to some, it can be further proven by the saying of **Galen** <note>A prominent Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries</note>, who says that the natural substance is incompatible with any more violent movement—a point to which **Aristotle** <note>The famous Greek philosopher</note> also agrees. This is to be understood as follows: this potable gold, of which we made mention above, cannot be made except through the excessive fiery nature of the Fifth Essence itself; which fiery nature, when it has been **sublimated** <note>A process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then becomes solid again, used here to describe increasing its "potency"</note> beyond its proper limit, dissolves the Sun <note>gold</note> very quickly. Therefore, in my opinion, such excessive fieriness causes more discomfort to human nature than benefit. And for this reason, not without cause, another more subtle method must be found, by which gold into a [potable] substance...

And therefore it can wonderfully temper and moderate natural heat with coldness, and preserve the **humors** <note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) which were thought to govern health and temperament</note> themselves from decay and corruption; and therefore we attribute to it the power of the Sun and Jupiter, whose duty it is to grant the **vital spirit** <note>The life-force believed to be carried through the blood to animate the organs</note> to the internal organs. Hence the philosophers themselves prove that no thing is more suitable for preserving human life somewhat from corruption than a most subtle substance...

### THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTAD. 60

H iiii

[temperate] and hardest, almost incorruptible, which penetrates to the inner parts of the body. For this reason, gold itself, beaten into very thin leaves and mixed with **cordial spices** <note>Medicinal ingredients believed to strengthen the heart</note>, is to be reduced to a powder. Therefore, **John of Rupescissa** <note>A 14th-century Franciscan alchemist known for his works on the "Fifth Essence"</note> says that we must take care to work with what is possible and to avoid what is impossible. For we see many wonders regarding steel, lead, iron, tin, and silver; why then should we not believe there is greater efficacy in that which is more precious, such as gold itself?

Indeed, we see (as **Avicenna** <note>A famous Persian physician and philosopher (c. 980–1037) whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe</note> testifies) that the very water in which glowing steel has been **quenched** <note>The process of cooling hot metal rapidly in liquid to change its properties</note> restrains a **flux of the belly** <note>original: "fluxum ventris"; a historical term for diarrhea</note> and, as it were, binds the gathered intestines together. <margin>Against the flux of the belly.</margin> This may also be seen with iron itself. How much more, then, should this be believed concerning gold, in which there is nothing corruptible of the four elements, as there is in iron, steel, and the rest. 

For if steel itself has the power of constricting and, as it were, binding the viscera and intestines together, does this not seem more possible in gold, since it is purer, more refined, and more incorruptible than steel? Secondly, it can also be proven from evident reason and experience: when lead is repeatedly melted into water in which a plate of gold, steel, or iron has been quenched, we see the lead itself harden. From this, we can clearly recognize that the water itself has drawn out the **virtue** <note>original: "vim"; the inherent power or medicinal property</note> from the matter which was quenched in it. Conversely, we also experience through daily use that if lead is melted into some water, and then iron, steel, or gold is quenched in that same water, it is most manifest that the metal softens from...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the medicinal properties of metals. The previous page ended with a comparison of steel's astringent effects when quenched in water. <header>ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE</header>

...water, which extracts power from lead, [granting] a softness that they do not possess by their own nature. Therefore, just as this is discovered from daily use regarding iron and lead, so also is it the case regarding gold. And this is the true method among natural philosophers concerning **potable gold** <note>original: "auro potabili"; gold in a drinkable form, highly sought after as a universal medicine or elixir of life</note>, as **Marsilio Ficino** <note>A celebrated 15th-century Italian scholar and physician who sought to harmonize Platonic philosophy with medicine and magic</note> says. However, it is not that its physical substance is drunk, but rather its very virtue and efficacy, extracted from it in the most subtle manner. For it is entirely false to believe that the matter of gold itself enters the composition of the human body, as the aforementioned Marsilio most beautifully proves. It is also false to believe that it is assimilated into the human body, or that it is converted into a potable substance. For it would first have to be transmuted into blood, and after that into flesh, which is impossible because of its hardness.

And therefore it should be known that in very many things the virtue of gold strengthens and sustains the human body, even though the material substance does not enter the [body's] composition. For just as the **magnet** <note>original: "magnes"</note> itself, through its vigor—by which it acts upon iron—attracts it to itself, so also gold itself, through its exceptional brilliance, infuses its virtue into the water in which it is quenched, by which the human body is wonderfully strengthened. And therefore, those who thus convert gold into a drinkable substance through **strong water** <note>original: "aquam fortem"; usually refers to nitric acid, a powerful corrosive used to dissolve metals</note> or (as we said above) through **distilled vinegar** <note>original: "acetum distillatum"</note>, corrupt its virtue more than they ennoble it, even though they think they are extracting it from the metal.

# THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTAD. 61

If, however, you wish to make **potable gold** <note>original: "aurum potabile"; a medicinal elixir believed to contain the spiritual essence of gold</note> with simple botanical ingredients, some of which draw out superfluous moisture while others strengthen the nobler **organs** <note>original: "mēbra nobiliora"; specifically the heart, liver, and brain</note>. Therefore, such potable gold must be used with a just and true regimen and diet—concerning waking, sleeping, eating, drinking, filling, and emptying. And it is made thus:

Take thirty-two pounds of the best wine you can obtain, and let it be placed in the boiler of a **Mary’s bath** <note>original: "balnei Mariae"; a double boiler or water bath used to provide gentle, even heat</note>, and let it be distilled until it is reduced to one **measure** <note>original: "cantarum"</note>. Then take other wine and distill it in the same manner. And do this repeatedly until you have thirty-two pounds of distilled wine. Afterward, you shall take all the distilled wine and place it again in the boiler, and let it be distilled until half of it is consumed. Then let it be placed in a **cucurbit** <note>original: "cucurbitam"; the lower, gourd-shaped vessel of a distillation apparatus</note> and distilled until all the watery substance has departed. 

Afterward, let it be distilled in a Mary’s bath four times, and after the distillation, let these ingredients be added: raw **agarwood** <note>original: "Ligni aloes"; the fragrant, resinous heartwood of the aquilaria tree</note>, cardamom, and fresh **cubebs** <note>original: "cubebarum"; a type of pepper from Java</note>, one **drachm** <note>original: "drag. i."</note> of each; cinnamon, nutmeg, mace, white ginger, **long pepper** <note>original: "macropiperis"</note>, grains of paradise, and both types of **sandalwood** <note>original: "sandali utriusque"; referring to red and white sandalwood</note>, four drachms of each.

Let all these be very finely powdered and placed in a glass vessel, pouring the distilled wine over them. Closing the glass vessel very tightly, let it be placed in a Mary’s bath for eight days, placing an **alembic** <note>original: "alembicum"; the cap or lid of the still that directs vapors into a cooling tube</note> over it, and let it be distilled with a slow fire. Then let it be placed in a long-necked cucurbit. Afterward, add two drachms of pure **gold leaf** <note>original: "auri puri foliati"</note>, and one drachm each of **avens** <note>original: "gariofilatæ"; a medicinal herb, though sometimes used to refer to cloves</note> and good **ambergris** <note>original: "ambræ bonæ"</note>. Let these likewise be powdered, and when they have been placed in the cucurbit, let it be closed and placed in the boiler, in the Mary’s bath, for

# PHILIP ULSTAD’S BOOK

...of the best and second [substances], take two scruples with half an ounce of distilled water of an old capon <note>original: "aque caponis veteris"; a common nourishing base in medieval pharmacy, made by distilling the essence of a cooked rooster or chicken</note>. <margin>For leprosy</margin> Ramon Lull, however, says that it ought to be drunk with such a julep: Take syrup of verjuice <note>original: "Syr. agrestæ"; the juice of unripe grapes</note> and syrup of simple vinegar, half an ounce of each; one ounce of bugloss water <note>a plant used to treat "melancholy" and heart ailments</note>; and two ounces of chicory water. Let a julep <term>julep: a sweet medicinal drink, usually used as a vehicle for other drugs</term> be made, and let it be given to the leper with the aforementioned gold.

John of Rupescissa <note>a 14th-century Franciscan alchemist known for his work on the "Quintessence"</note> says: if this potable gold is taken with two ounces of celandine water, it is most beneficial for all ailments, principally for hectic fevers <note>original: "febres ethicas"; persistent fevers often associated with wasting diseases like tuberculosis</note> and dropsy <note>original: "hydropicas"; swelling caused by excess fluid</note>. And note, that if a young man uses this gold, he must be <term>phlebotomized: the practice of bloodletting to balance the body's humors</term> at least twice a year. 

An old man, however, may take one drop every day with five drops of the **Fifth Essence**, while the stomach is still fasting, and let him fast for a full hour after taking it; for it miraculously drives away gray hair. It also strengthens the heart, the brain, and all other principal members.

## ¶ Concerning potable gold made from cordial flowers.
### Chapter Twenty-seven.

We had intended to write nothing further concerning potable gold, but the true love which we hold toward all scholars—whom we know to be eager to read better and more useful things than have been written until now—[moves us]. Therefore, lest we dull their most tender ears with long, winding paths of words, we will teach the true potable gold itself. Indeed, no philosopher, however skilled, can ever find fault with this or say that it is in any way contrary to human nature, because it can be given to a person in even the most prolonged and greatest illness. It is highly approved by Arnold of Villanova and by the said Marsilio Ficino <note>a famous Renaissance philosopher and physician who wrote extensively on longevity</note>. And although it does not require great

...expended, it should not for that reason be considered worthless. And it is made in this way: Take bugloss <note>original: "buglossa"; a plant traditionally used to treat "melancholy" and heart conditions</note> with its flowers, root, herb, and its entire substance on a clear day—provided that there was no rain the preceding night—while the Moon is waxing and in a favorable aspect of Jupiter and Venus <note>astrological timing was considered crucial in medieval pharmacy to ensure the "virtue" or potency of the plant was at its peak</note>. Let it be crushed with its entire substance and digested in horse manure <term>digested in horse manure: the practice of burying a sealed vessel in a pile of fermenting dung to provide a gentle, constant, and natural heat for chemical reactions</term> for eight days. 

Afterward, let it be distilled three or four times, always pouring the distilled liquid back over its own dregs. Then let it be digested again in a Mary’s bath <term>balneo Mariae: a water bath or "bain-marie," used to heat a substance slowly and prevent burning</term> for four or five days. In the same manner, take also borage <note>a plant closely related to bugloss, often used to "gladden the heart"</note> with its entire substance during the same lunar phases as was said of the bugloss, always pulverizing the dregs themselves after distillation and pouring the distilled liquid back over them. And let the same be done with celandine and lemon balm. 

And when you have the distilled water from all of these prepared in the aforementioned shape and manner, let them be mixed together in equal parts and placed in a glass vessel with a long neck, setting it in the Sun. Let these waters be very well fermented until you wish to use them. 

When you wish, therefore, to make potable gold, take one pound of the aforementioned mixed waters and place them in an earthenware vessel, glazed on the inside. Then take a plate of pure gold—not counterfeit—weighing sixteen ounces or more, or as much as you can obtain; however, the more there is, the better. And let the aforementioned plate be heated in the fire until it glows white-hot, then quench it in the aforementioned waters as many times as it takes until a fourth part of the waters is consumed. And this is the opinion of Marsilio Ficino <note>a famous 15th-century Italian physician and philosopher who sought to reconcile Neoplatonism with medicine</note>. Afterward, let it be strained very well through a most clean linen cloth and placed in a very well-sealed glass. From there, take of pearls...

...of the pulverized substances, 4 drachms, over which are poured 6 ounces of aqua vitae <term>aqua vitae: literally "water of life," a high-proof distilled spirit or brandy</term> distilled four times from red wine. Let these be placed in a well-sealed glass vessel and put in a Mary’s bath <term>balneo Mariae: a water bath or "bain-marie," used to heat a substance slowly and evenly</term> for 24 hours. When it has been distilled, you shall keep it stored separately. 

And whenever you wish to help a sick person who has been judged to be near death by doctors: You shall take half an ounce of the water in which a plate of gold has been quenched <note>original: "extincta"; the alchemical process of heating metal until it glows and plunging it into liquid to transfer its "virtue" or spiritual essence to the water</note>, and 1 drachm of the aqua vitae with pearls, and let them be mixed together. Give this to the patient at each dose, and after six hours have passed, give the same amount, and the same again after twelve hours have passed. 

But if this should heat the patient too much, it shall be given to him with 1 drachm of syrup of violets. One might also take up to one and a half ounces of the Quintessence <term>Quinta essentia: the "fifth essence," the most purified and potent spirit of a substance, believed to be incorruptible</term> made from red wine in the manner we taught above, in which should be placed 1 drachm of pure gold leaf—not crushed or pulverized—and the Quintessence will draw the virtue and color of the gold into itself. 

And let these leaves of gold remain in this way with the Quintessence. And when you give the aforementioned waters to the sick person, you shall add three or four drops of this essence. Marsilio <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), an influential Italian priest and scholar whose medical-philosophical works on "The Triple Life" popularized the use of gold and spirits to prolong life</note> also maintains that although a gold plate may have been quenched many times in the aforementioned waters, gold leaf ought nonetheless to be placed in them.

<margin>The benefits of true potable gold.</margin>
Therefore, in place of those gold leaves, you shall take the Quintessence which has already received its power from the gold. And thus you have the truest potable gold <term>aurum potabile: "drinkable gold," a legendary alchemical remedy thought to be a universal cure and a means to achieve longevity</term>, which is better and more precious than if you had spent a thousand ducats searching for something more useful or more expensive. It has the power to comfort the heart, and it consumes all superfluous humors <note>In the medical theory of the time, health depended on the balance of four "humors" or bodily fluids; excess humors were thought to cause disease</note>, generates the best blood, and expels every evil; it preserves youth wonderfully, provokes urine, and [cures] ill...

### On Potable Gold from the Quintessence by means of the Stone. Chapter Twenty-Eight.

If you wish to make potable gold <term>aurum potabile: "drinkable gold," a legendary alchemical medicine believed to cure all ills and grant longevity</term> in another way: Take of the Quintessence distilled by the aforementioned Philosopher’s Stone, in which gold leaves should be placed, and thus let them be dissolved in the said Quintessence, so that from the gold a certain drinkable liquid is made. And this is of miraculous effect for sustaining life, for expelling all diseases, for reviving a man who is half-dead, and for bringing him back to his former health. It will be all the more perfect if you add two parts of the Quintessence of honey, extracted just as we taught above. 

Let these be placed in a circulatory glass vessel <term>vitrum circulatorium: a "pelican" or similar vessel where vapors condense and flow back into the base, allowing for constant redistillation</term>, such that there is one pound of the aforementioned aqua vitae, 10 ounces of the Quintessence of honey, and 4 drachms of gold leaf. Close the top of the circulatory vessel very well and seal it with wax <note>original: "lutetur"; to seal a vessel with "lute" or airtight paste</note> as we taught above. Then let it be placed in a Mary’s bath <term>balneum Mariae: a water bath used for gentle, even heating</term> for four or five months; for the more it is circulated, the better the substance becomes, and the more the gold itself is dissolved and incorporated with the other matter. 

If, however, you cannot obtain gold leaf, you shall take three or four ducats <note>high-purity gold coins</note> and hammer them very thinly, just as is done by goldsmiths when they wish to gild something; yet nonetheless, nothing is better than gold leaf, if it can be obtained from the good gold of ducats. If however—

...and you will find the gold itself at the bottom <note>original: "in acie"; literally the edge or point, here referring to the base of the cone-shaped vessel</note> of the casting vessel, and above this is the antimony itself, separated. And this is the best method of refining gold. And when everything has cooled down, the gold itself should be broken away from the other material at the bottom, and if it has not been sufficiently refined, let it be melted again with antimony, and let it be done just as the first time.

<margin>
Another method of refining the Sun <note>the Sun: alchemical shorthand for gold</note>.
</margin>
You can also refine the Sun by means of such a cement <term>cementation: a dry refining process where gold is heated with reactive chemicals to draw out silver and other base metal impurities</term>. Take Roman vitriol <term>Roman vitriol: iron or copper sulfate</term>, or in its place take reddened saltpeter <term>salis nitri: potassium nitrate, here heat-treated until red</term>, sal ammoniac <term>salis armoniaci: ammonium chloride</term>, verdigris <term>viridis æris: copper acetate</term>, Armenian bole <term>bolus armenus: a type of red medicinal clay</term>, and the powder of well-baked bricks, one ounce of each. Let all these be thoroughly powdered and made into a paste with human urine <note>Urine was commonly used in alchemy as a source of ammonia and phosphates for chemical reactions</note>. Let it be layered upon layer in a crucible <term>tigillum: a heat-resistant pot for melting or heating substances</term>, so that at its bottom there is a layer of the said paste, and another layer on top of florins <note>florins: high-purity gold coins used as a standard for metallurgical work</note>, and again a layer of paste, and another of florins, always proceeding in this way until you have placed all the gold. And at the top of the crucible let there be a layer of paste, upon which another layer of quicklime <term>calce viua: calcium oxide</term> is placed. And know that no florin should touch another. And when everything has been thus placed, let a lid be made for the crucible which has a hole made with a needle in the center, and let the crucible be sealed well on all sides with a lute <term>luto: a sealing clay</term> made from hair <note>Adding hair to the clay lute helped prevent it from cracking under high heat</note>. When this is done, let a charcoal fire be made at a distance of one palm’s breadth, removed from the crucible on all sides, for one full hour. When that has passed, let the fire be moved a little closer for another hour; when that has finished, let it be moved slightly closer again for another hour, so that it touches the crucible itself. Finally, let another fire be made over the crucible, touching it on all sides, and let it also last for a full hour. When the final hour has passed, let the crucible cool down, and break it...

Or, if you wish, you can also refine the Sun by means of such a cement: Take 1 ounce of well-prepared common salt, half an ounce of brick dust, 1 ounce of red vitriol <term>red vitriol: iron(III) sulfate, which releases acidic vapors when heated</term>, and 6 drams of sublimated mercury <term>sublimated mercury: mercuric chloride, a powerful corrosive agent</term>. *Another cement.*
</margin>
Then let the gold be melted, and over it pour 1 dram of sal ammoniac <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, used as a flux to clean the surface of molten metal</term> and half a dram of sublimated mercury; let these be placed little by little into the crucible <term>tigillum: a small, heat-resistant pot used for melting metals</term> where the gold is melted. There is also another cement, which is made in this way: Take 1 ounce of sal ammoniac, 1 ounce of verdigris <term>verdigris: copper acetate, a green substance formed on weathered copper</term>, and 6 drams of well-calcined <term>calcined: heated to high temperatures to remove moisture or oxidize the substance</term> vitriol. Let a dry powder be made, with which a layer upon layer is created; and in this last way, when you refine gold, from four parts of one florin <note>florin: a common gold coin of the period; the text suggests a 25% loss of weight as impurities are removed</note>, only three parts will remain.

But if you desire to separate and refine the Sun <note>gold</note> or the Moon <note>silver</note> from antimony, let a little tartar <term>tartar: potassium bitartrate, a byproduct of winemaking used here as a reducing agent</term> be added, and the antimony itself will not be able to touch the Sun or Moon. At that time, however, the Moon <note>silver</note> should be mixed with Venus <note>Venus: the alchemical term for copper</note>.

The method of making a powder from the Sun itself is as follows: Take ducats <note>high-purity gold coins</note> or florins prepared in the ways mentioned above and place them in the fire until they glow white-hot; then let them be hammered as thinly as you can, just as is done for gold leaf, afterwards let them be cut into pie-

Therefore, let this instrument be heated, and in its bottom let a layer of liquid lead be made, one finger thick, upon which another layer of tartar <term>tartar: potassium bitartrate, a byproduct of winemaking used as a flux in alchemy</term> should be made. Afterward, let a ducat or florin <note>gold coins of the period</note> be placed on top, and let it be set upon the cross, placing it in such a furnace. This should be done in such a way that the lead remains always liquid. And when the gold has been on one side for a little while, let it be turned over to the other side; and in this way, it will become ready <note>original: "obediens" - compliant or workable</note> for making **Solar powder** <term>Solar powder: powdered gold, named for the Sun, which is the alchemical symbol for gold</term>. Afterward, let it be calcined <term>calcined: reduced to powder through intense heat</term> in a reverberatory furnace, as has already been said. And in this way, you can treat four or five florins, and other silver coins.

### On Potable Gold with Honey. Chapter Twenty-Nine.

Take of well-prepared salt gem <term>salt gem: "sal gemmae," or pure rock salt</term>, the best honey extracted from the honeycomb without fire (as we said above), and rectified water of life <term>water of life: "aqua vitae," or highly distilled alcohol</term>, of each equal parts. Let them all be distilled together three times. Afterward, take one pound of this distilled material and as much of the finest gold leaf as you wish, and put it into a cucurbit, placing it over ashes <note>a sand or ash bath used to provide even, indirect heat</note>. Then add of choice rhubarb, well-ground wood of aloes <note>agarwood</note>, rhapontic <note>original: "reupontici," a root similar to rhubarb used medicinally</note>, the bone from a stag’s heart <note>a traditional medicinal ingredient—actually a hardened piece of cartilage—believed to strengthen the human heart</note>, fresh Alexandrian musk, and good ambergris <note>original: "ambra"</note>, of each one dram. Let all be mixed together, and after eight days have passed, filter it <note>original: "distilla per filtrum" - literally to distill through a filter, meaning to strain or clarify</note>, and you will have the best **potable gold**, conducive to many ailments and wonderfully restoring the human body.

at half the weight of the gold, and let it be circulated as is proper.

of a lemon-yellow or saffron color, such that it even dyes 
human hair. And in this way, you also have true potable gold.

Therefore, it is necessary to dissolve it again in the Mary’s bath, then calcine it, until you can have a true indication that it can be dissolved no more.

# THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTAD

<margin>The dosage of this potable gold.</margin> 
Take care, therefore, that it is not given to a healthy or sick person unless necessity itself demands it, such that in three months you shall give at least three drops; for if you were to give it more often, the soul itself would take such delight and joy in it that it might even separate from the body <note>Alchemists believed that the "spiritual" nature of Potable Gold was so intense it could entice the soul to leave its "corrupt" physical shell.</note>. When you give it to someone in the summer, it shall be given with pure spring water. In winter, however, it should be given with capon broth or with wine. 

If, however, you wish to make small stones or Crystals in a shorter way, let the **phlegm** <term>phlegm: the watery, inactive distillate that remains after the "spirit" has been extracted</term> that remained when you first distilled the water be distilled. This must be placed in another cucurbit, again pouring the phlegm over it and distilling just as the first time. Let this be done again so that you can extract that matter, which must be placed in a boiler; let it remain there until it is turned again into a thin substance, just as it was in the beginning. ## ¶ Concerning another excellent potable gold. Chapter 34.

There are those who distill and draw out the **water of life** <note>original: "aquam vitæ" — high-proof distilled alcohol or wine, seen as the life-force of the substance</note> from the "earth" of the best wine they can obtain, so that the **phlegmatic matter** <note>original: "materia phlegmatica" — the watery, tasteless, and medically inert residue left after distillation</note> remains at the bottom. Therefore, lest you be deceived in this: first you shall distill half of the portion, and the other half is like clear spring water, at which you should not wonder; and this ought to be distilled again with a slow fire, then let it be set aside separately. But the thick matter, which remained at the bottom, you should cause to boil over the fire until it is dried out. You can also extract from this a **black oil**, dense and thick, which is hot, and this also ought to be very well guarded; then you shall **calcine** <term>calcination</term> <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the air to reduce it to ashes or "earth," often used for purification</note> the black matter itself in a **reverberatory furnace** <term>reverberatory furnace</term> <note>a furnace designed to reflect or "reverberate" the heat from the roof onto the material being treated, preventing direct contact with the fuel</note>, and for one part of the "earth" <note>the solid residue left after heating</note> you shall take five parts of the aforementioned water of life, which ought to be sweet, pouring it over, and so let it remain for fifteen days until it is dissolved. Afterward let it be filtered through a cloth, discarding the dregs, distilling the water again, and calcining it for four or five days. Which being done, you will find a **white earth**. See therefore that you work this cautiously, so that you may have a good portion of that earth, and you will be able to whiten and cleanse it through the heat of fire and the moisture of water. And thus you can understand that the water itself is worth nothing except for cleansing, and therefore it ought to have no **spirit**. It is indeed true that in the water of life itself there is fire and spirit, but it has in itself a watery quality (which is the **phlegm**) and an earthy quality, which is the very impediment to **perfection**, <note>original catchword: "ctionis," completing "perfectionis"</note>

### Concerning Potable Gold with Gems. Chapter Thirty-Four.

### Concerning another good potable gold. Chapter 36.

Take two ounces of simple *aqua vitae* and two drachmas of borax of Pera <note>original: "boracis de pera"; likely referring to borax imported through Pera, the Genoese/Venetian trading quarter of Constantinople</note>. Dissolve the borax in the said *aqua vitae*. Then, dissolve one ounce of white sugar in two and a half ounces of *aqua vitae*. Also dissolve half an ounce of camphor in two and a half ounces of *aqua vitae*. 

Finally, let leaves of the purest gold <note>gold leaf</note> be taken, which should also be dissolved in the said *aqua vitae*. Let these four portions of *aqua vitae* be placed into a cucurbit <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel used as the bottom part of a still</note>, closing it very tightly, and let it rest thus for one day. 

Afterward, by placing an alembic <note>the "head" or cap of the still that catches the vapors</note> on top, distill it until half has passed over. Then, let the cucurbit be placed in a damp place, and those four materials will be converted into a very white and bright crystal. Afterward, dissolve the following things in the water distilled from this crystal: one drachma each of good ambergris and fresh fine musk, and one ounce of the best pearls. Let these be placed in a Mary’s Bath <note>original: "balneū Mariae"; a gentle water bath or double-boiler named after Mary the Jewess, an early alchemist</note>,

->**Concerning another potable gold with strong water.**<-
->**Chapter Thirty-seven.**<-

Take the best gold that you can obtain, which must be dissolved in strong water <note>original: "aqua forti"; nitric acid, a powerful solvent capable of dissolving most metals</note>, and then let the water be evaporated and drawn off through an alembic <note>a distilling apparatus consisting of a gourd-like vessel and a cap with a long neck</note>; however, it should not be dried out entirely, because it should be like an oil. 

Afterward, let honey water <note>original: "aqua mellis"; a distilled solution of honey</note> be poured over it, which should also be distilled through an alembic, so that it rises four fingers’ breadth above the matter itself. Let it be set to digest for four days and as many nights in a Mary’s bath <note>original: "balneo Mariae"; a water bath used to provide gentle, uniform heat, similar to a modern bain-marie</note>. Afterward, let the honey water be drawn off again through the alembic, then you shall add other honey water and digest it as before, and in all things you shall do just as you did the first time. 

And know that it remains like a thin oil, since a little of that honey water remains behind afterward. Then you shall draw off the honey water as many times as necessary until no saltiness remains from the substance of the strong water in which the gold was dissolved; and when you perceive a bitterness on the tongue through the taste of the honey, take *aqua vitae* <note>original: "aquam vitae"; "water of life," referring to highly concentrated distilled alcohol</note> that has been distilled twelve times, in which there is no phlegm <note>original: "phlegma"; the watery, inactive part of a distillate</note>. 

In this you shall dissolve the gold, so that the liquid rises four fingers above the gold; then let the distilling vessel in which you have placed it be well sealed with strong lute, and let it be placed in a Mary’s bath until it is dissolved. However, this can be done more quickly in horse dung <note>original: "fimo equino"; alchemists often buried vessels in fermenting manure to maintain a low, steady "natural" heat for long periods</note>, and it is more natural than the other method, provided that a circulator <note>original: "infixatorium"; a vessel used for circulatory distillation</note> or a "pelican" <note>original: "pellicanum"; a specialized alchemical vessel with two side-arms that allow the distillate to flow back into the main body, creating a continuous cycle</note> is used. Then let the *aqua vitae* be drawn off from the gold through an alembic, so that the gold remains at the bottom of the glass like a certain oil or butter; and this is the potable gold itself.

## Concerning another potable gold. Chapter
## Thirty-eighth.

Take pure gold leaf, let it be ground with prepared common salt, and when it has been ground, add a little more salt again, and let them be mixed well together, and let them be calcined <note>original: "calcinentur"; the process of heating a substance to a high temperature, but below its melting point, to make it friable or to change its chemical state</note> in a reverberatory furnace <note>original: "furno reverberationis"; a furnace that reflects or "reverberates" heat from the roof onto the material being treated</note> for four or five hours, making a good flame of fire; then let it be allowed to cool. Afterward, let it be washed with warm fresh water. Afterward, let it be placed in the following water to digest <note>original: "digerendum"; a process of slow, gentle heating to encourage chemical change</note> for nine days. After this, let the water itself be drawn off from the gold, and let it be poured over again, and digested, and drawn off again; and let this always be done in this way two or three times with a slow fire. Afterward, let pure spring water be poured over it, and drawn off again. But take care that the gold is not dried out too much, because in solution it would be too harsh, and as it were, resistant to dissolving. Once it has been completely dissolved, wash it again with fresh water, and grind it with **mastic** <note>a resin obtained from the mastic tree, used as a binder</note>, and do everything as described above. And if it has any blackness or impurity, it ought to be washed with distilled vinegar. <margin>The dissolution of the Sun.</margin>The dissolution of the Sun is performed in this way: Take half an ounce of good, pure gold and three ounces of well-washed and purified **Mercury** <note>quicksilver</note>; let an **amalgam** <note>a blend of mercury and another metal</note> be made. Afterward, let the Mercury be separated from the gold over a **cupel** <term>cinericium</term> <note>a porous vessel made of bone ash used by goldsmiths to separate precious metals</note>, as is done by goldsmiths; the gold will remain like a kind of lemon-yellow powder. Afterward, draw off all moisture with the heat of a "fire of the first degree" <note>the gentlest level of heat</note>, and it will be ready. 

Then take that gold so prepared and place it in a clean glass **cucurbit** <term>cucurbitã</term> <note>the lower, gourd-shaped vessel of a still</note>, attaching an **alembic** <note>the "cap" of the still</note> with a spout, and let everything be sealed very well with **lute** <note>a paste used to make the glass joints airtight</note>. When this is done, open the cucurbit, and if anything remains in it, do everything as described above; you shall do this until nothing remains in the glass. ### ¶ On volatile potable gold. Chapter Thirty-Nine.

Volatile potable gold <note>original: "aurum potabile volatile"; an alchemical elixir where gold is supposedly rendered into a liquid, vaporous state for medicinal use</note>, which is useful for many things, is made in this way. Take the best wine you can obtain, as much as you wish, and let it be placed in a glass vessel with a long neck. Let it be sealed, as is customary, with the **Seal of Hermes** <note>original: "sigillo Hermetis"; an airtight, hermetic seal created by melting the glass neck of a vessel shut</note>. Afterward, let it **putrefy** <note>original: "putrefiat"; in alchemy, this refers to a slow digestion or decomposition through gentle, sustained heat</note>, and let it be very carefully guarded so that it does not grow cold. You shall allow it to rest until it acquires a sediment, just as vinegar does, and this sediment is lemon-yellow. Leave it, therefore, to stand longer until the sediment all recedes, and it will have white veins as if they were little flames of fire; this commonly happens in the space of three months. 

Then let it be placed in a **cucurbit** <term>cucurbita</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel used for distillation</note> that is very well closed, and let it be distilled with a fire made from dry wood in lukewarm water, as is done in a **water bath** <term>balneo Mariæ</term> <note>literally "Mary’s Bath"; a method of gentle heating by placing the vessel in a container of hot water to prevent scorching</note>. After this, take two pounds of this distilled material and place within it fifteen leaves of the purest gold. Let it boil in a water bath for twelve continuous hours, and let the glass be very well blocked with the Seal of Hermes, as was said above. Then, let it be distilled over a very slow fire—so slow that the fire is not even at the first degree—and once this is done, when the whole matter has been distilled, let it be reserved. This is called **volatile potable gold**.

### ¶ On another potable gold. Chapter Forty.

Potable gold is also made in this way: Take thick red wine and let it be distilled four times. Afterward, take **calcined tartar** <note>original: "tartaro calcinato"; potassium carbonate produced by heating the crusty deposits from wine barrels to a high temperature</note>, pour the wine over it, and let it putrefy for twenty-four hours. Then, let all the moisture be drawn off over a very slow fire, and you will see the tartar itself, which was previously white, [turn] into blackness...

->¶ On another most precious potable gold.<-

### Chapter Forty.

There are indeed more recent philosophers who make potable gold in this way: Take as much red or white wine as you wish; let it be distilled seven times through ashes. Then gather all the dregs <note>original: "feces"; the solid residue left after distillation</note> and distill, straining the water itself until it reaches the thickness of honey; then pour the phlegm <note>original: "phlegma"; in alchemy, the watery distillate that lacks the potency of the "spirit"</note> over it, and let it rest thus for three days and as many nights, and all the little stones <note>original: "lapilli"; likely referring to tartaric acid crystals or salts precipitated from the wine</note> will settle to the bottom. Once this is done, you shall wash the aforementioned stones with the phlegm until they are purified; then let them be dried and placed in a **cucurbit** <term>cucurbita</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel used for distillation</note>, placing a blind **alembic** <note>original: "alembicum caecum"; a distillation head without a spout, used to force vapors to circulate back down rather than exit the vessel</note> on top, and let it be put in a water bath <term>balneum Mariae</term> for three days and as many nights, pouring over it some **water of life** <term>aqua vitae</term> <note>highly concentrated alcohol</note> to the height of three fingers. Afterward, let it be strained little by little, so that the wine does not become cloudy, and do this until the wine takes no color from those stones. Finally, let all the wine be drawn off from the stones so that they remain entirely dry in the vessel. When this is done, the oil itself should be taken away through sand with a strong fire <note>a "sand bath" used to achieve higher temperatures than a water bath</note>,

## ¶ On vegetable potable gold. Chapter 42.

There is also another **potable gold** <note>original: "aurum potabile"; a medicinal liquid gold thought to grant long life and health</note>, which wonderfully strengthens the superfluous humidity in man, and it is made in this way: Take of the **Fifth Essence** <term>Quintessence</term> <note>the most purified part of a substance, representing its heavenly or essential power</note> of wine itself, extracted as we taught above, in which gold can be dissolved while preserving its own power. Let this be done subtly by way of continuity within the humidity of a water bath <term>balneum</term>, and let the water itself be distilled with a slow fire, separating all the humidity from it; in this way, the substance of the gold will remain entirely dry in the bottom of the still. 

After this, take some of that wine and let it be distilled through an **alembic** <term>alembicum</term> <note>a distillation apparatus</note> so many times until it can no longer be burned due to the reduction of its **sulfur** <note>original: "sulphur"; the principle of combustibility; here, the chemist is stripping the alcohol of its flammable parts</note>. Always receive each distillation in a new receptacle, and take the second water so many times until no "vein" <note>original: "vena"; the oily streaks or "legs" visible on the glass during the distillation of spirits</note> appears in the alembic itself. Into this water, you shall throw the substance of the gold, and very quickly it will be dissolved into a vegetable water. This occurs because **Mercury** is rectified by **Mercury** from the **phlegm** <note>original: "phlegmate"; the watery, lifeless residue left after the spirit has been removed</note> itself, until you see...

<header>THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTADT. 80</header>

<note>original: "deris"; likely a fragment of "videris", meaning "you see"</note> ...until you see that it cannot be burnt any further. Since this is the case, you will mix it with the first substance of gold, and then it is true **water of life** <term>aqua vitae</term> <note>concentrated alcohol or a distilled spirit</note> or **potable gold** <term>aurum potabile</term>. This is the first water, serving and benefiting the human body.

<margin>Water 1.</margin> Take some water of life, and from it you will separate all moisture through distillation from the **Fifth Essence** <term>Quintessence</term> of the water, which is pure gold <note>original: "aurú purú"; the author identifies the distilled essence of the gold itself as the "Quintessence"</note>. Keep this separate, and into that same vegetable water place three parts of honey with its honeycomb. Let all of this be put together to ferment <note>original: "putrefiendum"; literally "to rot," but in alchemy this refers to the breakdown of substances through gentle heat to release their essence</note> in a **water bath** <term>balneum Mariae</term> <note>a "Mary's bath" or double boiler, named after Mary the Jewess, an ancient alchemist</note> for four days. This digestion must be performed nine times in the bath, or in manure <note>original: "fimo"; horse manure was frequently used by alchemists to provide a steady, low, and natural heat for long-term fermentation</note>, because through this the water is rectified <note>purified or improved in quality</note>.

<margin>2</margin> The second water is this: Take an old capon or a hen, and when all the bones and unnecessary parts have been removed, the meat itself must be finely ground in a stone mortar. Then you shall place it in a gourd <term>cucurbita</term> <note>the lower vessel of a still</note> with an alembic <note>the still-head used for cooling vapors</note>, and you will distill the water from it in a water bath. This water must again be kept separately.

<margin>3</margin> The third water is this: Take the meat of a hen or capon and let it be distilled over ashes <note>original: "per cineres"; a method of indirect heating where the glass vessel sits in a bowl of warm ash to prevent cracking</note>, drawing off all the moisture with a slow fire. Take care that the meat does not burn in the alembic, and you shall also keep this separately.

<margin>4</margin> The fourth water is this: Take three parts of wine and place them over one part of the aforementioned meat. Put it in a gourd, closing it very well with a glass lid and sealing it with paste <note>original: "lutando"; using "lute," a clay or paste used to make airtight seals on alchemical apparatus</note>. Then let it be placed over ashes with a slow fire for three days. Afterward, let it be distilled through an alembic, and you shall reserve the water.

<margin>5</margin> The fifth water is this: Take the whole substance of a capon or hen, and separate the entire liquid substance from it using an alembic, just as was said before, and you shall reserve this water also.

6 **The sixth water** is this. Take all the bones of a capon or a hen; let them be ground into small pieces in a stone mortar with a wooden pestle, and let them be placed in a **water bath** <term>balneum Mariae</term> <note>a double boiler used for gentle, even heating</note>, and afterward truly over the ashes <note>placing the vessel directly in a bed of hot ashes for higher heat</note>, and you shall distill it just as was said before; and this too must be guarded.

7 **The seventh water** is this. Take some of the third water, the fifth water, and the sixth water; let them be distilled together through an **alembic** <term>alembicum</term> <note>a chemical apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used for distilling volatile substances</note>, and let them be very well guarded.

The **rectification** <note>purification or refinement</note> of the aforementioned waters, which are distilled through the ashes, is this: Take the gold prepared in the way that was most recently described, and let it be coagulated—for it is a certain natural moisture—in every way and by heat, as if it were a yellow **orpiment** <note>original: "auri pigmentum citrinum"; a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral often compared to the color of gold</note>. Add half a part of its first water, and immediately it will be dissolved by that water and by the three aforementioned waters.

Therefore, take a quantity the size of one silver spoon and let it be very well mixed with a good portion of the best wine you can obtain. Give this to a **phlegmatic** <term>phlegmatico</term> person <note>According to the Four Humors theory, a person with an excess of phlegm—characterized as cold and moist</note> during the winter time, and it will preserve them in health in a wonderful way.

If, however, the man is **choleric** <term>colericus</term> <note>a person with an excess of yellow bile—characterized as hot and dry</note>, give it to him with pure water. To a **melancholic** <term>melancolico</term> person <note>one with an excess of black bile—characterized as cold and dry</note>, give it with a broth of cabbage <note>original: "iure caulium"</note>. To a **sanguine** <term>sanguineo</term> person <note>one with an excess of blood—characterized as hot and moist</note>, however, you shall not give this water, but you shall give him that which follows.

Take one spoonful of the **golden water**, let it be placed in wine and given to him, and he shall be safe from every infirmity. In the summer, however, it ought to be administered to the phlegmatic person with chicken broth in which **parsley** <term>petrosilinum</term> has been cooked; but for the choleric person, with another broth. You shall give it to the melancholic person when the time demands it, during a great operation of heat or cold. If, however, you wish to give it to a sick person, you shall give a second part of the dissolved gold in the second water of the capon, if he is phlegmatic...

### Rectification of the Waters
The rectification of the aforementioned waters, which have been distilled through ashes, is this: Take gold prepared in the way that was last described, and let it be coagulated <note>thickened or solidified</note>, because it is a certain natural moisture. This is done in every way and with heat, as if it were <term>yellow orpiment</term> <note>original: "auri pigmentum citrinum"; orpiment is a yellow mineral (arsenic trisulfide) used as a pigment, here used to describe the appearance of the gold preparation</note>. Add half a part of its "first water," and it will immediately be dissolved by that water and by the three aforementioned waters. 

Take, therefore, a quantity the size of one silver spoonful and let it be mixed very well with a good portion of the best wine you can obtain. Give this to a **phlegmatic** person <note>someone with a constitution dominated by phlegm, considered cold and moist</note> during the winter time, and it will preserve them in health in a wonderful way. If, however, the person is **choleric** <note>dominated by yellow bile; hot and dry</note>, give it to them with pure water. To a **melancholic** person <note>dominated by black bile; cold and dry</note>, give it with cabbage broth. You shall not give this water to a **sanguine** person <note>dominated by blood; hot and moist</note>, but instead you shall give them what follows: 

Take one spoonful of the "golden water," place it in wine, and give it to them; they shall be safe from every infirmity. In the summer, however, it should be administered to a **phlegmatic** person with hen broth in which <term>parsley</term> <note>original: "petrosilinum"</note> has been cooked. To a **choleric** person, give it with another broth. To a **melancholic** person, you shall give it when the time demands, during a great operation of either heat or cold.
