Give the patient half a spoonful with two parts of water; the same dosage should be given to a person of a **sanguine** temperament. For someone of a **choleric** temperament, give two spoonfuls of the "third water," and do the same for a **melancholic** person. Within three days, they will be cured of every ailment, no matter how severe or dangerous it may be. 

When you wish to administer this medicine with the aforementioned waters, ask the patient which food they enjoy most. Even if that food is usually considered harmful to their condition, give it to them anyway, along with a dose of this medicine the size of a pea or a large lentil. This medicine is effective against every illness, whether natural or accidental, and whether characterized by heat or cold <note>In the medical theory of the time, illnesses were categorized as "hot," "cold," "moist," or "dry" depending on which bodily humor was out of balance.</note>. 

If you intend to travel and wish to take the medicine with you, it should be thickened <note>original: "coagulari"</note> and stored in a glass vessel. When you are ready to use it, it should be prepared in the liquid of **potable gold** <note>original: "auri potabilis"; a legendary alchemical "liquid gold" believed to be a universal cure and life-extender</note>. 

Once you have prepared the vegetable substances <note>original: "vegetabilia"; in alchemy, this refers to substances derived from plants, often used to create the "Vegetable Stone"</note> as described earlier, take one and place gold leaves into a glass vessel. Seal the glass very well to prevent any evaporation and place it in hot ashes over a low fire. The gold will dissolve within twenty-two days. 

If you wish to make **potable gold**, draw off all the watery substance from the gold using a low fire. Place the gold remaining at the bottom into a <term>cucurbit</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation</note> and heat it in a <term>Mary’s bath</term> <note>original: "balneum Mariæ"; a vessel of hot water used to provide gentle, even heat</note>. It will dissolve over four natural days, resulting in true potable gold. This should then be placed in a small cucurbit. To half an ounce of gold leaf, add fifty [units] of mercury washed with salt and vinegar. In eight days, the entire mixture will be converted into true gold. 

If you wish to further <term>sublime</term> <note>original: "sublimare"; to heat a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again as a purified powder</note> this mixture, take the potable gold and add mercury that has been sublimated seven times with <term>vitriol</term> <note>a sulfate mineral used as a powerful corrosive or solvent</note> and

->On the Final Potable Gold. Chapter<-
->Forty-Third.<-

**T**ake half an ounce of refined gold and grind it very finely, as is done when gold is prepared for gilding. Cut it into the smallest possible pieces and add purified Mercury <term>Mercury: quicksilver, considered by alchemists to be the "mother" of all metals</term> to create an amalgam <term>amalgam: a soft paste made by mixing mercury with another metal</term>. Place this on a marble stone and grind it with as much sulfur as is necessary—or better yet, a weight of sulfur equal to the weight of the amalgam. When it has been sufficiently ground, place it over glowing coals in a crucible <term>crucible: a ceramic or metal container used for melting substances at very high temperatures</term>, and it will be consumed by the fire immediately. Once this is done, grind it again upon the marble stone and return it to a small crucible, placing it in the fire until it glows red. Afterward, remove the crucible, and you will find your gold perfectly calcined <term>calcination: the process of reducing a metal to a fine powder through high heat</term> into a most subtle powder, appearing just like the powder of saffron. 

Next, place it in a reverberatory furnace <term>reverberatory furnace: a furnace that directs or "reflects" the flame and heat down onto the material</term> and apply the flame as is proper, and it will be ready. Afterward, wash it with "water of life" <note>original: "aqua vitae"; high-proof distilled alcohol used as a solvent</note> that has been distilled four times, and then let it dry. Take as much of this powder as you wish and pour over it enough rectified water of life so that the liquid sits three fingers' height above the powder. Then, place it in a circulatory vessel <term>circulatorium: a glass vessel designed so that evaporating vapors condense and flow back down, "circulating" the substance</term> and set it in a Mary’s bath <term>balneum Mariae: a water bath, known today as a bain-marie, used for gentle, even heating</term> with half its stones <note>The "stones" likely refer to pebbles used to stabilize the vessel or distribute heat within the bath</note>. The gold itself will then dissolve into a saffron-colored water, which is so potent it will even stain human skin a red color. From this, you shall extract all the elements as described previously; let each element be guarded carefully from the air, because...

...[they] possess a power that is impossible to describe, and if it were possible, not a hundredth part of the unlearned would believe it. For the aqueous substance is effective against all infirmities arising from heat or cold.

<margin>The power of water</margin>
It is also effective for any ailment of the chest, heart, or lungs; it expels all poison and casts out all superfluous matter from the lungs. If there were an internal abscess <term>abscess: a localized collection of pus; the original Latin uses "apostema"</term>, it is easily cured by this; it purges the blood, sharpens the sight, and preserves all the spiritual organs <term>spiritual organs: in the Renaissance, these were the organs responsible for the "vital spirits," primarily the heart and lungs</term> from decay.

<margin>The power of oil</margin>
The oil is especially beneficial for young people, for it preserves their strength and beauty when used in food. It prevents the blood from rotting and does not allow phlegm to dominate, nor choler to burn <term>choler: yellow bile, one of the four humors; "burning" it was thought to cause illness or "adust" humors</term>, nor melancholy to take hold; it increases both blood and seed <term>seed: referring to reproductive fluids or "sperma"</term>. Therefore, those who use this oil should more frequently undergo bloodletting <term>bloodletting: the surgical opening of a vein to balance the body's humors</term>. If any limbs are injured or weakened, the oil restores them; it even recovers lost sight if you place one drop in the eye every night for a month.

<margin>The efficacy of fire</margin>
The element of fire is effective for everything mentioned above. It makes the old young again and brings the dead back to life. The elderly should use this with a small amount of "golden water" <note>original: "aquæ aureæ"</note>; it removes all the ailments of old age, and for this reason, it is called the Elixir of Life <term>Elixir of Life: a legendary potion supposed to grant eternal life or youth</term>. Let the fire be dissolved in "water of life" <term>water of life: "aqua vitae," usually distilled alcohol used as a solvent in alchemy</term> until it turns red. Then, let it be placed over "fixed Mercury" <term>fixed Mercury: mercury that has been treated so it no longer evaporates when heated</term> on a slow fire, and it will dissolve very quickly. Afterward, add a third part of gold, and it will immediately be transformed into a solid body, which can be hardened with a little alum. Once this is done, you will see it turn into a red stone; if it is revived again in moisture and arsenic, it will become a perfect elixir to be used upon Mercury itself. As for the element of earth, if it is well purified as it should be,

### ¶ On composite water of life for people of a cold constitution or region. Chapter 44.

We have spoken more than enough in the previous chapters concerning simple **water of life** <note>original: "aqua vitae"; high-proof distilled alcohol used as a medicinal base</note>. It remains now to clarify something about the composite version, even if it is not strictly necessary. But because the learned reader is wonderfully moved by variety, it seems not unwise to discuss the composite, for which we will describe various ways of compounding from various sources. First, therefore, we must consider that which ought to be administered to people of a **cold constitution** <term>cold constitution: in medieval medicine, a temperament thought to be phlegmatic or melancholic, requiring "hot" medicines for balance</term>, or to those suffering from a "cold" illness. You can also use it in cold weather and regions, and it is this: Take of ginger, choice white cinnamon, fresh cubebs <note>cubebs: a type of pepper with a stalk, often used in medieval cooking and medicine</note>, cleaned cloves, nutmeg, choice mace, cardamom, **zedoary** <term>zedoary: a root related to ginger, prized for its aromatic and medicinal properties</term>, **galangal** <term>galangal: a sharp, peppery rhizome common in early pharmacy</term>, and long pepper—equal parts of each. Let them all be crushed coarsely; pour six parts of simple water of life over one part of the spices; place them in a long **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit: the lower, gourd-shaped vessel of a distillation apparatus</term>, placing a **blind alembic** <term>blind alembic: a distillation lid without a spout, used for "digesting" or circulating vapors back into the liquid</term> on top...

...with [it], and let it be put to digest for fourteen days. Afterward let it be distilled in a Mary’s bath <note>original: "balneo Mariae"; a vessel of water used to heat another container gently, known today as a double boiler</note> with a very slow fire; let the dregs <term>feces: the solid residue or sediment left over after distillation</term> be poured again over the distilled matter and digested again for eight days. And when it is distilled three times, the first water is called **blessed water** <note>original: "aqua benedicta"</note>. The second, **composite water of life** <note>original: "aqua vitæ composita"</note>. The third indeed is **balsam water** <note>original: "aqua balsami"</note>.

Some also add to its composition leaves of sage, rue, fresh castor <note>castoreum: a substance secreted by beavers, historically used to treat hysteria and skin conditions</note>, lemon peel, laurel berries, lavender flowers, and rosemary flowers—three drachms <term>drachma: a traditional unit of apothecary weight, approximately 3.9 grams</term> of each. All these ought to be distilled with the spices described above, and always pour six parts of simple water of life over one part of the spices. 

Therefore, this water of life is good for all ailments of the head proceeding from a phlegmatic humor <note>original: "humore phlematico"; in ancient medicine, one of the four body fluids, characterized as cold and moist and thought to cause sluggishness or congestion</note>, provided it is taken in the morning at a weight of two drachms with one ounce <term>uncia: an ounce</term> of the best wine. Some, however, dip a piece of bread into this water; others indeed anoint the head with half an ounce of this water, adding one ounce of wood betony water <note>original: "aquæ bethonicæ"; a common herbal remedy used for various head and nerve pains</note>. But take care not to use it for ailments and pains of the head proceeding from heat <note>heat: referring to "choler" or "sanguine" imbalances which are hot in nature, as opposed to "phlegm" which is cold</note>, unless another cold medicine is mixed in which can temper the heat of this water.

It is also effective for **mania**: while you shear the hair from the head, then moisten cloths in this water, mixed with waters of marjoram and rosemary, and wrap the head, and you will feel wonder[ful results]...

## On the composite water of life of Frederick III,
### Chapter Forty-Five.

Take four pounds of simple rectified water of life <note>aqua vitae: concentrated distilled alcohol</note>, four pounds of malmsey wine <note>a sweet, high-alcohol Mediterranean wine</note>, three ounces of choice cinnamon, one ounce of cloves, one and a half ounces of white ginger, one ounce of nutmeg, half an ounce of mace, half an ounce of zedoary <note>a root similar to ginger</note>, two drams of galangal, half an ounce each of cubebs and hyssop, one ounce of holy root <note>radicis benedictæ: likely wood avens, used as a tonic</note>, half an ounce each of sage and lavender flowers, one ounce each of lemon balm, iris, and costmary <note>balshamithae: an aromatic herb often used in brewing and medicine</note>, and one and a half ounces of white roses. Let all these be crushed and placed in a large cucurbit <term>cucurbit: a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation</term> that can hold fifteen or sixteen pounds, adding three ounces of white sugar, six ounces each of raisins and fat figs, half an ounce of camphor, and two pounds each of rose water, endive water, and elderflower water. Let all be put into the cucurbit, sealing it very well with lute <term>lutando: applying a paste (lute) to seal the vessel joints to prevent the escape of vapors</term>, and let them be placed in the sun for twenty days—specifically ten days before the feast of Saint John <note>June 24th, traditionally associated with the peak of solar power and herb gathering</note>, and ten days after his feast. Then the water should be strained and distilled...

### ¶ On another composite water of life. Chapter Forty-Six.

Take twelve ounces of sage, four drams each of nutmeg, cloves, white ginger, grains of paradise <note>original: "granorum paradisi"; a pungent West African spice related to ginger, highly prized in medieval pharmacy</note>, and cinnamon; one ounce of laurel oil; one dram of fresh castoreum <term>castoreum: a yellowish secretion from the scent glands of the beaver, historically used to treat headaches and hysteria</term>; half a dram each of Indian spikenard <note>original: "spicæ indicæ"; an aromatic essential oil from the Himalayas</note> and rosemary; one dram of rosemary flowers; one ounce of rue leaves; one dram of marjoram leaves; and two drams of bark. All these should be fresh. If, however, they cannot be obtained fresh, let the old ones be powdered, pouring over them the best white wine you can find.

Then let them be placed in a digestion vessel to putrefy for a month. This digestion should take place in a Mary’s bath <term>balneum Mariæ: a "bain-marie" or water bath, used to provide gentle, even heat by surrounding the vessel with warm water</term>, heated to the first degree of heat. Afterward, let them be distilled through an alembic <note>a chemical still consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</note> in a Mary’s bath. Once they have been distilled, pour the liquid back over the dregs <note>original: "feces"; in an alchemical context, this refers to the solid residue or sediment remaining after distillation, not waste</note>. Let them be distilled a third time over ashes, and store it very well in a tightly closed glass vessel.

It has the following virtues: any food moistened with it retains its flavor and smell. It makes the wine in which it is placed flavorful, drives out pestilential air <note>referring to the "miasma" or foul air believed to carry the plague</note>, heals all ailments and defects of the eyes, and all wounds washed with it are cured; it dries up dropsy <term>dropsy: an old term for edema, or painful swelling caused by excess fluid in the body's tissues</term>. It is also effective against all pains of the lungs, the spleen, the liver, and the intestines, as well as the head. It removes all spots from the face, and takes away the stench of the mouth and nostrils, and the pain...

It soothes the pain of the teeth, procures good digestion, acts as a laxative, expels corrupt blood, and wonderfully strengthens the memory. It even restores youthfulness and takes away all sadness. It is also effective against jaundice <note>original: "ictericia"</note>. It relaxes the intestines <note>original: "ilia"</note> when they are too tightly constricted, tints copper into a silver color, cures all trembling of the limbs, removes stammering, takes away hollowed teeth, and expels worms from the belly. It removes leprosy, provided it is not long-standing, and heals every swelling of the throat and neck. And it possesses all the virtues of natural balsam, because it preserves a person in health in a wonderful way. However, those who are of a hot nature [humoral temperament] should not use it, but it is very good for the phlegmatic <term>phlegmatic: a person whose temperament is dominated by the humor of phlegm, characterized as cold and moist</term>.

...burnet, feverfew, angelica, and birthwort, 4 drams of each; of prepared Armenian bole and sealed earth <term>terra sigillata: "sealed earth," a medicinal clay pressed into tablets and stamped with a seal, used since antiquity as an antidote to poison</term>, 1 ounce of each; 2 drams of rhubarb; 1 dram each of spodium <note>original: "spodij"; likely burnt ivory or zinc oxide</note> and ivory shavings; 3 units of nux vomica; and 6 pounds of rectified water of life. When the water has been distilled, add 1 scruple each of fresh Alexandrian musk and good ambergris; 2 drams of Oriental saffron wrapped in a silk cloth (but it must not be ground); half a pound of white sugar; 4 ounces of syrup of stoechas <note>French lavender</note>; 2 ounces of syrup of liquorice; half an ounce of fresh cassia fistula <note>a tropical pod used as a laxative</note> recently extracted from the cane; and 1 scruple of unicorn <note>Renaissance physicians highly valued "unicorn horn"—often narwhal tusk—as a universal purifier and antidote.</note>. 

The Armenian bole <term>bolus armenus: a red medicinal clay from Armenia used as an astringent and to treat the plague</term> must be prepared in this way: Take it in powdered form and knead it into a paste with two parts each of sorrel, rose, and tansy waters, and one part each of scabious, burnet, and coltsfoot <note>original: "vngulæ caballinæ", literally horse-hoof water</note> waters. Mix these together, form a paste with the Armenian bole, and let it dry. Then grind it and knead it again with the aforementioned waters; do this three times—grinding and kneading again—and thus you will have the prepared Armenian bole. 

This water has a most wonderful effect against the plague (as has been proven in many cases) if it is given with scabious water, sorrel water, or coltsfoot water.

### ¶ Concerning compounded water of life.
### Chapter Forty-eight.

Take four pounds of water of life <term>aquæ vitæ: "water of life," the alchemical term for concentrated ethyl alcohol or distilled spirits</term> distilled three times; four drams each of select cinnamon, white ginger, and nutmeg; and two ducats of the finest gold. Let all these be placed in a tin vessel, well-sealed on all sides, for fourteen days. Afterwards, let it be distilled three times through an alembic in a bath, and kept for use. This water is effective for soothing all infirmities when the patient uses it every morning and every evening; it makes an old man grow young again. It also possesses other innumerable virtues, and for that reason, it is most highly esteemed by physicians.

### ¶ Concerning another compounded water of life.
### Chapter Forty-nine.

Take four pounds of simple water of life distilled once; half an ounce each of cloves, white ginger, and rosemary. These spices should be coarsely powdered and then placed in the water itself to digest in horse dung <note>original: "infimo equino"; rotting horse manure was used as a natural, low-heat incubator to maintain a steady temperature for fermentation or extraction</note> for eight days in a digestion vessel <term>digestiuo: a vessel used for "digestion," the slow heating of a substance to extract its medicinal properties</term> well-sealed with lute <term>lutato: sealed with "lute," a specialized clay or paste used to make alchemical apparatus airtight</term>. Afterwards, let it be distilled through an alembic in a Mary’s bath <term>balneo Mariæ: a water bath or bain-marie, used for gentle, indirect heating</term>. Having done this, let it be poured again over the dregs <note>original: "feces"; the solid residue remaining at the bottom of the vessel after distillation</note>, and distilled a second time. Let it also be fermented and distilled a third time in the manners and forms mentioned above, and it will then be prepared. This water is effective for all human <term>defectus: failings, deficiencies, or physical ailments</term> and pains proceeding from coldness. It is also effective against...

This water must be distilled in this manner: Take twelve pounds of rectified water of life <note>original: "aquæ vitæ"; a high-proof distilled spirit used as a base for medicines</note>, in which let the roots and herbs of coarse substance be placed. Let them be put into a glass vessel, sealed perfectly, and digested in horse manure <note>the natural fermentation of horse manure provided a steady, gentle heat for several days, a process known as "digestion"</note> for four days. Afterward, let them be distilled in a Mary's bath <note>original: "balneo Mariæ"; a double boiler or water bath used to heat substances gently and evenly</note>. After this distillation, add the species that have been coarsely powdered, and let it stand for eight days; then let it be distilled again. Finally, when all things have been distilled, you shall add the musk, ambergris, camphor, and sugar, which require no distillation, because their scent would be stripped away by the process.

And note, that in every distillation the alembic <note>original: "alembicus"; the cap or head of the still where vapors collect and condense</note> must be wrapped all around with a cloth moistened with flour and egg whites; otherwise, the matter to be distilled would evaporate.

It should be taken in this way: whoever wishes to use it, whether in the morning or the evening, in drink or in food, should take a small crumb of bread and pour over it three or four drops of the aforementioned water, and it will marvelously comfort the brain. It also very quickly removes all ailments of the head and the entire body. It takes away all superfluities <note>original: "superfluitates"; in medieval medicine, these were excess humors or fluids that were thought to cause disease</note> of the whole body, whether they proceed from cold or from heat. It preserves all the members <note>limbs and organs</note> of the entire body in the best condition. Young people who have not yet passed their thirtieth year should use it rarely, unless they are afflicted by some infirmity. Those who are in their thirties, however, may use it every day; and the older a man is, the more he should use it, because all [virtue]...

<header>THE BOOK OF PHILIPPUS ULSTADIUS</header>
<meta>catchword: their</meta>

...it wonderfully strengthens the natural virtue <note>in medieval medicine, "virtue" refers to the inherent power or life force of the body</note>, so much so that a person, with a proper regimen of life, can reach the term of life predestined for them. For it possesses the power and efficacy of all other medicines, and you may use it by taking four or six drops in other wine with every meal. This is the true water of life <note>original: "aqua vitæ"; here referring to a highly refined medicinal spirit</note>, which has a scent and flavor more excellent than all others. For it is most certain, by the proof of many doctors of the Asclepian art <note>the art of medicine, named after Asclepius, the Greek god of healing</note>, that by the scent of this water alone, a person cannot be infected by polluted air <note>referring to "miasma," the historical belief that diseases like the plague were spread by foul-smelling air</note>. Therefore, they advise that in pestilential times it should be taken every day before one leaves the house. For there is no medicine that better sustains the natural heat than this water of life. Indeed, it shares a property with nature itself, and thus it is converted into blood or natural heat. And on whatever day someone uses it, they are safe; on that day they cannot suffer paralysis, the plague, or the French disease <note>original: "morbum gallicum"; an early term for syphilis</note>. It has the best virtue for strengthening the brain and drives away baldness of the head. If someone also has an unclean head from mange <note>original: "scabie"; a skin infection or crusting</note> or other impurity, it should be anointed with the said water. It is also effective against every flow from the brain <note>likely referring to catarrh or excess mucus</note> and against fainting <note>original: "sincopim"</note>. It heals all abscesses <note>original: "apostemata"; inflammatory swellings or boils</note> when a cloth is moistened with it and placed over them. It cures epilepsy <note>original: "Epilentiam"</note> when you put cotton <note>original: "bombacem"</note> soaked in it into the nostrils and anoint the pulse points with it. It takes away the trembling of all limbs, preserves youth, purifies the face, drives away all wrinkles, and finally removes all blemishes from the face; it clarifies dimness of vision and cures weeping eyes when they are anointed with it. If it is also applied over the eyes, [it heals] all ailments...

It also reopens all the hidden passages of the entire human body if it is drunk and if one anoints <gloss>anoints</gloss> <note>The original text "uxerit" is corrected to "unxerit" (to anoint) by the interlinear gloss.</note> oneself with it. Whoever wishes to be cured of gout in the feet <term>podagra|Gout specifically affecting the feet or big toe</term> or gout in the hands <term>cyragra|Gout affecting the hands or wrists</term> must first be thoroughly purged. <margin>For the lungs, chest, cough, & heart.</margin>
In the summer, take one ounce <note>original: "vnc .i."; an ounce was a standard unit of weight in the apothecary system</note> of endive water and one spoonful of the aforementioned water <note>Referring to "aqua vitae," the distilled alcohol base discussed throughout the book.</note>. For the chest and for a cold cough, even one proceeding from catarrh <note>a buildup of mucus in the nose or throat</note>, take one ounce of hyssop, borage, or fennel water; let it be mixed with the aforementioned water. For the heart, have half an ounce of bugloss or borage water, and just as much of the aforementioned water, and also an equal amount of lemon balm <note>original: "melissæ"</note> water; let them be mixed together and drunk on an empty stomach.

<margin>Stomach</margin>
For the stomach, let one ounce of wormwood <note>original: "abshinthij"</note> or mint water be taken with one spoonful of the good water, and let it be consumed as described above. For the lungs, let parsnip or polypody <note>a type of fern</note> water be taken with the aforementioned water, and used as above.

For the spleen, take bugloss water or tamarisk water with the water as above. For a stone in the bladder, let one ounce of parsnip or radish water be taken with two spoonfuls of the aforementioned water.

For strangury <note>a condition characterized by painful, frequent, and slow urination</note>, take one ounce of watercress, parsley, or saxifrage water with a full spoonful of the water written above.

<margin>Menstruation</margin>
For excessive menstruation: Take plantain water or nightshade water; let it be drunk morning and evening with one spoonful of the aforementioned water. To provoke menstruation <note>original: "menstrua euocanda"</note>, let one ounce of mugwort <note>original: "arthemissiæ"</note> water or its seed be taken with one spoonful of the aforementioned water; let her drink it at the time when she ought to have her period.

### Concerning another composite water of life. Chapter
### Fifty-Three.

Take one pound <note>original: "librā vnā"</note> of the best honey, and one and a half pounds of aqua vitae distilled four times. Take three drachms of aloeswood <note>original: "ligni aloes"</note>, two drachms of gum arabic, and two drachms each of nutmeg, galangal, fresh cubebs <note>a species of pepper</note>, choice cinnamon, mace, cloves <note>original: "gariofilorum"</note>, and spikenard <note>original: "spicæ nardi"; an aromatic plant from the Himalayas</note>. Take one scruple <note>original: "℈. i."; a small unit of weight equal to 1/3rd of a drachm</note> each of fresh Alexandrian musk and good ambergris <note>original: "ambræ"; a rare substance used in perfumes and medicines</note>. Let all these be coarsely powdered, placing everything into a glass gourd <note>term: "cucurbitam vitream": a glass vessel used in distillation, named for its gourd-like shape</note> to digest in horse dung <note>original: "infimo equino"; in early chemistry, a pile of fermenting horse manure provided a steady, gentle heat for slow chemical reactions</note> for eight days. Afterwards, let them be distilled in a Mary’s bath <note>term: "balneo Mariæ": a water bath or bain-marie, used to heat substances gently and evenly without burning them</note> with a slow fire, and in this way you will also have an excellent composite water of life, outstanding for many ailments.

Let them digest in horse dung <note>original: "fimo equino"; horse manure provided a steady, gentle heat for slow extraction in early chemistry</note> for fourteen days. ### effective for paralysis and dizziness
### of the head. Chapter Fifty-
### Five.

Take nine ounces of fresh sage; four ounces of lavender flowers; two handfuls <note>original: "M. ij."; short for "manipuli," a standard apothecary measure based on what a hand can hold</note> each of hyssop and mint; a half-ounce each of cloves, nutmeg, choice cinnamon, white ginger, grains of paradise, zedoary <note>a root similar to turmeric</note>, and galangal; one ounce of aromatic calamus; one ounce of juniper berries; a half-ounce of peony seeds; and eight pounds of the best white wine. Let them digest in horse dung for eight days, or in a Mary’s bath for four days. Afterwards, let it be distilled through an alembic and kept for use in a very well-closed glass vessel, because it has a miraculous effect against all paralysis and dizziness of the head.

-># CONCERNING THE SECRETS OF NATURE<-

Some of our German physicians have produced a most noble and excellent **water of life** <note>original: "aquam vitæ"; high-proof distilled alcohol often infused with herbs</note>, in which they have experienced many remarkable effects for strengthening and preserving the whole human body from sickness. It is especially effective for the head, the brain, and other internal maladies of the head proceeding from moisture and coldness <note>In the medical theory of the time, "cold" and "moist" imbalances were thought to cause sluggishness, congestion, and memory loss</note>, which it serves to dissolve and expel; it also cheers the instruments of the soul and the five senses. It also wonderfully strengthens the four principal organs <note>the heart, brain, liver, and lungs</note>, along with the kidneys and all the intestines. It also procures excellent digestion in the stomach and gives a wonderful vigor to all the external limbs by its scent alone. For this, one should take one part of this water and two parts of sage, lavender, and rosemary waters in equal portions; let them be mixed together and a sponge moistened with it. Rub the limbs with this in the morning and evening, letting them dry on their own. Alternatively, you may place three or four drops into a cup full of wine and drink it before beginning lunch or dinner.

<margin>For the head.</margin>
It is also effective for strengthening the head: Take, therefore, 1 drachm <note>original: "drag. i."; about 3.9 grams</note> of this composite water of life with 1.5 ounces <note>original: "℥. semis"; an ounce was about 28 grams</note> of betony water <note>a common medicinal herb used for head pains</note>. Let it be drunk every morning and evening, or dip bread into the water itself and eat it every day on an empty stomach three hours before lunch.

<margin>The brain. Memory.</margin>
For the brain and memory: Take 1 drachm of this water and 1.5 ounces each of rosemary and marjoram waters. Use it in the manner described above.

<margin>The chest.</margin>
For the chest: Take 1 drachm of this water and 2 drachms each of hyssop water and maidenhair fern water <note>original: "capillorum veneris"; literally "hair of Venus"</note>. Use it in the manner described above.

<margin>The heart.</margin>
For the heart: Take 1 drachm of this water and 2 drachms each of borage and bugloss waters <note>borage and bugloss were historically used to treat "melancholy" and gladden the heart</note>. Use it in the [same] manner—

For the spleen: Take 1 drachm of this water, and 2 ounces <note>original: "℥. ii."; about 56 grams</note> each of tamarisk water and hart's-tongue fern water <note>original: "scolopendriæ"</note>; use it in the form and manner aforesaid. Add half an ounce of fresh cassia fistula <note>original: "crassiæ fistulæ"; the pulp of the purging cassia pod</note> newly extracted from the cane, half an ounce of sugar candy, 1 ounce of syrup of licorice, half an ounce of syrup of French lavender <note>original: "syrupi sticados"</note>, 1 drachm of rosemary flowers, and 1 scruple <note>original: "℈. i."; about 1.3 grams</note> of Alexandrian musk. Over these spices, pour two pounds of simple **water of life** <note>distilled alcohol</note>, excellently rectified <note>purified and strengthened</note> by three-fold distillation in a Mary’s bath <note>original: "balneo Mariæ"; a double boiler or water bath used for gentle heating</note>. Afterward, let it be distilled again in a Mary’s bath through an alembic <note>a distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels and a tube</note> with a very slow fire, so that you may count one, two, three, up to seven, before a single drop is distilled. The musk ought to be tied in a red silk cloth and suspended in the vial where the aforesaid distilled water of life is collected, and it should not be removed as long as the water lasts. There are, however, some who steep <note>original: "putrefaciunt"; literally "to rot," but in alchemy referring to the process of maceration or digestion in liquid</note> the aforesaid spices with one pound of bugloss water, half a pound of lemon balm water, 4 ounces of basil water, and 2 ounces of rosemary water, and afterward they distill them in a Mary’s bath.

ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE

## On another composite water of life, the most precious of all. Chapter Fifty-Seven.

Therefore, so that we may bring a good end to our work, we shall declare a most rare and secret water of life, which is in a certain way of celestial operation, and it is made in this manner: Take fresh sage with its flowers, rosemary, cinnamon <note>original: "darseri", likely a variant of darsini/cinnamon</note>, white ginger, cloves, nutmeg, grains of paradise, galangal, aromatic calamus <note>sweet flag</note>, long pepper, and zedoary <note>a root related to ginger</note>, half an ounce <note>original: "℥. semis"; approx. 14 grams</note> of each. Take mace, lesser cardamom, fresh cubebs, leaves of rue, leaves of marjoram, lavender flowers, and red roses, 2 drachms <note>original: "drag. ii."; approx. 7.8 grams</note> of each. Take Galen’s or Andromachus’ theriac <note>a famous ancient compound of many ingredients used as an antidote and panacea</note> and the best mithridate <note>a similar complex medicinal paste</note>, 1.5 drachms of each. Take laurel oil, citron peel, bugloss flowers, borage flowers, rosemary flowers, masterwort <note>original: "ostrucij"</note>, angelica, rhapontic <note>false rhubarb</note>, juniper berries, horse mint, garden mint, and feverfew, 1.5 drachms of each. Take fresh castoreum <note>a secretion from beaver scent glands</note>, vervain with its flowers, betony, aloeswood, xylobalsam <note>balsam wood</note>, carpobalsam <note>balsam fruit</note>, spikenard <note>original: "spicæ indicæ"</note>, oak mistletoe, and peony seeds, 1 drachm of each. Take rhubarb <note>original: "rauedseni", likely Rha-Sini or Chinese rhubarb</note>, camphor, daemon’s grape <note>original: "vuæ demonis"; likely herb paris or black nightshade</note>, peony root, poly-mountain <note>original: "polij"</note> with its flowers, basil seed, fennel seed, doronicum <note>leopard's bane</note>, and oriental saffron, half a drachm of each. Take good ambergris and fresh Alexandrian musk, half a scruple <note>original: "℈. semis"; approx. 0.6 grams</note> of each. Take ten pounds of water of life <note>aqua vitae/alcohol</note> distilled at least seven times. All the following should be placed with the water of life to digest in a Bain-Marie <note>original: "balneo Mariæ"; a warm water bath</note> for four days and as many nights: the sage, rosemary, rue, marjoram, lavender, roses, mithridate...

[mi]thridate <note>original: "tridatum"; completing the word "mithridatum" from the previous page, a complex medicinal paste used as an antidote</note>, theriac, laurel oil, bugloss, borage, masterwort, angelica, rhapontic <note>original: "reuponticum"; a root similar to rhubarb</note>, juniper berries, horse mint, garden mint, feverfew, vervain, betony, mountain germander <note>original: "polium"</note>, and castoreum. And when these have been macerated <note>original: "putrefacta"; in alchemy, this refers to a controlled aging or "rotting" to break down materials</note>, let them be distilled in ash <note>a method of even heat distribution</note> using an alembic over a slow fire, so slow that you can count "one, two, three" before a single drop falls. 

When all these have been distilled, add cinnamon, cloves, mace, nutmeg, grains of paradise <note>a peppery West African spice</note>, galangal, sweet flag <note>original: "calamū aromaticū"</note>, long pepper, zedoary <note>a ginger-like root</note>, cubebs, cardamom, aloeswood, citron peels, carpobalsam <note>fruit of the balsam tree</note>, xylobalsam <note>wood of the balsam tree</note>, spikenard, oak mistletoe, peony root and its seeds, prepared coriander, basil seed, Herb Paris <note>original: "semen vuae daemonis"; literally "demon’s grape"</note>, and leopard's bane <note>original: "deronicum"</note>. All these should be coarsely powdered, placed in the distilled water, and allowed to digest together for fourteen days. Afterward, distill them again in a water bath <note>original: "balneo Mariae"</note> through an alembic. Then add camphor, rhubarb, saffron, ambergris, and musk.

If you wish to have an even better water, add one drachm <note>approx. 3.9 grams</note> each of the following: powder of pearls <note>original: "diamargariti"</note> according to Nicolaus <note>Nicolaus Praepositus, a medieval medical authority</note>, iris-powder with musk <note>original: "diapliris cum musco"</note> according to Nicolaus, the Abbot's rose-powder <note>original: "diarhodon abbatis"</note>, musk-powder according to Mesue <note>an influential Persian physician</note>, and ambergris-powder. Also add half a drachm each of: electuary of gemstones, Galen's "powder of joy" <note>original: "pul. laeticiae"; a mixture intended to lift the spirits</note>, and aloeswood powder. Add fifteen or twenty ducats <note>gold coins</note> of the finest gold, with half a pound of white sugar. Let these sit for three days in a water bath, ensuring the water of the bath is only lukewarm. Afterward, it should be filtered into retort glasses <note>curved glass vessels used in distillation</note>, and one glass should stand higher than the other. ### ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE

[place them] <note>completing a verb from the previous page, likely "ponantur," meaning "let them be placed"</note>, and through them the material may be distilled, with the glass vessels excellently sealed <note>original: "lutatis"; refers to the use of 'lute,' a clay or paste used to make laboratory seals airtight</note>, so that nothing can evaporate from them. And this method of distillation is the most excellent among all others.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a circulatory distillation apparatus. Two large, curved retort-like glass vessels are positioned with their necks joined together, allowing vapor to circulate between them. They rest on flat rectangular bases or furnaces. The scene is framed by architectural elements: two side pillars support an arched structure featuring two decorative, ribbon-like banners or scrolls.</image-desc>

There are some physicians who, wishing to strengthen the stomach, have had **green water** <note>original: "aquam viridem"</note> made, which they used with the water described above, or with the following claret <note>a sweetened, spiced medicinal wine</note>, just as a certain Count Palatine <note>a high-ranking official of the Holy Roman Empire</note> had done. 

**Green water**, moreover, is made in this way: take four pounds <note>original: "lib. iiii."</note> of <term>aqua vitae|literally "water of life"; a high-proof distilled spirit used as a solvent for medicines</term> that has been distilled four times through an alembic in a water bath <note>original: "balneo Mariae"; a gentle heating method using a vessel of hot water</note>. Take four ounces <note>original: "℥. iiii."</note> of dried lemon balm <note>original: "melissae"</note> and two ounces of costmary <note>original: "balsamitae"; an aromatic herb also known as alecost</note>. Let the water of life be poured over the aforementioned herbs, and they should all rest thus for eight days; then use it. And note— <note>original: "no-"; catchword for the next page, completing the word "notandum" or "note"</note>

### THE BOOK OF PHILIP ULSTAD
<meta>catchword: said</meta>

[dr]ied, because herbs should not be dried in the Sun, but in the air, for if they were dried in the sun, the water would become dark, as if it were the juice of some herb. But if herbs are dried in the air, they keep a most beautiful green color, and it is most pleasant for the one drinking it. This **claret** <note>a sweetened, spiced medicinal wine</note>, however, is made in this way: Take four pounds <note>original: "lib. iiii."</note> of the best white wine; four ounces <note>original: "℥. iiii."</note> of hard white sugar; one ounce of cinnamon; three drams <note>original: "drag. iii."</note> of prepared coriander; two drams of cloves; one and a half drams each of grains of paradise and white ginger; two scruples <note>original: "℈. ii."</note> of <term>long pepper|macropiper; a hotter, more earthy relative of black pepper</term>; and half a dram of zedoary <note>a root similar to turmeric or ginger</note>. Let all these be ground very finely and reduced to a powder; afterward, it must be strained, as is done among the French with spiced wine, which in French is called **Hypocras**. Let it be kept in a tin vessel, and it is customary for some to take it with the aforementioned water. The virtues of the aforementioned water are these:

<margin>For the memory.</margin>
It is effective for the memory if half an ounce is drunk every day, mixed with half an ounce of rosemary water, and two drams each of marjoram water and lemon balm water.

<margin>For mania, or even pain of the brain proceeding from cold.</margin>
For mania <note>referring to various states of mental agitation or delirium</note>, or even pain of the brain proceeding from cold: Take half an ounce of this water, and two drams each of betony water and mountain germander water <note>original: "aquæ polij"</note>; let them be mixed together and moisten a linen cloth, wrapping it around the head. Or let two drams of this water and four drams of betony water be drunk every day. Let there also be taken one scruple each of <term>golden pills|pillulae aureae</term> and <term>cochiae pills|pillulae cochiae</term> <note>both were common medieval purgative recipes believed to clear the head of "corrupt humors"</note>; let the pills be made with betony water, take five in number, and take them every day when you go to sleep, or even when you rise, take one dram of <term>dianthos|a medicinal confection made of rosemary flowers</term> in tablet form.

<margin>For dizziness of the head, baldness, and whitening the face.</margin>
For dizziness <note>original: "vertiginem"</note> of the head: Take half an ounce of this water, and three drams each of lavender and sage waters; and let it be taken every day on an empty stomach with <term>diapliris|a cordial powder made from various roots and spices</term> with musk, according to Nicolaus <note>likely referring to the *Antidotarium Nicolae*, a famous 12th-century medical text</note>. For baldness: Take half a dram each of white lily water and <term>Aaron's beard|Arum maculatum; a plant used in traditional medicine</term> water; and half an ounce or six drams of this [medicinal] water; adding one dram of oil of tartar. To cleanse and whiten the face: Take one part bean flower water, and one part of the [aforementioned] water...

<summary>This page provides a detailed recipe for a medicinal spiced wine (Hypocras) and lists various pharmaceutical applications for "green water," including treatments for memory loss, mania, and cosmetic concerns like baldness and skin whitening.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the list of medical applications for the "aforementioned water" (the green water/distilled balm mentioned previously), moving from neurological and mental health treatments to external remedies for the face, ears, eyes, and mouth.</meta>

### ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE

the said two parts, let them be mixed together, and every day you shall wash your face once. You can also every day take one part of the aforementioned water, and two parts of the best wine; let it be drunk on an empty stomach, and it will be effective for the same <note>referring to the facial complexion or the "mania" mentioned on the previous page</note>.

<margin>For deafness.</margin>
For deafness. Every morning and evening, you shall place some of this water into the ears; then you shall lie down for a quarter of an hour and rest upon that same ear, so that it may come out again. Alternatively, take equal parts of this water and the best wine; let it be taken every day on an empty stomach.

<margin>For ear worms.</margin>
For ear worms: Take half an ounce <note>original: "vn. semis"</note> of the aforementioned water, and two drams <note>original: "drag. ij."</note> each of rue juice and peach leaf juice. If, however, you cannot obtain the juices, you shall take water distilled from them, adding a little <term>hepatic aloe|a medicinal resin from the Aloe vera plant, traditionally used in "liver-colored" chunks for its bitter, anti-parasitic properties</term>, very finely ground and powdered. Every morning and evening, you shall distill a little of this into the ear itself, lying on the other side; and after a short time you shall lie on the side where the worms are, and they will immediately come out and die.

<margin>For ophthalmia, swelling of the gums, and foul odor of the mouth or nostrils.</margin>
For <term>ophthalmia|a general historical term for inflammation or infection of the eye</term>:
Take half an ounce <note>original: "℥. semis"</note> of the aforementioned water, and one dram each of fennel water, <term>poly water|water distilled from hulwort or mountain germander</term>, and valerian water; let them be applied to the eyes. For swelling of the gums: Take nine ounces of white wine and one ounce of <term>pellitory|Anacyclus pyrethrum, a root used since antiquity to stimulate saliva and treat toothaches</term>; let them be boiled together. Take one part of this and one part of the aforementioned water, then hold this mixture in the mouth, and frequently take it fresh; do this until you recover. For foul odor of the mouth or nostrils: let half an ounce be given every day with white wine in which one ounce of mint and red roses have been boiled, adding a little musk. Some, however, take two ounces of the aforementioned water, and half an ounce each of rosemary and red rose waters; let them be mixed together, and let musk wrapped in a silk cloth and good <term>ambergris|a rare, waxy substance from the digestive system of sperm whales, highly prized in perfumery and medicine</term>, six grains <note>original: "gra. vi."</note> each, be steeped in it.

# PHILIP ULSTADIUS BOOK. 95

...and the gums with that silk cloth dipped in the water; you should wash them often, at least once or twice daily. You can also drink two drams <note>original: "drag. .ii."</note> of the aforementioned water every month.

<margin>For epilepsy.</margin>
For epilepsy <note>original: "epilentiam," a common variant of "epilepsiam"</note>: Let half an ounce <note>original: "℥. semis"</note> be drunk every day, along with one ounce of peony water. Some indeed do this: they take peony root and seeds, and oak mistletoe in equal parts, boil them in the best white wine, and give it to the patient with the aforementioned water.

<margin>For paralysis.</margin>
For paralysis: the limbs should be frequently anointed with the aforementioned water. Additionally, one ounce <note>original: "vn. i."</note> of this water should be drunk every day in the morning. In the evening, however, you shall take <term>diacastoreum|a medicinal electuary based on castor (beaver secretion), used to treat "cold" nervous disorders</term> with white wine in which a handful <note>original: "M. i." or manipulus</note> each of lavender, sage, and juniper berries have been cooked, pouring over four pounds of the best wine.

<margin>For loss of speech, melancholy, and putrefaction.</margin>
For loss of speech: Take half an ounce of the aforementioned water, and two drams each of lavender, peony, and sage waters. Let it be drunk and taken with one dram of <term>mithridatium|a famous ancient "universal antidote" containing dozens of ingredients</term> with wine in which castor has been boiled.

For melancholy: Take half an ounce of the aforementioned water, with one ounce of bugloss water, two drams each of basilicon water and lemon balm water, and one dram each of <term>scolopendria|hart's-tongue fern</term> and borage water. Let these be mixed together and drunk three hours before lunch. Take also one tablet of the "confection of joy" by <term>Galen|the influential 2nd-century Greek physician</term>, or the "rejoicing compound" of <term>Al-Mansur|referring to the Persian physician Rhazes and his 'Book for Mansur'</term>.

For putrefaction <note>decay or inflammation</note> of the liver: Take half an ounce of this water with liverwort water. Let one or one-and-a-half ounces be drunk every evening. In the morning, two or three hours before lunch, take <term>diarhodon abbatis|a rose-based cooling compound from the Salernitan medical tradition</term> according to the instructions of <term>Nicholas|Nicolaus Salernitanus, author of a standard medieval text on compounding medicines</term>.

<margin>Dropsy.</margin>
For <term>dropsy|edema, or the unhealthy accumulation of fluid in the body</term>: Take one part of the aforementioned water, two parts of elderflower water, and three parts of fennel water. Let them be mixed together, and every...

<meta>This page concludes Philip Ulstadius's medical treatise, finishing the recipes for dropsy (edema) and continuing into treatments for kidney stones and sterility before providing the publication details of the 16th-century Parisian printer Vivant Gaultherot.</meta>

# ON THE SECRETS OF NATURE

...drink half an ounce <note>original: "vnc. semis."</note> in turn. In the evening, however, when you wish to go to bed, take one dram <note>original: "drag. i."</note> of <term>Galen’s great theriac|a famous ancient medicinal paste consisting of dozens of ingredients, used as a universal antidote and restorative</term> in wine. This wine should be prepared by boiling one part each of parsley, fennel, and celery roots, along with maidenhair fern <note>original: "capillorum veneris"</note>, in ten parts of wine. These should be boiled until one-third of the liquid has evaporated. Every three days, two or three hours before lunch, give the patient suffering from <term>dropsy|edema or swelling caused by fluid retention</term> two drams of <term>dialacca|a medicine made from lac (insect resin) used for liver and spleen ailments</term> according to the recipe of Mesue <note>an influential Arabic medical authority</note>. The sick person must be careful to avoid unnecessary drinking, for in this disease, the less a person drinks, the more they urinate, and vice versa.

<margin>Stone.</margin> **For the Stone:** Take one part of the aforementioned water, and two parts each of saxifrage water, <term>alkekengi|winter cherry or bladder cherry water</term>, <term>gromwell|literally "sun-millet" (milii solis) water</term>, and radish water. One hour after this potion, give two drams of <term>lithontripton|a "stone-breaking" medicine intended to dissolve kidney or bladder stones</term>.

<margin>Sterility.</margin> **For Sterility:** Take two drams of this water, one ounce of white wine, and two drams each of yellow lily water, rosemary water, and motherwort water. This should be drunk in the morning or evening. Every week, use two drams of <term>diamargariton|a restorative powder or electuary made with pearls</term> according to Avicenna <note>a renowned Persian physician</note> two or three times.

It possesses other innumerable virtues which I omit for the sake of brevity.

->THE END.<-
